Trace Moments for Schrödinger Operators with Matrix White Noise and the Rigidity of the Multivariate Stochastic Airy Operator
Pierre Yves Gaudreau Lamarre

Abstract
. We study the semigroups of random Schrödinger operators of the form H ^ ​ f = − 1 2 ​ f ′′ + ( V + ξ ) ​ f \widehat{H}f=-\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}+(V+\xi)f , where f : I → 𝔽 r f:I\to\mathbb{F}^{r} ( 𝔽 = ℝ , ℂ , ℍ \mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H} ) are vector-valued functions on a possibly infinite interval I ⊂ ℝ I\subset\mathbb{R} that satisfy a mix of Robin and Dirichlet boundary conditions, V V is a deterministic diagonal potential with power-law growth at infinity, and ξ \xi is a matrix white noise. Our main result consists of Feynman-Kac formulas for trace moments of the form 𝐄 ⁡ [ ∏ k = 1 n Tr ⁡ [ e − t k ​ H ^ ] ] \mathbf{E}\Big[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{k}\widehat{H}}\big]\Big] ( n ∈ ℕ n\in\mathbb{N} , t k > 0 t_{k}>0 ). One notable example covered by our main result consists of the multivariate stochastic Airy operator (SAO) of Bloemendal and Virág [ 5 ] , which characterizes the soft-edge eigenvalue fluctuations of critical rank- r r spiked Wishart and GO/U/SE random matrices. As a corollary of our main result, we prove that if V V ’s growth is at least linear (this includes the multivariate SAO), then H ^ \widehat{H} ’s spectrum is number rigid in the sense of Ghosh and Peres [ 34 ] . Together with the rigidity of the scalar SAO [ 6 , 29 ] , this completes the characterization of number rigidity in the soft-edge limits of Gaussian β \beta -ensembles and their finite-rank spiked versions.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
General Setup and Main Result

In this paper, we are interested in the semigroup theory of a class of random
vector-valued Schrödinger operators defined as follows:
Let
\mathbb{F}
be the field of real numbers
\mathbb{R}
, the field of complex numbers
\mathbb{C}
, or
the ring of quaternions
\mathbb{H}
.
Let
r>0
be a fixed integer, and
I\subset\mathbb{R}
be a (possibly infinite) interval.
Consider the operator

Hf(i,x)=-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(i,x)+V(i,x)f(i,x),\qquad f:I\to\mathbb{F}^{r},

where
\big(f(i,\cdot)\big)_{1\leq i\leq r}
denote the components of a vector-valued function
f
,
the second derivative
f^{\prime\prime}
acts on the variable
x\in I
only, and
V(i,\cdot)
are locally integrable deterministic
functions with sufficient growth when
I
is infinite (see Assumption
2.8
).
Assume that
H
acts on functions whose components
f(i,\cdot)
satisfy a mix of Robin boundary conditions

f^{\prime}(i,c)+\alpha f(i,c)=0
(
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
)
and Dirichlet boundary conditions
f(i,c)=0
at the boundary points
c\in\partial I
; if there are any
(see Assumption
2.4
).

We are interested in random perturbations of
H
of the form

(1.1)

\displaystyle\widehat{H}=H+\xi,

where
\xi
is a matrix white noise. That is, if we let
\cdot^{*}
denote conjugation
in
\mathbb{F}
, then
\xi
is the singular matrix multiplication operator

\xi f(i,\cdot)=\sum_{j=1}^{r}\xi_{i,j}f(j,\cdot),

where
\xi_{i,j}=\xi_{j,i}^{*}
for all
1\leq i,j\leq r
, and informally,
we view
(\xi_{i,i})_{1\leq i\leq r}
as the derivatives of i.i.d. Brownian motions
in
\mathbb{R}
and
(\xi_{i,j})_{1\leq i<j\leq r}
as the derivatives of i.i.d. Brownian motions in

\mathbb{F}
(see Definition
2.7
for more details).

The aim of this paper is to provide a new avenue to study the
eigenvalues of
\widehat{H}
via probabilistic representations of
the semigroup
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}
.
Our main result in this direction,
Theorem
4.9
, consists of
Feynman-Kac formulas for the joint moments

(1.2)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}}\big]\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}}\big]\cdots\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{n}\widehat{H}}\big]\right],\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},~t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\geq 0.

Letting
\lambda_{1}(\widehat{H})\leq\lambda_{2}(\widehat{H})\leq\cdots
denote the eigenvalues of
\widehat{H}
, we recall that

\textstyle\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}\big]=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}\qquad\text{for every }t>0.

Thus, a calculation of the quantities (
1.2
) provides a means of characterizing the distribution of
\widehat{H}
’s
eigenvalue point process via its Laplace transform’s joint moments.

A notable feature of our Feynman-Kac formulas
when
r>1
is the following insight: In addition to the usual Brownian motion
\big(B(t)\big)_{t\geq 0}

(coming from the diffusion operator
f\mapsto-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}
), the dynamics are decorated by
a continuous-time random walk
\big(U(t)\big)_{t\geq 0}
on the complete graph with
r
vertices, whose jumps times

\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq\cdots
are conditioned to coincide with self-intersections of both
U
and
B
. That is,
there must exist a way to partition the jump times
into pairs
\{\tau_{\ell_{1}},\tau_{\ell_{2}}\}
such that

(1)

the jumps in
U
that occur at times
\tau_{\ell_{1}}
and
\tau_{\ell_{2}}

are between the same two vertices in
\{1,\ldots,r\}
, and

(2)

B(\tau_{\ell_{1}})=B(\tau_{\ell_{2}})
.

Among other things, this induces a surprisingly rich combinatorial structure in the mixed moments
(
1.2
), coming from counting paths of the random walk
U
for which such pairings exist.
We refer to the statement of Theorem
4.9
for the full details,
and to Section
1.3.3
for a high-level explanation of this result and how it is proved.

The remainder of this introduction is organized as follows:
In Section
1.2
, we discuss
the motivation for our work (mainly, understanding the multivariate stochastic Airy operators), as well as an application in the study of number rigidity of eigenvalue point processes.
In Section
1.3
,
we provide a high-level explanation of our method of proof,
and we survey past results related to the main technical innovation in this paper.
In Section
1.4
, we formulate an open problem
regarding a pathwise Feynman-Kac formula for
\widehat{H}
.
Then, in Section
1.5
,
we discuss the organization of the rest of the paper.

1.2.
Motivation and Application

1.2.1.
Motivation: Multivariate Stochastic Airy Operators

Our main motivation for developing the semigroup theory of
\widehat{H}
comes from
the special case where
I=(0,\infty)
,
V(i,x)=\frac{rx}{2}
, and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\xi
is a
standard

matrix white noise (that is, the derivative of a matrix Brownian motion with GOE/GUE/GSE
increments; see Remark
2.10
for more details).
In this special case,
2\widehat{H}
is the multivariate stochastic Airy operator
(SAO) introduced by Bloemendal and Virág in
[
5
]

(see also
[
4
,
55
]
for previous results concerning the scalar case
r=1
).

The interest in the multivariate SAO lies in the fact that its spectrum describes the asymptotic
soft-edge scaling limits of critical rank-
r
perturbations of Wishart and Gaussian
invariant ensembles; the so-called spiked models. More specifically, consider an

r\times r
generalized matrix of the form

(1.3)

\displaystyle W=\sum_{i=1}^{r}(-\overline{\alpha}_{i})u_{i}u_{i}^{\dagger},\qquad\overline{\alpha}_{i}\in(-\infty,\infty],

where the vectors
u_{i}\in\mathbb{F}^{r}
form an orthonormal basis
and
\dagger
denotes the conjugate transpose,
and let
2\widehat{H}
be the multivariate SAO with boundary conditions

(1.4)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}f^{\prime}(i,0)+\overline{\alpha}_{i}f(i,0)=0&\text{if }\overline{\alpha}_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\\
f(i,0)=0&\text{if }\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-\infty.\end{cases}

\bullet

On the one hand,
[
5
, Theorem 1.2]
showed that

2\widehat{H}
’s spectrum describes the limiting fluctuations
(as
n\to\infty
)
of the top eigenvalues of
n\times n
random matrices of the form
X_{n}+(W_{n}\oplus 0_{n-r})
,
where
X_{n}
is a GOE, GUE, or GSE matrix (depending on
whether
\beta=1,2,
or
4
),

(1.5)

\displaystyle W_{n}\approx\sqrt{n}\,\mathrm{Id}_{r}-n^{1/6}W

is a critical additive perturbation of rank
r
,
and
0_{k}
and
\mathrm{Id}_{k}
denote respectively the
k\times k
zero and identity matrices.

\bullet

On the other hand,
[
5
, Theorem 1.3]
showed that

2\widehat{H}
’s spectrum also describes the limit fluctuations
(as
n,p\to\infty
) of the top eigenvalues of
p
-variate real, complex, or quaternion
(again depending on
whether
\beta=1,2,
or
4
) Wishart matrices
with sample size
n
and a rank-
r
spiked covariance of the form
W_{n,p}\oplus\mathrm{Id}_{p-r}
,
where

(1.6)

\displaystyle W_{n,p}\approx\mathrm{Id}_{r}+\sqrt{\frac{p}{n}}\left(\mathrm{Id}_{r}-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{p}}\right)^{2/3}W\right).

Since the celebrated works of Baik, Ben Arous, and Péché

[
2
,
54
]
,
the extreme eigenvalue fluctuations of the above random matrix models
have been known to undergo a phase transition depending on the size of the perturbations

W_{n}
or
W_{n,p}
:

\bullet

In the
subcritical
regime (i.e.,
W_{n}<\sqrt{n}\mathrm{Id}_{r}
or
W_{n,p}<(1+\sqrt{p/n})\mathrm{Id}_{n}
), the perturbations are so small that
the top eigenvalues behave as though
W_{n}=W_{n,p}=0
. In particular, the limit
fluctuations follow the Tracy-Widom distributions (equivalently, the joint eigenvalue distribution
of any multivariate SAO with only Dirichlet boundary conditions; i.e.,
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-\infty
for all
1\leq i\leq r
).

\bullet

In the
supercritical
regime (i.e.,
W_{n}>\sqrt{n}\mathrm{Id}_{r}
or
W_{n,p}>(1+\sqrt{p/n})\mathrm{Id}_{n}
), the perturbations are so large that
the top eigenvalues separate from the edge of the semicircle/Marchenko-Pastur
distributions, and thus become outliers.

The perturbation sizes described in (
1.5
)
and (
1.6
) interpolate between these two settings,
and thus allow to describe
critical
perturbations. More specifically, the non-Dirichlet
boundary conditions in
2\widehat{H}
(i.e., the
indices
1\leq i\leq r
such that
\overline{\alpha}_{i}>-\infty
in (
1.3
)
and (
1.4
)) correspond to critical spikes,
while the Dirichlet boundary conditions (i.e.,
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-\infty
) correspond to subcritical spikes.
In particular, the results of
[
5
]
established the existence of a
continuous family of deformations of the classical Tracy-Widom laws, which
are parametrized by the non-Dirichlet components of the boundary
condition (
1.4
).
We also refer to
[
2
,
52
,
54
,
63
]
for partial results in this direction,
and more generally to the introductions of
[
4
,
5
]

for detailed expositions of these results
and their significance in the theories of random matrices and covariance estimation.
In this view, one of our main goals in this paper is to develop a new set of tools to study
the properties of limit laws that arise in critical spiked models.

Remark 1.1
.

The boundary conditions on the multivariate SAO used in
[
5
]
are slightly different from those that we stated in (
1.4
); namely:

(1.7)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}u_{i}^{\dagger}\big(f^{\prime}(0)-w_{i}f(0)\big)=0&\text{if }w_{i}\in\mathbb{R},\\
u_{i}^{\dagger}f(0)=0&\text{if }w_{i}=\infty,\end{cases}

for some
w_{i}\in(-\infty,\infty]
. The differences in weights
(i.e.,
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-w_{i}
) is merely a matter of
convention; we use
\overline{\alpha}_{i}
to get a slightly neater
statement of the Feynman-Kac formula. In contrast to this, at first glance the
presence of
u_{i}^{\dagger}
in (
1.7
) appears
genuinely different from (
1.4
), as the boundary conditions
are imposed in the directions of the
u_{i}
rather than the standard basis.

That said, we note that the potential
V(i,x)=\frac{rx}{2}
does not
depend on
i
(i.e.,
V
is a multiple of the identity matrix), and that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\xi
in the case of the SAO is the derivative of a matrix Brownian motion
with GOE/GUE/GSE increments. Thus, since the GOE/GUE/GSE are invariant (in distribution) with respect to conjugation by the unitary matrix
with columns
u_{i}
, the eigenvalues of the SAO with boundary conditions (
1.7
) have the same joint distribution
as the eigenvalues of the SAO with boundary conditions (
1.4
),
provided we take
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-w_{i}
. Given that
we are only interested in the eigenvalues of
\widehat{H}
, in this paper we assume (without loss of generality)
that the SAO’s boundary condition are diagonal (i.e., of the form (
1.4
)).

1.2.2.
Application: Number Rigidity

A point process is number rigid if for a certain class of Borel sets
\mathcal{K}
,
the number of points inside
\mathcal{K}
is uniquely determined by the configuration of points outside of
\mathcal{K}
.
(A common choice is to impose that
\mathcal{K}
be bounded; in our paper we allow sets that are
merely bounded from above, as per Definition
4.12
.)
Number rigidity was first introduced by Ghosh and Peres in
[
34
]
,
following-up on a number of studies of related notions of rigidity of point processes
(e.g., insertion and deletion singularity; notably
[
41
]
).
Throughout the past decade, an extensive literature on the notion was developed,
wherein number rigidity has been proved (or disproved) for many point processes
using a variety of techniques.

Looking more specifically at the point processes that arise
as scaling limits of random matrices and/or the spectrum of random Schrödinger operators:
Number rigidity was proved for the Ginibre process in

[
34
]
and for the Sine
β
processes in
[
9
,
16
,
33
]
.
In the scalar case
r=1
, the rigidity of the SAO
was proved in
[
6
,
29
]
; more general one-dimensional random Schrödinger
operators (continuous and discrete) were considered in
[
30
,
31
]
.

As an application of the main results of this paper, we prove in
Theorem
4.13

that if
V(i,x)\geq\kappa|x|-\nu
for some
\kappa,\nu>0

(this is trivially true when
I
is bounded), then
\widehat{H}
’s eigenvalue point process is number rigid.
This of course includes the multivariate SAO, as in this case
x\in(0,\infty)
and
V(i,x)=\frac{rx}{2}
.
Consequently, once combined with
[
6
,
29
]
,
Theorem
4.13
completely settles the question of number rigidity
in the point processes that describe the asymptotic soft-edge fluctuations of
\beta
-ensembles
and their critical rank-
r
spiked versions. More broadly, when combined with

[
30
,
31
]
, the present work shows that rigidity phenomena in random Schrödinger
operator eigenvalues also arise in general situations with matrix white noise.

Theorem
4.13
follows from
the standard methodology to prove number rigidity introduced in

[
34
, Theorem 6.1]
and
[
41
, Proposition 7.1 and Lemma 7.2]
:
Control the fluctuations of sequences of linear statistics that converge to
1 uniformly on compact sets.
More specifically, following previous
works on the number rigidity of random Schrödinger
operators—i.e,
[
30
,
31
]
, and most notably
[
29
]
—we
prove rigidity using the following sufficient condition (see
[
29
, Section 4.1]
for a proof):

Proposition 1.2
.

Suppose that

(1.8)

\displaystyle\limsup_{t\to 0}\mathbf{Var}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}\big]\Big]<\infty

and that for every fixed
t_{1}\in(0,1]
, one has

(1.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{t_{2}\to 0}\mathbf{Cov}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}}\big],\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}}\big]\Big]=0.

Then,
\widehat{H}
’s spectrum is number rigid.

The proof of (
1.8
) and (
1.9
)
under the hypothesis
V(i,x)\geq\kappa|x|-\nu

relies on a direct application of our Feynman-Kac formulas for (
1.2
).

1.2.3.
Future Applications

The main technical difficulties encountered in this paper
that are directly related to the generality of our setting
come from the cases where
I
has a boundary and
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}

(see Remark
1.14
for more details).
Both of these features are present in the SAOs. In particular, there is no additional
technical challenge that comes from considering generic one-dimensional operators,
which explains the generality of our setting beyond
just the multivariate SAO.
In this context, one potential source of future applications for our results
lies in the many recent advances in the study of random Schrödinger operators
with white noise potentials (one manifestation of the Anderson model); e.g.,
[
1
,
10
,
18
,
19
,
20
,
21
,
42
,
46
,
49
,
50
]
. In these works, the potential energy and the functions on which
the operators act are assumed to be scalar-valued (i.e.,
r=1
). The generality of our setting could
be used to begin exploring extensions of this theory to the vector-valued setting (i.e.,
r>1
).

Going beyond spectral theory, the techniques developed in this paper could potentially be
used to study the moments of finite systems of stochastic PDEs on
I

coupled by a matrix white noise. More specifically, if we define the function

u(t;i,x)=\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}f(i,x)
, for a fixed
f:I\to\mathbb{F}^{r}
, then
u

formally solves the system of stochastic PDEs

(1.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{t}u(t;i,\cdot)=\frac{1}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}u(t;i,\cdot)-V(i,\cdot)u(t;i,\cdot)-\sum_{j=1}^{r}\xi_{i,j}u(t;j,\cdot),\qquad 1\leq i\leq r,

with initial conditions
u(t;i,\cdot)=f(i,\cdot)
and a mix of boundary conditions of the form

\partial_{x}u(t;i,c)+\alpha u(t;i,c)=0
or
u(t;i,c)=0
for
c\in\partial I
.
When
r=1
and
V=0
, (
1.10
) reduces to the Stratonovich parabolic Anderson model
with time-independent white noise; e.g.,
[
7
,
8
,
40
,
48
]
.
While the results of this paper only concern the case where

f(i,\cdot)=\delta_{x}(\cdot)
(as we study the diagonal
of
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}
’s integral kernel), treating general
f
’s would
amount to a very minor extension of our results.

Finally, we expect that our technology
could be used to obtain various asymptotic expansions of the moments
of
\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}\big]
or
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}f(i,x)
for some function
f
, either as
t\to\infty
or
t\to 0^{+}
. Indeed, despite the complexity
of the moment formulas obtained in Theorem
4.9
, asymptotic expansions typically only involve a few dominant terms,
whose combinatorics should be tractable. In particular, in a forthcoming paper

[
27
]
, we use Theorem
4.9

to calculate the leading terms in the small-time (i.e.,
t\to 0^{+}
) asymptotic expansion of
\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\mathrm{SAO}/2}\big]
; given the connection
between SAO spectra and critically-spiked matrix models
(see Section
1.2.1
), this result will lead to new applications related to
the recovery of critical signals. A natural next step would be to investigate
the intermittency properties of (
1.10
) using large-time
(i.e.,
t\to\infty
) asymptotics of moment formulas similar to those in
Theorem
4.9
.

1.3.
Proof Method and Past Results

In this section, we provide a high-level explanation of the novel difficulties that arise in
the proof of our main result, as well as the method that we use to solve them. We
also take this opportunity to provide references to past results similar to
our method of proof.

Remark 1.3
.

In order to simplify
the exposition, we only consider the case where
I=\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
in this outline, with the
understanding that our results do apply to more general domains and fields/rings. In fact, as we highlight
in Remark
1.14
, the consideration of domains with boundaries
and quaternion-valued functions (both of which are mainly motivated
by the SAO) induces significant technical challenges.

1.3.1.
The Classical Feynman-Kac Formula and its Vector-Valued Extension

Consider a generic Schrödinger operator that acts on scalar-valued functions (i.e.,
r=1
):

(1.11)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}f(x)=-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(x)+\mathcal{V}(x)f(x),\qquad f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}.

In this classical setting, the Feynman-Kac formula has a well-known statement:

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be as in (
1.11
).
If
\mathcal{V}
is sufficiently well-behaved (see, e.g.,
[
62
, Theorem 4.9]

for a precise statement), then for every
t>0
,
\mathrm{e}^{-t\mathcal{H}}
has the kernel

\mathrm{e}^{-t\mathcal{H}}(x,y)=\Pi_{B}(t;x,y)\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{V}(B^{x,y}_{t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right],\qquad t\geq 0,~x,y\in\mathbb{R},

where
B
is a standard Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}
,

\Pi_{B}(t;x,y)=\mathbf{P}\big[B(t)\in\mathrm{d}y~\big|~B(0)=x\big]

is
B
’s transition kernel,
and
B^{x,y}_{t}
denotes
B
conditioned on

\big\{B(0)=x\text{ and }B(t)=y\big\}
.

Consider now a generic vector-valued operator of the form

(1.12)

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}f(i,x)=-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(i,x)+\sum_{j=1}^{r}\mathcal{V}_{i,j}(x)f(j,x),\qquad f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{r},

where
\mathcal{V}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
takes values in self-adjoint
r\times r
matrices.
In order to state the Feynman-Kac formula in this setting, we need to introduce the vector-valued
extension of the exponential integral
\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{V}(B(s))~\mathrm{d}s}
, which leads to the consideration of ordered exponentials
(OEs):

Definition 1.5
.

Let
\big(F(t)\big)_{t\geq 0}
be a
r\times r
matrix-valued function.
We define the forward OE of
F(t)
, denoted

\left(\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}F(s)~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}\right)_{t\geq 0}
, as the
r\times r

matrix-valued function that solves the differential equation
(assuming the solution exists and is unique)

(1.13)

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}F(s)~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}

\displaystyle=\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}F(s)~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}F(t),\qquad\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{0}F(s)\mathrm{d}s}\right\}=\mathsf{Id}_{r},

recalling that
\mathsf{Id}_{r}
denotes the identity matrix.

OEs are known in the literature under many different names
(e.g., path-ordered or time-ordered exponentials, product integrals, Volterra integrals),
which is a testament to their appearance in a wide variety of mathematical
problems. See
[
17
,
35
,
61
]
for
a comprehensive survey of the theory of OEs and their applications.
Our interest in OEs lies in their appearance in the vector-valued extension of the classical Feynman-Kac
formula in Theorem
1.4
, as per the following result of Güneysu:

Theorem 1.6

(
[
38
, Theorem 1.8]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{H}
be as in (
1.12
).
If
\mathcal{V}
is sufficiently well-behaved (
[
38
, Definition 1.3]
), then for every
t>0
,
\mathrm{e}^{-t\mathcal{H}}
has the (
r\times r
matrix) kernel

(1.14)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-t\mathcal{H}}(x,y)=\Pi_{B}(t;x,y)\mathbf{E}\left[\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{V}(B^{x,y}_{t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}\right],\qquad t\geq 0,~x,y\in\mathbb{R}.

In this context, the problem that we contend with in this paper is as follows:

Problem 1.7
.

Understand (
1.14
)
when
\mathcal{H}=\widehat{H}
(i.e.,
\mathcal{V}=V+\xi
).

1.3.2.
Difficulty: Interpreting Feynman-Kac with a Matrix White Noise

The main difficulty that we encounter in attacking Problem
1.7
is that
\xi
is a Schwartz distribution,
which fails to satisfy the standard regularity assumptions required for the Feynman-Kac formulas stated in Theorems

1.4
and
1.6
. In particular,
since
\xi
is not defined pointwise, it is not immediately obvious what the
expression
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}
should mean from the point of view of the matrix ODE
in (
1.13
).

In the scalar case
r=1
, a rigorous interpretation of the exponential integral
\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B(s))~\mathrm{d}s}
was provided
for the SAO in
[
32
,
36
]
—with a later extension to more general operators in

[
28
]
—as follows:

Definition 1.8
.

For any Borel set
\mathcal{K}\subset[0,\infty)
, we let
\big(L^{x}_{\mathcal{K}}(B)\big)_{x\in\mathbb{R}}
denote the continuous version
of the local time of
B
on
\mathcal{K}
, that is,

(1.15)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{K}}f\big(B(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s=\int_{\mathbb{R}}L^{x}_{\mathcal{K}}(B)f(x)~\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for every measurable }f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}.

In the case where
\mathcal{K}=[0,t)
, we use the convention
L^{x}_{[0,t)}(B)=L^{x}_{t}(B)
.

If
W
is the Brownian motion such that
\xi=W^{\prime}
,
then a formal application of (
1.15
)
suggests that
\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B(s))~\mathrm{d}s}
can be defined rigorously via

(1.16)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\xi\big(B(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}W^{\prime}\big(B(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s=\int_{\mathbb{R}}L_{t}^{x}(B)W^{\prime}(x)~\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\mathbb{R}}L_{t}^{x}(B)~\mathrm{d}W(x),

where the rightmost expression in (
1.16
) is interpreted as a stochastic integral.
However, (
1.16
) cannot work when
r>1
due to the non-commutativity
of the matrix function
s\mapsto\xi\big(B(s)\big)
. To see this, we recall the series/limit representations
commonly used to calculate or approximate OEs
(see, e.g.,
[
61
, Definition 2.4.1 and Theorems 2.4.3, 2.4.12, and 2.5.1]

for details on the assumptions on
F
required for (
1.17
)
and (
1.18
) to hold):

Theorem 1.9

(Informal)
.

Equivalent definitions of OEs include:

\bullet

(Product Integral.)
For every
t>0
, one has

(1.17)

\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}F(s)~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{t}{n}F(0)}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{t}{n}F(t/n)}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{t}{n}F(2t/n)}\cdots\mathrm{e}^{\frac{t}{n}F(t)}\\
=\lim_{n\to\infty}\big(\mathsf{Id}_{r}+\tfrac{t}{n}F(0)\big)\big(\mathsf{Id}_{r}+\tfrac{t}{n}F(t/n)\big)\big(\mathsf{Id}_{r}+\tfrac{t}{n}F(2t/n)\big)\cdots\big(\mathsf{Id}_{r}+\tfrac{t}{n}F(t)\big).

\bullet

(Dyson Series.)
For every
t>0
, one has

(1.18)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}F(s)~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}_{r}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\cdots\int_{0}^{s_{3}}\int_{0}^{s_{2}}F(s_{1})F(s_{2})\cdots F(s_{n})~\mathrm{d}s_{1}\mathrm{d}s_{2}\cdots\mathrm{d}s_{n}.

With this in hand, if we formally apply (e.g.) (
1.17
) to write

(1.19)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}\xi(B(s))\mathrm{d}s}\right\}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t}{n}\xi(B(0))}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t}{n}\xi(B(t/n))}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t}{n}\xi(B(2t/n))}\cdots\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{t}{n}\xi(B(t))},

then it becomes clear that we cannot calculate this quantity if we only know
how often
B
’s path
visits each coordinate
x\in\mathbb{R}
(i.e., the local time
L^{x}_{t}(B)
):
The noncommutativity of
\xi\big(B(kt/n)\big)
for different
k
’s means that we must know the order
in which each coordinate is visited by
B
(i.e., the entire history of the path

\big(B(s):0\leq s\leq t\big)
).

Finally, in the absence of an obvious candidate
for a pathwise interpretation of (
1.19
),
a natural strategy to define such an object is to use smooth approximations.
That is, introduce a sequence of smooth matrix noises
(\xi^{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon>0}

such that
\xi^{\varepsilon}\to\xi
as
\varepsilon\to 0
in the space of Schwartz distributions,
and then try to interpret the limit of the corresponding Feynman-Kac formulas.
However, the standard approximation theory of OEs
is not powerful enough to carry this out.
To illustrate this,
one such standard estimate is as follows (see, e.g.,
[
61
, Corollary 3.4.3]
):

\left\|\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{V}(B(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}-\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}\overline{\mathcal{V}}(B(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathcal{V}(B(s))\|_{\mathrm{op}}~\mathrm{d}s}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}\|\mathcal{V}(B(s))-\overline{\mathcal{V}}(B(s))\|_{\mathrm{op}}~\mathrm{d}s}-1\right),

where we denote the operator norm by

\|F\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\sup_{v\in\mathbb{F}^{r}}\frac{\|Fv\|_{\ell^{2}}}{\|v\|_{\ell^{2}}}.

For instance, a variation of this is used in
[
38
]
to prove
Theorem
1.6

for certain locally-integrable potentials
\mathcal{V}
,
starting from the easier assumption that
\overline{\mathcal{V}}

is continuous and bounded.
However, this strategy fails for white noise since
\|\xi(x)\|_{\mathrm{op}}=\infty
.

1.3.3.
Mixed Moments via a Probabilistic Representation of Ordered Exponentials

The key to our ability to compute the joint moments (
1.2
) is to use
a method
to represent ordered exponentials that is probabilistic in nature. A simple
version of this representation is as follows:

Definition 1.10
.

Let
\big(U(t)\big)_{t\geq 0}
be a uniform continuous-time random walk on
the set
\{1,\ldots,r\}

with jump rate
(r-1)
. That is, if
N(t)
is a Poisson process with rate
(r-1)

and
M
is a discrete Markov chain on
\{1,\ldots,r\}
with transition matrix

P_{i,j}=\begin{cases}0&\text{if }i=j,\\
\frac{1}{r-1}&\text{if }i\neq j,\end{cases}

then we can construct
U
as

U(t)=M\big(N(t)\big),\qquad t\geq 0,

assuming
M
and
N
are independent.
Next, we denote the jump times of the Poisson process
N
as

0=\tau_{0}\leq\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq\cdots
, and the corresponding jumps in
U
and
M
as

J_{k}=\big(U(\tau_{k-1}),U(\tau_{k})\big)=\big(M(k-1),M(k)\big)\qquad\text{for }k=1,2,3,\ldots.

Finally, we use
\Pi_{U}
to denote
U
’s transition kernel,
and
U^{i,j}_{t}
denotes the process
U

conditioned on the event
\big\{U(0)=i\text{ and }U(t)=j\big\}

(noting that this also induces a conditioning on
N,\tau_{k}
, and
J_{k}
).

Proposition 1.11

(Informal)
.

Let
\big(F(t)\big)_{t\geq 0}
be a continuous function that takes values in the space
of
r\times r
self-adjoint matrices.
Define the process

\mathfrak{o}_{t}(F,U)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)t}&\text{if }N(t)=0\\
\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)t}\prod_{k=1}^{N(t)}F_{J_{k}}(\tau_{k})&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

For every
t\geq 0
and
1\leq i,j\leq r
, one has

(1.20)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}F(s)~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}_{i,j}=\Pi_{U}(t;i,j)\mathbf{E}\left[\mathfrak{o}_{t}(F,U^{i,j}_{t})\exp\left(\int_{0}^{t}F_{U^{i,j}_{t}(s),U^{i,j}_{t}(s)}(s)~\mathrm{d}s\right)\right].

We postpone a discussion of Proposition
1.11
’s appearance in past
results to Section
1.3.4
, and focus for now on
explaining how it is used in the calculation of
the joint moments (
1.2
):
If we combine (
1.20
) with the
Feynman-Kac formula stated
in (
1.14
) (without worrying about
whether the hypotheses of the two results match for now), then we obtain the
following informal restatement of Theorem
1.6
:

Theorem 1.12

(Informal)
.

If
\mathcal{H}f(i,\cdot)=-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(i,\cdot)+\sum_{j}\mathcal{V}_{i,j}f(j,\cdot)
is as in (
1.12
),
then

(1.21)

\mathrm{e}^{-t\mathcal{H}}(x,y)_{i,j}=\Pi_{B}(t;x,y)\Pi_{U}(t;i,j)\\
\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\mathfrak{o}_{t}(-\mathcal{V}\circ B^{x,y}_{t},U^{i,j}_{t})\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{V}_{U^{i,j}_{t}(s),U^{i,j}_{t}(s)}\big(B^{x,y}_{t}(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s\right)\right]

for any
t\geq 0
,
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
1\leq i,j\leq r
;
assuming that
B
and
U
are independent,
and where
f\circ g=f\big(g(t)\big)
denotes function composition.

On the one hand, if
\xi
is a matrix white noise, then the term

\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{U(s),U(s)}\big(B(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s

(which would be contained in the exponential in (
1.21
) if

\mathcal{V}
contains
\xi
)
admits a straightforward pathwise interpretation similar to (
1.16
):
Recalling the definition of local time in (
1.15
)
and letting
\xi_{i,i}=W_{i,i}^{\prime}
for some i.i.d. Brownian motions
(W_{i,i})_{1\leq i\leq r}
,
if we denote the sets
\mathcal{K}_{t}(i)=\{s\in[0,t):U(s)=i\}
for all
t\geq 0
and
1\leq i\leq r
, then

(1.22)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\xi_{U(s),U(s)}\big(B(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{\mathcal{K}_{t}(i)}\xi_{i,i}\big(B(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}L_{\mathcal{K}_{t}(i)}^{x}(B)~\mathrm{d}W_{i,i}(x).

Thus, the only difficulty in interpreting Theorem
1.12

when the potential
\mathcal{V}
contains a matrix white noise comes from the product term

(1.23)

\displaystyle\prod_{k=1}^{N(t)}\xi_{J_{k}}\big(B(\tau_{k})\big),

which appears in the process
\mathfrak{o}_{t}(-\mathcal{V}\circ B,U)
if
\mathcal{V}=V+\xi
.
Indeed, since
(
1.23
)

does not involve any kind of integral (either with respect to space or time), it is not clear that we can exploit
the interpretation of
\int f(x)\xi(x)~\mathrm{d}x
as a stochastic integral to make sense of
this product.

In this context, one of the main insights of this paper—which leads to our ability to calculate
the trace moments in (
1.2
)—is
that the expression (
1.23
)
admits a fairly tractable rigorous interpretation when we take an expectation
with respect to
\xi
. More specifically, if
\xi^{\varepsilon}
is
a smooth matrix noise that approximates
\xi
, then
we can use Isserlis’ theorem (e.g.,
[
51
, (1.7) and (1.8)]
; see
[
43
]
for Isserlis’ original paper) to calculate

(1.24)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\xi^{\varepsilon}}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{N(t)}\xi^{\varepsilon}_{J_{k}}\big(B(\tau_{k})\big)\right],

where
\mathbf{E}_{\xi^{\varepsilon}}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\xi^{\varepsilon}
only,
conditional on
B
and
U
(which we assume are both independent of
\xi^{\varepsilon}
):

Definition 1.13
.

Let
\mathcal{P}_{0}=\varnothing
be the empty set, and
given an even integer
n\geq 2
, let
\mathcal{P}_{n}
denote the set of perfect pair matchings
of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
. That is,
\mathcal{P}_{n}
is the set

\Big\{\big\{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\},\{\ell_{3},\ell_{4}\},\ldots,\{\ell_{n-1},\ell_{n}\}\big\}:\ell_{i}\in\{1,\ldots,n\}\text{ and }\{\ell_{i},\ell_{i+1}\}\cap\{\ell_{j},\ell_{j+1}\}=\varnothing\text{ if }i\neq j\Big\}.

Following the statement of Isserlis’ theorem, (
1.24
)
can be simplified to

(1.25)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{N(t)\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(t)}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\varepsilon}_{J_{\ell_{1}}}\big(B(\tau_{\ell_{1}})\big)\xi^{\varepsilon}_{J_{\ell_{2}}}\big(B(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big)\right],

assuming that a sum over
p\in\mathcal{P}_{0}
is equal to 1 by convention.
Informally, the correlation of the Gaussian white noise is given by a multiple of the
Dirac mass kernel;
\mathbf{E}\big[\xi_{i,j}(x)\xi_{i,j}(y)^{*}\big]=c\delta_{0}(x-y)
for some
c>0
.
Thus, when
\varepsilon\to 0
, the covariance (
1.25
) will approximate
the expression

(1.26)

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{\{N(t)\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(t)}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{J}(J_{\ell_{1}},J_{\ell_{2}})}\cdot c\delta_{0}\big(B(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-B(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big),

where the indicator
\mathbf{1}_{\mathfrak{J}(J_{\ell_{1}},J_{\ell_{2}})}

essentially ensures that the jumps
J_{\ell_{1}}
and
J_{\ell_{2}}
are between the same elements in
\{1,\ldots,r\}

(the situation is a bit more complicated when
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}
)—otherwise the covariance is zero by the independence
of the noises
(\xi_{i,j})_{1\leq i<j\leq r}
.
While (
1.26
) is ill-defined on its own,
we recall that in Theorem
1.12
,
the product (
1.23
) sits inside an integral/expectation with respect to the laws of
B
and
U
.
In this context, we can interpret each nonvanishing summand in (
1.26
) as inducing a conditioning
on the probability-zero event

(1.27)

\displaystyle\big\{B(\tau_{\ell_{1}})=B(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\text{ for every }\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p\big\}.

In rigorous terms, this can be defined by resampling the pairs of jump times
\{\tau_{\ell_{1}},\tau_{\ell_{2}}\}

according to
B
’s self-intersection local time measure instead of the usual Poisson process measure;
see Definitions
4.4
and
4.8

and Remark
4.5
for the
details.

In summary: While the approach developed in this paper does not
exhibit a pathwise interpretation of the Feynman-Kac
formula for vector-valued operators with matrix white noise
(see Section
1.4
for a discussion of this problem), Proposition

1.11

nevertheless leads to a probabilistic interpretation
of any observable that relies
on the computation of the joint moments

(1.28)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}}(x_{1},y_{1})_{i_{1},j_{1}}\cdots\mathrm{e}^{-t_{n}\widehat{H}}(x_{n},y_{n})_{i_{n},j_{n}}\big].

The trace moments in (
1.2
) are one notable example of such observables,
since

\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}\big]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}(x,x)\big]~\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}(x,x)_{i,i}~\mathrm{d}x.

Thus, the proof of our main result, Theorem
4.9
, involves
the following two steps:

\bullet

In
Theorem
3.5
, we prove a rigorous version of
Theorem
1.12
that holds for
operators of the form
\widehat{H}=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta+V+\xi
, where

\xi
is a regular matrix noise (i.e., the components
\xi_{i,j}
are Gaussian processes
with continuous sample paths).

\bullet

Consider the operators
\widehat{H}=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta+V+\xi
and
\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta+V+\xi^{\varepsilon}

for
\varepsilon>0
, where
\xi
is a matrix white noise and
\xi^{\varepsilon}
are smooth approximations
such that
\xi^{\varepsilon}\to\xi
as
\varepsilon\to 0
. With Theorem
3.5
,
we can compute the mixed moments

(1.29)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}}\big]\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}}\big]\cdots\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{n}\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}}\big]\right],\qquad n\in\mathbb{N},~t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}\geq 0.

We then obtain the statement of
Theorem
4.9

by computing the
\varepsilon\to 0
limit of (
1.29
)
using the process outlined in (
1.24
)–(
1.27
),
and then showing that the expectations in (
1.29
)
also converge to the mixed moments in (
1.2
).

Remark 1.14
.

Contrary to what is suggested by the informal discussion in this section,
the proof of Theorem
3.5
is not simply
a matter of applying Proposition
1.11
to already-known
Feynman-Kac formulas, such as Theorem

1.12
.
This is in part because (to the best of our knowledge) the standard semigroup theory
for vector-valued Schrödinger operators concerns domains with no boundary
and matrix potentials
\mathcal{V}
that are complex-valued (e.g.,
[
38
]
, and the comprehensive survey

[
39
]
).
In contrast, a crucial
feature of Theorem
3.5
(which is explained by our specific interest in the multivariate SAO)
is that we allow operators that act on domains with
a boundary, and with potential functions that take values in matrices with quaternion entries.
Both of these requirements impose novel difficulties. Thus, some of the work
carried out in this paper—which partly explains its length—consists
of proving the Feynman-Kac formula in Theorem
3.5
”from scratch.”
We point to
Remark
3.8
for more details regarding the issue of boundaries, and to Remark
2.2
for more details on the issue of quaternions.

1.3.4.
Past Results

In closing this subsection, we note that the idea of writing
OEs or similar objects using Poisson/Markov jump processes has appeared in various parts
of the mathematics and physics literatures. We begin with two very well-known
special cases of Proposition
1.11
:

Example 1.15
.

Arguably the simplest example is
the special case
F(t)=\mathcal{G}+q(t)
, where
\mathcal{G}
is the generator of the process
U

in Definition
1.10
and
q(t)
is diagonal
with entries denoted

q_{i,i}(t)=q(t,i),\qquad 1\leq i\leq r,~t\geq 0.

In this case,
(
1.20
)
simply reduces to the well-known discrete Feynman-Kac formula:

\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}F(s)~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}_{i,j}=\Pi_{U}(t;i,j)\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}q(s,U^{i,j}_{t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right].

Example 1.16
.

If
F(t)
are the infinitesimal transition matrices
of a (possibly time inhomogeneous) Markov process
M
, then
(
1.13
) is the
Kolmogorov forward equation for
M
’s transition semigroup, whereby

(1.30)

\displaystyle\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{\int_{0}^{t}F(s)~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}_{i,j}=\mathbf{P}\big[M(t)=j~\big|~M(0)=i\big]=\Pi_{M}(t;i,j).

In this case, (
1.20
) is the known change of measure formula that
expresses
M
’s dynamics in terms of
U
; e.g.,
[
45
, Appendix 1, Propositions 2.6 and 7.3]
.

More generally, the idea of using Proposition
1.11

in the context of vector-valued Schrödinger semigroups (thus producing
a vector-valued Feynman-Kac formula similar to
Theorem
1.12
) is also not
new to this work.
For instance,
[
14
,
15
]
introduced the same idea in the context of Pauli Hamiltonians,
which act on vector-valued functions due to the interaction of the particle’s spin with an
external magnetic field. This idea has been extended in the mathematical physics literature
for various applications in the study of quantum systems subjected to magnetic fields, e.g.

[
23
,
24
]
.

Finally, we point out the work
[
13
]
, which proves a probabilistic representation
for an impressive class of deterministic and stochastic PDEs (such as heat, wave, and telegraph)
wherein a Poisson process plays a fundamental role. In particular, the product of the
potential terms
V(t-\tau_{i},X_{\tau_{i}})
in
[
13
, (3.1)]
has striking similarities with
the term
\mathfrak{o}_{t}(-\mathcal{V}\circ B^{x,y}_{t},U^{i,j}_{t})
in (
1.21
).
The result in
[
13
]
was later used in
[
12
]
to study intermittency properties
of the stochastic wave equation.

1.4.
Pathwise Feynman-Kac Formula

As hinted at in the previous section, one notable problem left unsolved in this work is as follows:

Problem 1.17
.

Find a pathwise (with respect to
\xi
) probabilistic representation of
the random semigroup
(\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}:t>0)
.

Given that
\xi=W^{\prime}
is most straightforward to understand once integrated against a
sufficiently regular test function, this would presumably rely on interpreting
the expectation of (
1.23
) with respect to
N
and
B

as an iterated stochastic integral against the joint densities of the points
B(\tau_{k})
.
That said, this would also require understanding the distribution of the exponential

\exp\left(-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{V}_{U^{i,j}_{t}(s),U^{i,j}_{t}(s)}\big(B^{x,y}_{t}(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s\right)

in (
1.21
) once we condition on
B(\tau_{k})

for
1\leq k\leq N(t)
.
We thus leave Problem
1.17
to future investigations.

1.5.
Organization

The remainder of this paper is organized as follows:
In Sections
2
–
4
, we provide the statements of our main results.
More specifically:

\bullet

Section
2
contains results related to
the construction of
\widehat{H}
and its smooth approximations
as self-adjoint operators with compact resolvents, as well as the resulting construction of
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}
by means of a spectral expansion
(i.e.,
Proposition
2.14
).

\bullet

In Section
3
, we state our results regarding
pathwise Feynman-Kac formulas for
\widehat{H}
’s smooth approximations
(i.e.,
Theorem
3.5
).

\bullet

In Section
4
, we state our two main
results, namely the trace moment formula for (
1.2
) (i.e.,
Theorem
4.9
)
and the number rigidity result (i.e.,
Theorem
4.13
).

Then, in Sections
5
–
8

we prove these four results in the order they are stated.

in

]
.

.

2.
Main Results Part 1. Construction of Operators

We now begin the process of providing precise statements for our results.
In this section, we describe the construction of the operator
\widehat{H}

and its smooth approximations
using quadratic forms. Among other things, this culminates in the ability to define
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}

via a spectral expansion; see (
2.11
).

2.1.
Vector-Valued Function Spaces

Following-up on Section
1.3.3

(more specifically Theorem
1.12
), we infer that the dynamics in the Feynman-Kac
formula for
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}

are a combination of a Brownian motion and a random walk on
\{1,\ldots,r\}
.
In order to provide a framework that is adapted to this, we henceforth reformulate vector-valued
functions
f:I\to\mathbb{F}^{r}
as
f:\{1,\ldots,r\}\times I\to\mathbb{F}
.
This difference is only cosmetic, as both options can be viewed as a direct sum of the one-dimensional functions

f(i,\cdot):I\to\mathbb{F}
over
1\leq i\leq r
. However, we settle on the latter to ensure that
the domain of the functions matches the state space of the combined random motions;
this makes for a much neater statement of our Feynman-Kac formulas
than Theorem
1.12
, thus
leading us to the following definitions:

Definition 2.1
.

Let
I\subset\mathbb{R}
and
f,g:I\to\mathbb{F}
.
We use
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
\|\cdot\|_{2}
to denote

\langle f,g\rangle=\displaystyle\int_{I}f(x)^{*}g(x)~\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|f\|_{2}=\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle}.

We let
L^{2}(I,\mathbb{F})
denote the associated Hilbert space.

Next, we denote
\mathcal{A}=\{1,\ldots,r\}\times I
, and we let
\mu
denote
the measure on
\mathcal{A}
obtained by taking the product of the counting measure
on
\{1,\ldots,r\}
and the Lebesgue measure on
I
.
Given
f,g:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{F}
, we denote

\langle f,g\rangle_{\mu}=\int_{\mathcal{A}}f(a)^{*}g(a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)=\big\langle f(1,\cdot),g(1,\cdot)\big\rangle+\cdots+\big\langle f(r,\cdot),g(r,\cdot)\big\rangle;\qquad\textstyle\|f\|_{\mu}=\sqrt{\langle f,f\rangle_{\mu}},

and we let
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
denote the associated Hilbert space; i.e.,

L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})=L^{2}(I,\mathbb{F})^{\oplus r}
.

Finally, Let
C_{0}^{\infty}(I,\mathbb{F})
be the set of
\mathbb{F}
-valued functions that are smooth and
compactly supported on
I
’s closure.
Let
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})=C_{0}^{\infty}(I,\mathbb{F})^{\oplus r}
.

Remark 2.2
.

When
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
,

L^{2}(I,\mathbb{F})
and
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
are bona fide Hilbert spaces on the field
\mathbb{F}
.
However, when
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}
, calling these Hilbert spaces is an abuse of notation:
These spaces are instead

quaternionic Hilbert spaces
, which are actually modules over the ring
\mathbb{H}
;
see,
e.g.,
[
25
, Section 4]
.
That said, we note that
L^{2}(I,\mathbb{H})
and
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{H})
can be made into
standard Hilbert spaces if we view
\mathbb{H}\cong\mathbb{R}^{4}
as a four-dimensional vector space on the field
\mathbb{R}
,
and we replace
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mu}
by the real inner products

(2.1)

\displaystyle\prec f,g\succ=\Re\big(\langle f,g\rangle\big)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\prec f,g\succ_{\mu}=\Re\big(\langle f,g\rangle_{\mu}\big),

where
\Re
denotes the real part.
At first glance, this might appear to be an inconsequential distinction, but this subtlety does
induce nontrivial concerns; most notably, two functions that are orthogonal with respect to
(
2.1
) need not be orthogonal with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
or
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mu}
.
While this does not change which numbers in
\mathbb{R}
are eigenvalues of
\widehat{H}
, it does change the multiplicity of these eigenvalues,
which in turn changes the value of the trace
\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}\big]
by a constant (see (
5.7
) and (
6.5
)
for more details).

With this said, given that
[
5
]
formulates its results using
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mu}

(and that
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}
’s kernel/semigroup is arguably easier to write and manipulate using quaternion multiplication rather than

4\times 4
matrix products over
\mathbb{R}^{4}
), in this paper we choose to state (most of) our results using
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mu}

as well. Nevertheless, we still need (
2.1
) in several places,
since most of the standard functional-analytic
results that we use to construct
\widehat{H}
and
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}
’s kernel
are stated under the assumption that the operator and kernel act on real/complex Hilbert spaces.
We refer to Sections
5.2
and
6.1.2
for
the full details of how this subtlety influences our constructions.

2.2.
Domains and Boundary Conditions

\widehat{H}
is assumed to act on functions on the following domains:

Assumption 2.3
.

I\subset\mathbb{R}
is one of the following three options: The full space
I=\mathbb{R}
,
which we call
Case 1
;
the positive half line
I=(0,\infty)
, which we call
Case 2
; or the bounded interval
I=(0,\vartheta)
for some
\vartheta>0
,
which we call
Case 3
.

In order to ensure that the operators that we consider are self-adjoint,
we must impose some appropriate boundary conditions when
I

has a boundary:

Assumption 2.4
.

In Case 2, we consider
functions
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{F}
that satisfy

(2.2)

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(i,0)+\overline{\alpha}_{i}f(i,0)=0,\qquad 1\leq i\leq r,

for a given vector
\overline{\alpha}=(\overline{\alpha}_{1},\ldots,\overline{\alpha}_{r})\in[-\infty,\infty)^{r}
,
with the convention that
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-\infty
corresponds to the Dirichlet
boundary condition
f(i,0)=0
.
In Case 3, we consider functions that satisfy

f^{\prime}(i,0)+\overline{\alpha}_{i}f(i,0)=0\qquad\text{and}\qquad-f^{\prime}(i,\vartheta)+\overline{\beta}_{i}f(i,\vartheta)=0,\qquad 1\leq i\leq r

for given vectors
\overline{\alpha},\overline{\beta},\in[-\infty,\infty)^{r}
,
once again with the convention that
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-\infty
and
\overline{\beta}_{i}=-\infty

respectively correspond to
f(i,0)=0
and
f(i,\vartheta)=0
.

2.3.
Deterministic and Random Potentials

Next, we describe the potential functions that we consider in
\widehat{H}
.
Most important for this are the following two definitions, which introduce the white
noise and its regular approximations:

Notation 2.5
.

When
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
, we use
\mathsf{i}
to denote the imaginary unit,
and if
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}
, then we use
\mathsf{i}
,
\mathsf{j}
, and
\mathsf{k}

to denote the quaternion imaginary units.

Definition 2.6
.

Let
\varrho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a positive semidefinite function.
We say that the random function

\mathcal{N}:I\to\mathbb{F}
is a regular noise in
\mathbb{F}
with covariance
\varrho:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

if
\mathcal{N}
is continuous with probability one, and we can write

(2.3)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(x)=\begin{cases}\mathcal{X}(x)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(\mathcal{X}(x)+\mathcal{Y}(x)\mathsf{i}\big)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C},\\
\frac{1}{2}\big(\mathcal{X}(x)+\mathcal{Y}(x)\mathsf{i}+\mathcal{Z}(x)\mathsf{j}+\mathcal{W}(x)\mathsf{k}\big)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H},\end{cases}

where
\mathcal{X},\mathcal{Y},\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{W}:I\to\mathbb{R}
are i.i.d. continuous Gaussian processes
with mean zero and
\mathbf{E}\big[\mathcal{X}(x)\mathcal{X}(y)\big]=\varrho(x-y)
.
We define the quadratic form induced by
\mathcal{N}
as

\mathcal{N}(f,g)=\langle f,\mathcal{N}g\rangle,\qquad f,g\in C_{0}^{\infty}(I,\mathbb{F}).

Definition 2.7
.

Let
W
be a standard Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}
.
For every
f,g\in C_{0}^{\infty}(I,\mathbb{F})
, we define

W^{\prime}(f,g)\,``="\,\langle f,W^{\prime}g\rangle
using the formal integration by parts

(2.4)

\displaystyle W^{\prime}(f,g)=\begin{cases}-\langle f^{\prime},Wg\rangle-\langle f,Wg^{\prime}\rangle&\text{Cases 1 and 2},\\
f(\vartheta)^{*}W(\vartheta)g(\vartheta)-\langle f^{\prime},Wg\rangle-\langle f,Wg^{\prime}\rangle&\text{Case 3}.\end{cases}

Then, we define the white noise in
\mathbb{F}
with variance
\sigma^{2}>0
as the bilinear form

(2.5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(f,g)=\begin{cases}\sigma\,W_{1}^{\prime}(f,g)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\\
\frac{\sigma}{\sqrt{2}}\big(W_{1}^{\prime}(f,g)+W_{\mathsf{i}}^{\prime}(f,\mathsf{i}g)\big)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C},\\
\frac{\sigma}{2}\big(W_{1}^{\prime}(f,g)+W_{\mathsf{i}}^{\prime}(f,\mathsf{i}g)+W_{\mathsf{j}}^{\prime}(f,\mathsf{j}g)+W_{\mathsf{k}}^{\prime}(f,\mathsf{k}g)\big)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H},\end{cases}

where
W_{1},W_{\mathsf{i}},W_{\mathsf{j}}
, and
W_{\mathsf{k}}
are i.i.d. standard Brownian motions.

With these definitions in hand, we now provide a precise description of
V+\xi
:

Assumption 2.8
.

For every
1\leq i\leq r
, the function
V(i,\cdot):I\to\mathbb{R}
is bounded below and
locally integrable on
I
’s closure. Moreover, in Cases 1 and 2, there exists a constant

\mathfrak{a}>0
such that

\lim_{|x|\to\infty}\frac{V(i,x)}{|x|^{\mathfrak{a}}}=\infty\qquad\text{for all }1\leq i\leq r.

Let
\xi
be the bilinear form on
C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})^{2}
defined as

(2.6)

\displaystyle\xi(f,g)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{r}\xi_{i,j}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(j,\cdot)\big),

where

(1)

\big(\xi_{i,i}:1\leq i\leq r\big)
are i.i.d. copies of a regular or white noise in
\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

\big(\xi_{i,j}:1\leq i<j\leq r\big)
are i.i.d. copies of a regular or white noise in
\mathbb{F}
.

(3)

For
j<i
, we let
\xi_{j,i}=\xi_{i,j}^{*}
.

(4)

\big(\xi_{i,i}:1\leq i\leq r\big)
and
\big(\xi_{i,j}:1\leq i<j\leq r\big)
are independent of each other.

Remark 2.9
.

We note that, in Assumption
2.8
, we allow
the situation where the diagonal entries
\xi_{i,i}
are white noises
and the off-diagonal entries
\xi_{i,j}
are regular noises, and vice-versa.
This detail will become important once we outline the proof of our main
theorem in Section
7.1
.

Remark 2.10
.

As advertised in the introduction, Assumptions
2.3
,

2.4
, and
2.8
are partly designed
to include the multivariate SAO as a special case. That is, the multivariate SAO corresponds to
Case 2 with
V(i,\cdot)=\frac{rx}{2}
and
\xi
defined as follows:

\xi_{i,i}\text{ are white noises in $\mathbb{R}$ with variance}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\\
\frac{1}{2}&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C},\\
\frac{1}{4}&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H};\end{cases}

and
\xi_{i,j}
are white noises in
\mathbb{F}
with variance
\frac{1}{2}
when
i\neq j
.

2.4.
Operators via Quadratic Forms

We now provide a rigorous construction of
\widehat{H}
using quadratic forms.
We begin with the quadratic forms for
H=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta+V
:

Definition 2.11
.

Let
\mathrm{AC}(I,\mathbb{F})
denote the set of functions
f:I\to\mathbb{F}
that are locally absolutely
continuous on
I
’s closure. Given a function
h:I\to\mathbb{R}
that is bounded below and locally integrable,
let

\mathrm{H}^{1}_{h}(I,\mathbb{F})=\big\{f\in\mathrm{AC}(I,\mathbb{F}):\|f\|_{2},\|f^{\prime}\|_{2},\|h_{+}^{1/2}f\|_{2}<\infty\big\},

where
h_{+}(x)=\max\{0,h(x)\}
.
For each
1\leq i\leq r
, we define the bilinear form

\mathcal{E}_{i}
and the
form domain
D(\mathcal{E}_{i})\subset\mathrm{H}^{1}_{V(i,\cdot)}(I,\mathbb{F})
of the scalar-valued operator

(2.7)

\displaystyle H_{i}f(x)=-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(x)+V(i,x)f(x),\qquad f:I\to\mathbb{F}

(with boundary
f^{\prime}(0)-\overline{\alpha}_{i}f(0)=0
and
f^{\prime}(\vartheta)-\overline{\beta}_{i}f(\vartheta)
in Cases 2 and 3) as follows:

(1)

In Case 1, we let

\begin{cases}D(\mathcal{E}_{i})=\mathrm{H}^{1}_{V(i,\cdot)}(I,\mathbb{F}),\\
\mathcal{E}_{i}(f,g)=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\rangle+\langle f,V(i,\cdot)g\rangle.\end{cases}

(2)

In Case 2, if
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-\infty
, we let

\begin{cases}D(\mathcal{E}_{i})=\left\{f\in\mathrm{H}^{1}_{V(i,\cdot)}(I,\mathbb{F}):f(0)=0\right\},\\
\mathcal{E}_{i}(f,g)=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\rangle+\langle f,V(i,\cdot)g\rangle;\end{cases}

and if
\overline{\alpha}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, we let

\begin{cases}D(\mathcal{E}_{i})=\mathrm{H}^{1}_{V(i,\cdot)}(I,\mathbb{F}),\\
\mathcal{E}_{i}(f,g)=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\rangle-\tfrac{\overline{\alpha}_{i}}{2}f(0)^{*}g(0)+\langle f,V(i,\cdot)g\rangle.\end{cases}

(3)

In Case 3, if
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=\overline{\beta}_{i}=-\infty
, then

\begin{cases}D(\mathcal{E}_{i})=\left\{f\in\mathrm{H}^{1}_{V(i,\cdot)}(I,\mathbb{F}):f(0)=f(\vartheta)=0\right\},\\
\mathcal{E}_{i}(f,g)=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\rangle+\langle f,V(i,\cdot)g\rangle;\end{cases}

if
\overline{\alpha}_{i},\overline{\beta}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, then

\begin{cases}D(\mathcal{E}_{i})=\mathrm{H}^{1}_{V(i,\cdot)}(I,\mathbb{F}),\\
\mathcal{E}_{i}(f,g)=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\rangle-\tfrac{\overline{\alpha}_{i}}{2}f(0)^{*}g(0)-\tfrac{\overline{\beta}_{i}}{2}f(\vartheta)^{*}g(\vartheta)+\langle f,V(i,\cdot)g\rangle;\end{cases}

and if
\overline{\alpha}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\overline{\beta}_{i}=-\infty
, then

\begin{cases}D(\mathcal{E}_{i})=\left\{f\in\mathrm{H}^{1}_{V(i,\cdot)}(I,\mathbb{F}):f(\vartheta)=0\right\},\\
\mathcal{E}_{i}(f,g)=\tfrac{1}{2}\langle f^{\prime},g^{\prime}\rangle-\tfrac{\overline{\alpha}_{i}}{2}f(0)^{*}g(0)+\langle f,V(i,\cdot)g\rangle\end{cases}

(if
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-\infty
and
\overline{\beta}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, then we define

D(\mathcal{E}_{i})
and
\mathcal{E}_{i}
in the same way as above, except that
we interchange the roles of
\overline{\alpha}_{i}
and
\overline{\beta}_{i}
, and
\vartheta
and
0
).

Then, we define the bilinear form
\mathcal{E}
and form domain
D(\mathcal{E})=D(\mathcal{E}_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus D(\mathcal{E}_{r})

of the operator
H=H_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus H_{r}

as

\mathcal{E}(f,g)=\mathcal{E}_{1}\big(f(1,\cdot),g(1,\cdot)\big)+\cdots+\mathcal{E}_{r}\big(f(r,\cdot),g(r,\cdot)\big),\qquad f,g\in D(\mathcal{E}).

Finally, we define the following norm on
D(\mathcal{E})
:

\|f\|^{2}_{\star}=\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|V_{+}^{1/2}f\|_{\mu}^{2}.

The quadratic form associated with the noise
\xi
is defined in (
2.6
).
That said, this form is a-priori only defined on smooth and compactly supported functions. If we want to define
the operator
\widehat{H}=H+\xi
via quadratic forms, then we have to ensure that the associated forms are well-defined
on the same domain. To this effect:

Proposition 2.12
.

Let Assumptions
2.3
,
2.4
, and
2.8
hold.
There exists a large enough
C>0
so that

(2.8)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{+1}=\sqrt{\mathcal{E}(f,f)+(C+1)\|f\|_{\mu}}

is a norm on
D(\mathcal{E})
, which is
equivalent to
\|f\|_{\star}
.
Moreover, almost surely,
for every
\kappa\in(0,1)
there exists a finite
\nu>0
such that

(2.9)

\displaystyle|\xi(f,f)|\leq\kappa\mathcal{E}(f,f)+\nu\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}\qquad\text{for every }f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F}).

Thanks to Proposition
2.12
,
almost surely,
there exists a unique continuous (with respect to
\|\cdot\|_{\star}
) extension of
\xi
to a quadratic form on

D(\mathcal{E})
, which satisfies (
2.9
) on all of
D(\mathcal{E})
. Thus:

Definition 2.13
.

We define the quadratic form of
\widehat{H}
on the form domain
D(\mathcal{E})
as

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(f,f)=\mathcal{E}(f,f)+\xi(f,f),\qquad f\in D(\mathcal{E}).

We now finally arrive at the main result in this section:

Proposition 2.14
.

Let Assumptions
2.3
,
2.4
, and
2.8
hold.
Almost surely, there exists a unique self-adjoint operator
\widehat{H}

with dense domain
D(\widehat{H})\subset L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
such that

(1)

D(\widehat{H})\subset D(\mathcal{E})
,

(2)

\langle f,\widehat{H}f\rangle_{\mu}=\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(f,f)
for all
f\in D(\widehat{H})
, and

(3)

\widehat{H}
has compact resolvent.

In particular, we can define the eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}(\widehat{H})\leq\lambda_{2}(\widehat{H})\leq\cdots

and corresponding eigenfunctions
\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})
using the variational theorem:
For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, one has

(2.10)

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})=\inf_{f\in D(\mathcal{E}),~f\perp\psi_{1}(\widehat{H}),\ldots,\psi_{k-1}(\widehat{H})}\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(f,f)}{\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}},

and
f=\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})
achieves the infimum.
The set
\big(\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded below and has no accumulation point, and
the set
\big(\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})\big)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
forms an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
.
Thus, for every
t>0
,

(2.11)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}f=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})\big\langle\psi_{k}(\widehat{H}),f\big\rangle_{\mu}.

Remark 2.15
.

If
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}
in Proposition
2.14
,
then when we say that
\widehat{H}
is self-adjoint with compact resolvent, we mean so with
\widehat{H}

viewed as a operator on the bona fide Hilbert space over
\mathbb{R}
endowed with
the real-inner product in (
2.1
). However, every
other claim uses
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mu}
. This includes the
orthogonality relation
\perp
in the variational formula (
2.10
),
and the claim that the eigenfunctions form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{H})
,
in the sense that each
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{H})
has the unique series representation

f=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})\big\langle\psi_{k}(\widehat{H}),f\big\rangle_{\mu}.

We further note that the order in the product
\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})\big\langle\psi_{k}(\widehat{H}),f\big\rangle_{\mu}
matters in
\mathbb{H}

due to the lack of commutativity, which explains our peculiar way of writing (
2.11
).

3.
Main Results Part 2. Feynman-Kac Formula for Regular Noise

In this section, we discuss our Feynman-Kac formula for the semigroup
(
2.11
) in the case where
\xi
’s
components are regular noises.

3.1.
Stochastic Processes

We begin by defining the stochastic processes that arise in
the scalar Feynman-Kac formula.

Definition 3.1
.

We use
B
to denote a standard Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}
,

X
to denote a reflected standard Brownian motion on
(0,\infty)
,
and
Y
to denote a reflected standard Brownian motion on
(0,\vartheta)
.
Then, we use
Z
to denote the generic ”reflected” Brownian motion on
I
,
namely,

Z=\begin{cases}B&\text{Case 1,}\\
X&\text{Case 2,}\\
Y&\text{Case 3.}\end{cases}

In Cases 2 and 3,
given a boundary point
c\in\partial I
,
we let
\big(\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{[s,t)}(Z):0\leq s<t\big)

denote the continuous version (jointly in
s
and
t
) of
Z
’s boundary local time. That is,
for every
0\leq s<t
, we have the almost-sure equalities

(3.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{[s,t)}(Z)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{0\leq Z(u)<\varepsilon\}}~\mathrm{d}u\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{[s,t)}(Y)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}\int_{s}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{\{\vartheta-\varepsilon<Y(u)\leq\vartheta\}}~\mathrm{d}u

(
\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{[s,t)}(Z)
is used in both Cases 2 and 3,
whereas
\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{[s,t)}(Y)
is only used in Case 3).
See, e.g.,
[
59
, Chapter VI, Theorem 1.7 and Corollary 1.9]

for a proof that a continuous version of this process exists.
Finally, for any
t>0
, we denote
\mathfrak{L}_{t}^{c}(Z)=\mathfrak{L}_{[0,t)}^{c}(Z)
.

Next, we introduce the processes in the vector-valued formula:

Definition 3.2
.

Let
U(t)
and
\tau_{k}
be as in Definition
1.10
(i.e., uniform continuous-time random walk on
the complete graph with vertices
\{1,\ldots,r\}
with jump rate
(r-1)
).
We define the Markov process
A(t)=\big(U(t),Z(t)\big)
, assuming that
U
and
Z

are independent.
See Figure
1
in Appendix
A
for an illustration
of
A
’s path, which takes values in
\mathcal{A}
.

In Cases 2 and 3,
for every
1\leq i\leq r
,
c\in\partial I
, and
0\leq s\leq t
, we define

(3.2)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{(i,c)}_{[s,t)}(A)=\sum_{k\geq 1:~U(\tau_{k-1})=i}\mathfrak{L}_{[\tau_{k-1},\tau_{k})\cap[s,t)}^{c}(Z);

i.e.,
Z
’s boundary local time at
c
restricted to
the subset
\{u\in[s,t):U(u)=i\}
. We note

\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{[s,t)}(Z)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\mathfrak{L}^{(i,c)}_{[s,t)}(A).

However, we are more interested in the
combination of these boundary local times weighted by the constants
\overline{\alpha}
and
\overline{\beta}

that determine the boundary conditions
in Assumption

2.4
,
thus leading us to define the boundary term

(3.3)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A)=\begin{cases}0&\text{Case 1,}\\
\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}\overline{\alpha}_{i}\mathfrak{L}^{(i,0)}_{t}(A)&\text{Case 2,}\\
\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}\overline{\alpha}_{i}\mathfrak{L}^{(i,0)}_{t}(A)+\sum_{i=1}^{r}\overline{\beta}_{i}\mathfrak{L}^{(i,\vartheta)}_{t}(A)&\text{Case 3.}\end{cases}

Finally, we state a general notation for random paths conditioned on their start and end points,
which in the sequel will be applied to
U,Z,A,
and other processes, as well as some notation
for conditional expectations:

Definition 3.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}=\big(\mathfrak{X}(t)\big)_{t\geq 0}
be any continuous-time random process
with càdlàg sample paths.
We denote
\mathfrak{X}
’s transition kernel by

\Pi_{\mathfrak{X}}(t;x,y)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{X}(t)=y|\mathfrak{X}(0)=x]&\text{if $\mathfrak{X}$ is discrete,}\\
\mathbf{P}[\mathfrak{X}(t)\in\mathrm{d}y|\mathfrak{X}(0)=x]&\text{if $\mathfrak{X}$ is continuous.}\end{cases}

For any states
x,y
and any time
t\geq 0
, we denote
the conditionings

\mathfrak{X}^{x}=\big(\mathfrak{X}\big|\mathfrak{X}(0)=x\big)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathfrak{X}^{x,y}_{t}=\big(\mathfrak{X}\big|\mathfrak{X}(0)=x\text{ and }\mathfrak{X}(t)=y\big).

Definition 3.4
.

Given a random variable/process
\mathfrak{X}
and a functional
F(\mathfrak{X})

that may depend on other sources of randomness, we let

\mathbf{E}{{}_{\mathfrak{X}}}\big[F(\mathfrak{X})\big]
denote the expectation with respect to
\mathfrak{X}

only, conditional on any other source of randomness.

3.2.
Regular Feynman-Kac Formula

Theorem 3.5
.

Let Assumptions
2.3
,
2.4
, and
2.8
hold,
assuming further that each entry
\xi_{i,j}
is a
regular
noise
in the sense of Definition
2.6
.
To simplify our notation, let us write
V+\xi
’s diagonal part as

S(i,x)=V(i,x)+\xi_{i,i}(x),\qquad 1\leq i\leq r,~x\in I,

and the non-diagonal part as

Q_{i,j}(x)=\xi_{i,j}(x),\qquad 1\leq i\neq j\leq r,~x\in I.

Denote the functional

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)=\begin{cases}\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)t}&\text{if }N(t)=0,\\
\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)t}(-1)^{N(t)}\left(\prod_{k=1}^{N(t)}\xi_{J_{k}}\big(Z(\tau_{k})\big)\right)&\text{if }N(t)>0,\end{cases}

where we recall that
N(t)
,
\tau_{k}
, and
J_{k}
were introduced in
Definition
1.10
.
Given
t>0
, let
\widehat{K}(t)
be the random integral operator on
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
with kernel

(3.5)

\displaystyle\widehat{K}(t;a,b)=\Pi_{A}(t;a,b)\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a,b}_{t})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a,b}_{t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a,b}_{t})}\right],\qquad a,b\in\mathcal{A},

where for
a=(i,x)
and
b=(j,y)
, one has
\Pi_{A}(t;a,b)=\Pi_{Z}(t;x,y)\Pi_{U}(t;i,j)

(
\widehat{K}(t)
is random because it depends on the realization of
\xi
via
S
and
Q
).
In other words,

\widehat{K}(t)f(a)=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}(t;a,b)f(b)~\mathrm{d}\mu(b),\qquad f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F}),

where we recall the definition of
\mu
in Definition
2.1
.
Almost surely, for every
t>0
,

(3.6)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}=\widehat{K}(t)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}\big]=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)<\infty.

Before moving on, a few remarks:

Remark 3.6
.

In (
3.5
),
we use the conventions that
x-\infty=-\infty
for all
x\in[-\infty,\infty)
, and that
\mathrm{e}^{-\infty}=0
.
Thus, when one of the boundary conditions is Dirichlet (in the convention of
Assumption
2.4
,
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-\infty

and/or
\overline{\beta}_{i}=-\infty
for some
1\leq i\leq r
), its contribution to the
boundary term
\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A)
defined in (
3.3
) is interpreted as

\mathrm{e}^{-\infty\mathfrak{L}^{(i,c)}_{t}(Z)}=\mathbf{1}_{\{\mathfrak{L}^{(i,c)}_{t}(Z)=0\}}=\mathbf{1}_{\{Z(s)\neq c\text{ for every }s\in[0,t)\text{ such that }U(s)=i\}}.

Remark 3.7
.

When
r=1
, the process
U(t)
is just constant on
1
,
and
\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)=1
. In this case, we can simply identify
\mathcal{A}=\{1\}\times I=I
,
and Theorem
3.5
reduces to
the one-dimensional Feynman-Kac formula for
Hf=-\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}+Sf
,
with
S(x)=V(x)+\xi(x)
:

(3.7)

\displaystyle\widehat{K}(t;x,y)=\begin{cases}\Pi_{B}(t;x,y)\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(B^{x,y}_{t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right]&\text{Case 1,}\\
\Pi_{X}(t;x,y)\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(X^{x,y}_{t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\overline{\alpha}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{t}(X^{x,y}_{t})}\right]&\text{Case 2,}\\
\Pi_{Y}(t;x,y)\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(Y^{x,y}_{t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\overline{\alpha}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{t}(Y^{x,y}_{t})+\overline{\beta}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{t}(Y^{x,y}_{t})}\right]&\text{Case 3.}\end{cases}

See, e.g.,
[
11
,
53
,
62
]

(more precise references can be found in Section
6.4.1
).

Remark 3.8
.

Looking back at (
3.1
),
we can informally write

(3.8)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{[s,t)}(Z)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{s}^{t}\delta_{c}\big(Z(u)\big)~\mathrm{d}u,

where
\delta_{c}
denotes the delta Dirac mass at
c\in\{0,\vartheta\}
.
Thus, the
presence of the boundary terms (i.e., the multiples of
\mathfrak{L}_{t}^{c}(Z)
)
in the scalar Feynman-Kac formula
(
3.7
) in Cases 2 and 3
can be explained by the following heuristic:
The process of going from the scalar full-space Feynman-Kac formula to the half-line or interval
involves

(1)

replacing
B
by the appropriate reflected version
X
or
Y
, and

(2)

replacing
V(x)
by
V(x)-\frac{\overline{\alpha}}{2}\delta_{0}(x)
in Case 2
or
V(x)-\frac{\overline{\alpha}}{2}\delta_{0}(x)-\frac{\overline{\beta}}{2}\delta_{\vartheta}(x)
in Case 3.

(This heuristic can also be justified by examining the quadratic forms of the operators involved;
see Definition
2.11
.) In the vector-valued case, we can
explain the presence of the boundary term
\mathfrak{B}_{t}(Z)
in (
3.5
)
by performing the same heuristic; the only difference is that we replace the entries of the matrix
potential
V+\xi=S+Q
by

(3.9)

\displaystyle\begin{cases}S(i,x)-\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{i}}{2}\delta_{0}(x)&\text{if }i=j\\
Q_{i,j}(x)&\text{if }i\neq j\end{cases}\qquad\text{or}\qquad\begin{cases}S(i,x)-\frac{\overline{\alpha}_{i}}{2}\delta_{0}(x)-\frac{\overline{\beta}_{i}}{2}\delta_{\vartheta}(x)&\text{if }i=j\\
Q_{i,j}(x)&\text{if }i\neq j\end{cases}

in Cases 2 or 3 respectively. By Proposition
1.11
, we understand that
the contribution of
V+\xi
’s diagonal to
\overrightarrow{\mathcal{T}}\left\{\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}V(Z(s))+\xi(Z(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right\}
is in the exponential
\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A(s))~\mathrm{d}s}
; hence
(
3.9
) very naturally leads to

\mathfrak{B}_{t}(Z)
.
In contrast, the contributions of the Dirac masses in (
3.9
)
are arguably more difficult to parse/calculate if we use either
(
1.13
),
(
1.17
), or (
1.18
)
to write the operator exponentials.

4.
Main Results Part 3. Trace Moments and Rigidity

We now finish the statements of our main results with the
Feynman-Kac formulas for the trace moments (
1.2
),
as well as our rigidity result.

4.1.
Combinatorial and Stochastic Process Preliminaries

We begin by introducing the combinatorial objects that
are used to characterize which jump sequences
J_{1},J_{2},J_{3},\ldots
in the process

U
contribute to the sum (
1.26
) (the latter of which
we recall arises from an application of Isserlis’ theorem):

Definition 4.1
.

Given
n\in\mathbb{N}
, let

\mathcal{B}_{n}
denote the set of binary sequences with
n
steps,
namely, sequences of the form
m=(m_{0},m_{1},\ldots,m_{n})\in\{0,1\}^{n+1}
.
Given
h,l\in\{0,1\}
, we let
\mathcal{B}^{h,l}_{n}
denote the set of
sequences
m\in\mathcal{B}_{n}
such that
m_{0}=h
and
m_{n}=l
.

Definition 4.2
.

Suppose we are given a realization of the process
U

in Definition
3.2
.
Let
t\geq 0
be fixed, and suppose that
N(t)\geq 2
is even.
Given any jump
J_{k}=(i,j)
,
we let
J_{k}^{*}=(j,i)
denote that same jump in reverse order.
Given any matching
p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(t)}

(recall Definition
1.13
), we define the constant
\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)
as follows:

(1)

If
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
, then

\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if either $J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}$ or $J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}^{*}$ for all $\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2)

If
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
, then

\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}^{*}$ for all $\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(3)

If
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}
, then

\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)=\begin{cases}\mathfrak{D}_{t}(p,U)&\text{if either $J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}$ or $J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}^{*}$ for all $\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Here, we define
\mathfrak{D}_{t}(p,U)
as follows: A binary sequence
m\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(t)}

is said to respect
(p,J)
if for every
pair
\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p
, the following holds:

(3.1)

if
J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}
, then
\big((m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}}),(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})\big)

are equal to one of the following pairs:
\big((0,0),(1,1)\big)
,
\big((1,1),(0,0)\big)
,

\big((0,1),(1,0)\big)
, or
\big((1,0),(0,1)\big)
.

(3.2)

if
J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}^{*}
, then
\big((m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}}),(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})\big)

are equal to one of the following pairs:
\big((0,0),(0,0)\big)
,
\big((1,1),(1,1)\big)
,

\big((0,1),(1,0)\big)
, or
\big((1,0),(0,1)\big)
.

Lastly, we call any pair
\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p
such that
J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}

and

\big((m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}}),(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})\big)=\big((0,1),(1,0)\big)\text{ or }\big((1,0),(0,1)\big)

a flip, and we let
f(m,p,J)
denote the total number of flips that occur
in the combination of
m,p,
and
J
.
With all this in hand, we finally define

(4.1)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{t}(p,U)=2^{-N(t)/2}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(t)},~m\text{ respects }(p,J)}(-1)^{f(m,p,J)}.

Remark 4.3
.

We refer to Figures
3
,
4
,
5
,

6
, and
7
in Appendix
A
for illustrations
of the objects related to the combinatorial constants in Definition
4.2
.
Moreover, we note that for any
p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(t)}
, the number of binary paths that respect

(p,J)
is at most
2^{N(t)/2}
(since every step
(m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}})
determines
the other step
(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})
that is matched to it via
\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p
). Consequently, in all cases (i.e., for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}
), one has

(4.2)

\displaystyle|\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)|\leq 1.

Next, in order to formalize the singular conditioning on the event
(
1.27
), we introduce self-intersection
local time measures induced by a permutation, the latter of which require
a precise definition of local time processes:

Definition 4.4
.

Let
\big(L^{x}_{[s,t)}(Z):0\leq s<t,~x\in I\big)
denote
the continuous (jointly in
s
,
t
, and
x
) version of
Z
’s local time process on the time intervals

[s,t)
, that is,

(4.3)

\displaystyle\int_{s}^{t}f\big(Z(u)\big)~\mathrm{d}u=\int_{I}L^{x}_{[s,t)}(Z)f(x)~\mathrm{d}x\qquad\text{for all measurable }f:I\to\mathbb{R}.

(see, e.g.,
[
59
, 5, Chapter VI, Corollary 1.6 and Theorem 1.7]
for a proof of the existence of this continuous version). We use the convention
L_{t}^{x}(Z)=L_{[0,t)}^{x}(Z)
.

Next, we define the local time process
\big(L_{[s,t)}^{a}(A):0\leq s<t,~a\in\mathcal{A}\big)
of
the multivariate process
A=(U,Z)
as follows:

(4.4)

\displaystyle L^{(i,x)}_{[s,t)}(A)=\sum_{k\geq 1:~U(\tau_{k-1})=i}L_{[\tau_{k-1},\tau_{k})\cap[s,t)}^{x}(Z);

\int_{s}^{t}f\big(A(u)\big)~\mathrm{d}u=\sum_{k\geq 1}\int_{[\tau_{k-1},\tau_{k})\cap[s,t)}f\big(U(\tau_{k-1}),Z(u)\big)~\mathrm{d}u=\int_{\mathcal{A}}L^{a}_{[s,t)}(A)f(a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a).

In particular, for every
0\leq s<t
and
x\in I
, one has

(4.5)

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{r}L^{(i,x)}_{[s,t)}(A)=L^{x}_{[s,t)}(Z).

Finally, let
t>0
, let
n\geq 2
be an even integer, and let
q\in\mathcal{P}_{n}
.
Given a realization of
Z
, we let
\mathrm{si}_{t,n,q,Z}
denote
the unique random Borel probability measure on
[0,t)^{n}
such that for every

s_{1},t_{1},\ldots,s_{n},t_{n}\in[0,t)
with
s_{k}<t_{k}
for
k=1,\ldots,n
, one has

(4.6)

\mathrm{si}_{t,n,q,Z}\big([s_{1},t_{1})\times[s_{2},t_{2})\times\cdots\times[s_{n},t_{n})\big)\\
=\frac{1}{\|L_{t}(Z)\|_{2}^{2n}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q}\left\langle L_{[s_{\ell_{1}},t_{\ell_{1}})}(Z),L_{[s_{\ell_{2}},t_{\ell_{2}})}(Z)\right\rangle

(this uniquely determines the measure since product rectangles are a determining class
for the Borel
\sigma
-algebra).

Remark 4.5
.

Informally, one can think of
\mathrm{si}_{t,n,q,Z}
as the uniform probability measure
on the set of
n
-tuples of time coordinates in
[0,t)^{n}
such that each pair of times matched
by
q
coincide with a self-intersection of
Z
; namely:

\big\{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in[0,t)^{n}:Z(t_{\ell_{1}})=Z(t_{\ell_{2}})\text{ for all }\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q\big\}.

(We point to
[
7
, Theorem 2.3.2 and Proposition 2.3.5]
for a proof that

\mathrm{si}_{t,n,q,Z}
is supported on that set.)
Recall that, conditional on
N(t)
, the jump times
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{N(t)}

are the order statistics of i.i.d. uniform random variables in
[0,t)
. Thus,

\mathrm{si}_{t,n,q,Z}
serves as a natural means of formalizing the conditioning on the event (
1.27
)
(assuming
q
and the matching
p
in (
1.27
) are related to each other
in such a way that
p
matches the same pairs of times
t_{k}
as
q
once they are arranged in nondecreasing order).

Finally, given that products of Feynman-Kac kernels involve multiple independent copies
of the processes
U
and
X
, we introduce some notation to help write such expressions
tidily:

Definition 4.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{X}
be a stochastic process with càdlàg sample paths.
Given
n
-tuples of times
\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})
and states
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, we let

(4.7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}^{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}=\mathfrak{X}^{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})}_{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})}

denote a càdlàg concatenation of independent paths

\big(\mathfrak{X}^{x_{1}}(s):s\in[0,t_{1})\big),\big(\mathfrak{X}^{x_{2}}(s):s\in[0,t_{2})\big),\ldots,\big(\mathfrak{X}^{x_{n}}(s):s\in[0,t_{n})\big)

on the time-interval
[0,t_{1}+\cdots+t_{k})
.
That is, to generate
\mathfrak{X}^{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)
,
we follow the steps:

(1)

firstly, we run a path of
\mathfrak{X}
started at
x_{1}

on the time interval
[0,t_{1})
;

(2)

secondly, we run an independent path of
\mathfrak{X}
started at
x_{2}

on the time interval
[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})
;

(3)

\cdots

(4)

lastly, we run an independent path of
\mathfrak{X}
started at
x_{n}

on the time interval
[t_{1}+\cdots+t_{n-1},t_{1}+\cdots+t_{n})
.

Given
n
-tuples of times
\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})

and states
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
,
\boldsymbol{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
, let

(4.8)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{X}^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}=\mathfrak{X}^{(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}),(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})}_{(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})}

be the càdlàg concatenation
\mathfrak{X}^{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
with the additional endpoint conditioning

\mathfrak{X}^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}=\left(\mathfrak{X}^{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}~\bigg|~\lim_{s\to(t_{1}+\cdots+t_{k})^{-}}\mathfrak{X}^{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)=y_{k}\text{ for all }1\leq k\leq n\right).

See Figure
2
in Appendix
A
for an illustration
of one such càdlàg concatenation in the case
\mathfrak{X}=A
.
Finally, we use the following shorthand for a product of transition kernels:

\Pi_{\mathfrak{X}}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y})=\prod_{k=1}^{n}\Pi_{\mathfrak{X}}(t_{k};x_{k},y_{k}).

4.2.
Trace Moment Formulas

We are now finally in a position to state our trace moment formulas
in the case where
\xi
is a white noise. First,
we provide a precise statement of how the conditioning on the singular event (
1.27
)
is achieved:

Notation 4.7
.

Let
t>0
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed.
Given
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{n})\in[0,t]^{n}
, let
(\overrightarrow{s_{1}},\ldots,\overrightarrow{s_{n}})

denote
\boldsymbol{s}
’s coordinates
in increasing order, i.e.,
\overrightarrow{s_{1}}\leq\overrightarrow{s_{2}}\leq\cdots\leq\overrightarrow{s_{n}}
.

Definition 4.8
.

Suppose that we are given a realization of
Z

on some time interval
[0,t)
. Given this, we generate the process
\widehat{U}
on
[0,t)
as follows:

\bullet

Given
Z
, sample
\widehat{N}(t)
according to the distribution

\mathbf{P}\big[\widehat{N}(t)=2m\big]=\left(\frac{(r-1)^{2}\|L_{t}(Z)\|^{2}_{2}}{2}\right)^{m}\frac{1}{m!}\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{(r-1)^{2}}{2}\|L_{t}(Z)\|_{2}^{2}},\qquad m=0,1,2,\ldots,

i.e.,
\widehat{N}(t)/2
is Poisson with parameter
\frac{(r-1)^{2}}{2}\|L_{t}(Z)\|_{2}^{2}
.

\bullet

Given
\widehat{N}(t)
, sample
\widehat{q}
uniformly at random in
\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{N}(t)}
.
