\bullet

Given
Z
,
\widehat{N}(t)
, and
\widehat{q}
,
draw
\boldsymbol{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{\widehat{N}(t)})\in[0,t]^{\widehat{N}(t)}

according to the self-intersection measure
\mathrm{si}_{t,\widehat{N}(t),\widehat{q},Z}
.
Then, using Notation
4.7
, we define
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}=(\widehat{\tau}_{1},\ldots,\widehat{\tau}_{\widehat{N}(t)})
to be such that

\widehat{\tau}_{k}=\overrightarrow{T_{k}}
for all
k\geq 1
.
In words,
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
is the tuple
\boldsymbol{T}
rearranged in nondecreasing order.

\bullet

Given
\widehat{N}(t)
,
\boldsymbol{T}
, and
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}
, we let
\widehat{\pi}
be the random permutation
of
\{1,\ldots,\widehat{N}(t)\}

such that
\widehat{\tau}_{\widehat{\pi}(\ell)}=T_{\ell}
for all
\ell\leq\widehat{N}(t)
, and we let
\widehat{p}\in\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{N}(t)}

be the random perfect matching such that
\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in\widehat{q}
if and only if

\{\widehat{\pi}(\ell_{1}),\widehat{\pi}(\ell_{2})\}\in\widehat{p}
. In words,
\widehat{p}
ensures that each pair of times
T_{k}

that were matched by
\widehat{q}
are still matched once arranged in nondecreasing order
\widehat{\tau}_{k}
.

\bullet

Sample the jumps
J_{k}
in the same way as for
U
, i.e., uniform random walk on
the complete graph (without self-edges) on
\{1,\ldots,r\}
.

\bullet

Combining all of the above, we then let

\big(\widehat{U}(s):1\leq s\leq t\big)
be the càdlàg
path with jump times
\widehat{\boldsymbol{\tau}}

and jumps
J
(in particular, if
\widehat{N}(t)=0
, then
\widehat{U}(t)
is constant).

See Figure
8
in Appendix
A
for an illustration
of
(\widehat{U},Z)

Our trace moment formulas are as follows:

Theorem 4.9
.

Let Assumptions
2.3
,

2.4
, and
2.8
hold,
assuming further that
\xi_{i,i}
are white noises in
\mathbb{R}
with variance
\sigma^{2}>0
,
and that
\xi_{i,j}
(
i\neq j
) are white noises in
\mathbb{F}
with variance
\upsilon^{2}>0
.
Define the functionals

\mathfrak{M}_{t}(\widehat{p},\widehat{U})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\widehat{N}(t)=0\\
\upsilon^{\widehat{N}(t)}\mathfrak{C}_{t}(\widehat{p},\widehat{U})&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}

and

(4.9)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{H}_{t}(\widehat{U},Z)=\frac{(r-1)^{2}}{2}\|L_{t}(Z)\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|L_{t}(\widehat{U},Z)\|_{\mu}^{2}-\int_{0}^{t}V\big(\widehat{U}(s),Z(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(\widehat{U},Z).

For every
\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in(0,\infty)^{n}
, one has

(4.10)

\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}}\big]\cdots\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{n}\widehat{H}}\big]\right]\\
=\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\mathbf{E}\left[\mathfrak{M}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(\widehat{p},\widehat{U}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{H}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(\widehat{U}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\mathbf{1}_{\{\forall k:~\widehat{U}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(t_{1}+\cdots+t_{k-1}+s)\to i_{k}\text{ as }s\to t_{k}^{-}\}}\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{x}),

where we denote
\boldsymbol{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})\in\{1,\ldots,r\}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})\in I^{n}
.

4.3.
Rigidity

We now state our result regarding the number rigidity of
\widehat{H}
. For this purpose,
we recall the general formal definition of point processes on the real line:

Definition 4.10
.

Let
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}
be a Borel set
and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{K}}
be the
\sigma
-algebra of all Borel subsets of
\mathcal{K}
.
Let
\mathcal{N}^{\#}_{\mathcal{K}}
denote the set
of integer-valued measures
\mathcal{M}
on
(\mathcal{K},\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{K}})
such that
\mathcal{M}(\overline{\mathcal{K}})<\infty
for every bounded set
\overline{\mathcal{K}}\in\mathcal{B}_{\mathcal{K}}
.
We equip
\mathcal{N}^{\#}_{\mathcal{K}}
with the topology generated by the maps

\mathcal{M}\mapsto\int f(x)~\mathrm{d}\mathcal{M}(x),\qquad f:\mathcal{K}\to\mathbb{R}\text{ is continuous and compactly supported,}

as well as the associated Borel
\sigma
-algebra.
We note that for every
\mathcal{M}\in\mathcal{N}^{\#}_{\mathcal{K}}
, there exists a sequence

(x_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\mathcal{K}
without accumulation point such that

\mathcal{M}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\delta_{x_{k}}
;
i.e.,
\mathcal{M}(\overline{\mathcal{K}})
counts the number of points in the sequence
x_{k}

that are within the set
\overline{\mathcal{K}}
. Finally,
a point process on
\mathcal{K}
is a random element
that takes values in
\mathcal{N}^{\#}_{\mathcal{K}}
.

In this paper, we are only interested in the eigenvalue point process of
\widehat{H}
:

Definition 4.11
.

Let Assumptions
2.3
,

2.4
, and
2.8
hold.
We define

\widehat{H}
’s eigenvalue point process as

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\delta_{\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}\in\mathcal{N}^{\#}_{\mathbb{R}}.

For every Borel set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}
, we denote the restriction

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}|_{\mathcal{K}^{c}}:=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{\{\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})\in\mathcal{K}^{c}\}}\,\delta_{\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}\in\mathcal{N}^{\#}_{\mathcal{K}^{c}}.

That is, the same random measure as
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
, but excluding the masses in
\mathcal{K}
.

The definition of number rigidity used in this paper is as follows:

Definition 4.12
.

We say that a Borel set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}
is bounded above if
there exists some
c\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\mathcal{K}\subset(-\infty,c]
.
(Note that by Proposition
2.14
,

\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
is such that, almost surely,
\mathcal{\widehat{M}}(\mathcal{K})<\infty
for every
Borel set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}
that is bounded above.)
We say that
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
is number rigid if
for every bounded above Borel set
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathbb{R}
, there exists
a deterministic measurable function
F_{\mathcal{K}}:\mathcal{N}^{\#}_{\mathcal{K}^{c}}\to\mathbb{R}
such that

(4.11)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{M}}(\mathcal{K})=F_{\mathcal{K}}\big(\widehat{\mathcal{M}}|_{\mathcal{K}^{c}}\big)\qquad\text{almost surely};

in words, the number of eigenvalues in
\mathcal{K}
is a deterministic function of the
configuration of eigenvalues outside
\mathcal{K}
.

Our result regarding number rigidity is as follows:

Theorem 4.13
.

Let Assumptions
2.3
,

2.4
, and
2.8
hold,
assuming further that each entry of
\xi
is a white noise.

\bullet

In Cases 1 and 2, if there exists
\kappa,\nu>0
such that
V(i,x)\geq\kappa|x|-\nu
for all
1\leq i\leq r
and
x\in I
,
then
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
is number rigid. In particular, the eigenvalue point process of any
multivariate SAO (as defined in Remark
2.10
) is number rigid.

\bullet

In Case 3,
\widehat{\mathcal{M}}
is number rigid with no further condition on
V
.

5.
Proof of Proposition
2.14
: Construction of Operators

Suppose that Assumptions
2.3
,
2.4
, and
2.8
hold.
In the argument that follows, we record (as remarks) a few properties of the operators
H
and
\widehat{H}
that are not
part of the statement of Proposition
2.14
, but which are nevertheless important
in the proofs of other results. Moreover, following-up on the Hilbert-space subtlety mentioned in Remark

2.2
, we deal with the real/complex and quaternion cases separately
throughout this section.

5.1.
The Cases
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}

Assume first that
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
.
We begin by constructing the deterministic operator
H
.
For this purpose, for each
1\leq i\leq r
, recall the definition of

\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
D(\mathcal{E}_{i})
in Definition
2.11
.
By
[
28
, Proposition 3.2]
, the operators
H_{i}
defined in (
2.7
)
are the unique self-adjoint operators with dense domains
D(H_{i})\subset D(\mathcal{E}_{i})\subset L^{2}(I,\mathbb{F})

such that
\langle f,H_{i}g\rangle=\mathcal{E}_{i}(f,g)
for all
f,g\in D(H_{i})

(in
[
28
]
, the result is stated for operators acting on real-valued functions,
but the latter can be trivially extended to
\mathbb{C}
or
\mathbb{F}
by linearity).
Moreover, each
H_{i}
has compact resolvent.

Remark 5.1
.

For
1\leq i\leq r
, let
\mathrm{FC}_{i}
denote the set of functions

\varphi\in C_{0}^{\infty}(I,\mathbb{F})
that satisfy the following:

(1)

\varphi
is supported inside
I
in Case 1, as well as Cases 2 and 3
with Dirichlet boundary conditions (i.e.,
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-\infty
in Case 2 and
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=\overline{\beta}_{i}=-\infty
in Case 3).

(2)

\varphi
is supported inside
I
’s closure in Cases 2 and 3 with Robin boundary conditions (i.e.,
\overline{\alpha}_{i},\overline{\beta}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
).

(3)

In Case 3,
\varphi
is supported in
[0,b)
if
\overline{\alpha}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
and
\overline{\beta}_{i}=-\infty
,
and
\varphi
is supported in
(0,b]
if
\overline{\alpha}_{i}=-\infty
and
\overline{\beta}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
.

In
[
28
, Proposition 3.2]
, it is also proved that
\mathrm{FC}_{i}\subset D(\mathcal{E}_{i})
is a form core for
\mathcal{E}_{i}
.

Remark 5.2
.

In
[
28
, Theorem 5.4 and Lemma 5.13]
, it is also proved that

\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-tH_{i}}\big]=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\lambda_{k}(H_{i})}<\infty.

With this in hand, we can now define the operator

(5.1)

\displaystyle Hf(i,x)=-\tfrac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(i,x)+V(i,x)f(i,x),\qquad(i,x)\in\mathcal{A}

as the direct sum
H=H_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus H_{r}
.
By
[
56
, Theorem XIII.85]
,
H
is self-adjoint
on the domain
D(H)=D(H_{1})\oplus\cdots\oplus D(H_{r})
, which is dense in
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
.
Recalling the definition of
\mathcal{E}
and
D(\mathcal{E})
at the end of Definition
2.11
,
this means that
D(H)\subset D(\mathcal{E})
and
\langle f,Hg\rangle_{\mu}=\mathcal{E}(f,g)
for all
f,g\in D(H)
.
Finally, we note that since each
H_{i}
has compact resolvent, the same is true for
H
:
Given that
H=H_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus H_{r}
,
the eigenvalue-eigenfunction pairs
\big(\lambda_{k}(H),\psi_{k}(H)\big)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
of
H
are of the form

(5.2)

\displaystyle\lambda_{k}(H)=\lambda_{\ell}(H_{i})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi_{k}(H)=0\oplus\cdots\oplus 0\oplus\psi_{\ell}(H_{i})\oplus 0\oplus\cdots\oplus 0,

for some
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
.

Remark 5.3
.

If we combine Remark
5.2
with (
5.2
), then
we get that

(5.3)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-tH}\big]=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\lambda_{k}(H)}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\lambda_{k}(H_{i})}<\infty.

With the construction of
H
completed, we are now ready to wrap up the proof of
Proposition
2.14
. By Proposition
2.12
,
almost surely,
\xi
is an infinitesimally form-bounded perturbation of
\mathcal{E}
.
Thus, Proposition
2.14
-(1) and -(2) follow directly from the
KLMN theorem (e.g.,
[
58
, Theorem VIII.15]
and
[
57
, Theorem X.17]
), and the fact that
\widehat{H}
has compact
resolvent follows from the fact that
H
has compact resolvent and standard variational estimates
(e.g.,
[
56
, Theorem XIII.68]
).

Remark 5.4
.

Another conclusion of the KLMN theorem is that any form core for
\mathcal{E}

is also a form core for
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}
(e.g.,
[
60
, Theorem 7.5.7]
). Thus,
following-up on Remark
5.1
,
\mathrm{FC}=\mathrm{FC}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathrm{FC}_{r}

is a form core for both
\mathcal{E}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{E}}
(the latter almost surely).

5.2.
The Case
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}

Consider now the case
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}
. The results in

[
56
,
57
,
58
,
60
]
that were cited in
Section
5.1
require that

H_{i}
,
H
, and
\widehat{H}
be defined on a standard real or complex
Hilbert space. For this purpose, we first assume
that
L^{2}(I,\mathbb{H})
and
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{H})
are equipped with the
real inner products (
2.1
),
and that the bilinear forms for
H_{i}
and
H
are replaced by

\mathcal{Q}_{i}(f,g)=\Re\big(\mathcal{E}_{i}(f,g)\big)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathcal{Q}(f,g)=\Re\big(\mathcal{E}(f,g)\big);

the quadratic form

\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(f,f)=\mathcal{Q}(f,f)+\Re\big(\xi(f,f)\big)=\mathcal{E}(f,f)+\xi(f,f)

does not need to be changed, as
\mathcal{E}(f,f)
and
\xi(f,f)
are always real.
The argument in Section
5.1
then yields the conclusion of Proposition
2.14
,
the only difference being that, in the variational spectrum

(5.4)

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\ell}(\widehat{H})=\inf_{f\in D(\mathcal{E}),~f\perp\Psi_{1}(\widehat{H}),\ldots,\Psi_{\ell-1}(\widehat{H})}\frac{\widehat{\mathcal{E}}(f,f)}{\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}},\qquad\ell=1,2,3,\ldots,

the eigenfunctions
\Psi_{\ell}(\widehat{H})
form an orthonormal basis with respect to the standard
inner product
\prec\cdot,\cdot\succ_{\mu}
instead of
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mu}
,
and we thus have

(5.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}f=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\Lambda_{\ell}(\widehat{H})}\prec\Psi_{\ell}(\widehat{H}),f\succ_{\mu}\Psi_{\ell}(\widehat{H}).

In order to recover the actual statement of Proposition
2.14

from this, we first note that each eigenvalue
\Lambda\in\big(\Lambda_{\ell}(\widehat{H})\big)_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
defined via (
5.4
) has a multiplicity that is a multiple of
4
. This is because the corresponding eigenfunction
\Psi

is part of a four-dimensional subspace spanned by the orthonormal (with respect to
\prec\cdot,\cdot\succ_{\mu}
; see (
5.6
)) basis
\{\Psi,\Psi\mathsf{i},\Psi\mathsf{j},\Psi\mathsf{k}\}
(however, these basis elements
are not orthonormal with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mu}
). Up to reordering the
eigenfunctions
\big(\Psi_{k}(\widehat{H})\big)_{k\geq 1}
for repeated eigenvalues if necessary, we can assume that
for every
k\geq 1
, one has

\displaystyle\Psi_{4(k-1)+2}(\widehat{H})=\Psi_{4(k-1)+1}(\widehat{H})\mathsf{i},

\displaystyle\Psi_{4(k-1)+3}(\widehat{H})=\Psi_{4(k-1)+1}(\widehat{H})\mathsf{j},

\displaystyle\Psi_{4(k-1)+4}(\widehat{H})=\Psi_{4(k-1)+1}(\widehat{H})\mathsf{k}.

That is, the eigenfunctions are chosen in increments of four consecutive
eigenfunctions within the same space of the form
\Psi,\Psi\mathsf{i},\Psi\mathsf{j},\Psi\mathsf{k}
,
in that particular order.

With this in hand,
we claim that the statement of Proposition
2.14

holds with the eigenvalues and eigenfunctions

\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})=\Lambda_{4(k-1)+1}(\widehat{H})\text{ and }\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})=\Psi_{4(k-1)+1}(\widehat{H})\qquad k=1,2,3,\ldots.

To see this, notice that for any
f,g\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
, we can write

\langle f,g\rangle_{\mu}=\prec f,g\succ_{\mu}+\prec f\mathsf{i},g\succ_{\mu}\mathsf{i}+\prec f\mathsf{j},g\succ_{\mu}\mathsf{j}+\prec f\mathsf{k},g\succ_{\mu}\mathsf{k}.

Indeed, for any
\ell\in\{1,\mathsf{i},\mathsf{j},\mathsf{k}\}
, one has

(5.6)

\displaystyle\prec f\ell,g\succ_{\mu}=\Re\big(\langle f\ell,g\rangle_{\mu}\big)=\Re\big(\ell^{*}\langle f\mathsf{,}g\rangle_{\mu}\big),

and by the properties of quaternion multiplication, the real part of

\ell^{*}\langle f\mathsf{,}g\rangle_{\mu}
coincides with the scalar multiplying
\ell

in
\langle f\mathsf{,}g\rangle_{\mu}
. Given how we have ordered the
\Psi_{\ell}(\widehat{H})

in the previous paragraph, this readily implies that the
\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})
are an orthonormal
basis with respect to
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mu}
.
Moreover, rewriting (
5.5
)
using
\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})
and
\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})
yields

\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}f=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}\left(\sum_{\ell\in\{1,\mathsf{i},\mathsf{j},\mathsf{k}\}}\prec\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})\ell,f\succ_{\mu}\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})\ell\right)\\
=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})\left(\sum_{\ell\in\{1,\mathsf{i},\mathsf{j},\mathsf{k}\}}\prec\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})\ell,f\succ_{\mu}\ell\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}\psi_{k}(\widehat{H})\big\langle\psi_{k}(\widehat{H}),f\big\rangle_{\mu}

as desired.

Remark 5.5
.

Following-up on Remark
2.2
, we see from
the analysis in this section that
the choice of inner product influences the trace of
\widehat{H}
’s semigroup as follows:

(5.7)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\Lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}=4\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}=4\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}\big].

5.3.
Proposition
2.12

At this point, the only part of Proposition
2.14
that remains to
be proved is the technical estimate in Proposition
2.12
.
Given that the ideas used to prove the latter are also needed in several other results in the paper,
we postpone the proof of Proposition
2.12
to Section
7.7

below to make for a more streamlined treatment.

6.
Proof of Theorem
3.5
: Regular Feynman-Kac Formula

Throughout Section
6
, we assume without further mention that
Assumptions
2.3
,
2.4
, and
2.8
hold,
and moreover that each entry
\xi_{i,j}
is a regular noise.
This section is organized as follows: In Section
6.1
,
we provide an overview of the proof of Theorem
3.5
, wherein we state a number of
technical results without proof. Then, we provide the proofs of the technical
results in question in Sections
6.2
,

6.3
, and
6.4
.

6.1.
Proof Outline

Recall the definition of
\widehat{K}(t)
in (
3.5
).
The proof of Theorem
3.5
relies on the following two
technical results:

Proposition 6.1
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.5
,
almost surely,

\big(\widehat{K}(t)\big)_{t\geq 0}
is a strongly-continuous trace-class symmetric
semigroup on
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
; that is:

(1)

\widehat{K}(t;a,b)=\widehat{K}(t;b,a)^{*}
for every
t>0
and
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
.

(2)

For every
s,t>0
, and
a,b\in\mathcal{A}
,

\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}(s;a,c)\widehat{K}(t;c,b)~\mathrm{d}\mu(c)=\widehat{K}(s+t;a,b).

(3)

Letting
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
denote the Hilbert-Schmidt norm, for every
t>0
,

\|\widehat{K}(t)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}|\widehat{K}(t;a,b)|^{2}~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\mathrm{d}\mu(b)=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}(2t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)<\infty.

(4)

For every
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
, one has
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\|\widehat{K}(t)f-f\|_{2}=0.

Proposition 6.2
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
3.5
,
almost surely,

(6.1)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\left\|t^{-1}\big(f-\widehat{K}(t)f\big)-\widehat{H}f\right\|_{2}=0\qquad\text{for every $f\in D(\widehat{H})$}.

Before discussing these propositions, we use them to prove Theorem
3.5
:

6.1.1.
Cases
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}

Suppose first that
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
.
Consider an outcome in the probability-one event
where the conclusions of Propositions
6.1

and
6.2
hold.
By Proposition
6.1
-(4),
there exists
a closed operator
G_{K}
(called
\widehat{K}(t)
’s generator) with dense domain

(6.2)

\displaystyle D(G_{K})=\left\{f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F}):\lim_{t\to 0}t^{-1}\big(f(a)-\widehat{K}(t)f(a)\big)\text{ exists in }L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})\right\}

such that

(6.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\big\|t^{-1}\big(f-\widehat{K}(t)f\big)-G_{K}f\big\|_{2}=0\qquad\text{for every }f\in D(G_{K})

(e.g.,
[
22
, Chapter II, Theorem 1.4]
). In other words,
\mathrm{e}^{-tG_{K}}=\widehat{K}(t)
.
Our goal is to prove that
G_{K}=\widehat{H}
.
This can be done with Proposition
6.2
:
First, we note that Proposition
6.1
-(1) implies that
G_{K}

is symmetric.
Then, given (
6.2
)
and (
6.3
), the limit (
6.1
) implies that

D(\widehat{H})\subset D(G_{K})
and that
G_{K}
restricted to
D(\widehat{H})
is equal to
\widehat{H}
.
Since
\widehat{H}
is self-adjoint and
G_{K}
is symmetric,
D(G_{K})\subset D(G_{K}^{*})\subset D(\widehat{H}^{*})=D(\widehat{H})
;
hence
\widehat{H}=G_{K}
. This proves that
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}=\widehat{K}(t)
.

It now remains to prove the trace formula

\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}\big]=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)<\infty
.
For this purpose,
we note that

\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}\big]=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\big(\mathrm{e}^{-(t/2)\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}\big)^{2}=\|\mathrm{e}^{-(t/2)\widehat{H}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\|\widehat{K}(t/2)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2},

where the last equality follows from
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}=\widehat{K}(t)
.
Then,
Proposition
6.1
-(3) gives

\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}\big]=\|\widehat{K}(t/2)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}|K_{t/2}(a,b)|^{2}~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\mathrm{d}\mu(b)=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a),

as desired.

6.1.2.
The Case
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}

In similar fashion to the construction of
\widehat{H}
,
several elements of the proof in the previous section require
the operators under consideration to be self-adjoint on a standard
Hilbert space on
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
. The same argument can
apply to the case where
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}
, provided we reframe

\widehat{K}(t)
as an integral operator with respect to
\prec\cdot,\cdot\succ_{\mu}
, which acts on functions taking values in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
.
For this purpose, let us expand the components of
\widehat{K}(t)
and any
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{H})
in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
as follows:

\widehat{K}(t;a,b)=\widehat{K}_{1}(t;a,b)+\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{i}}(t;a,b)\mathsf{i}+\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{j}}(t;a,b)\mathsf{j}+\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{k}}(t;a,b)\mathsf{k},\qquad a,b\in\mathcal{A}

f(a)=f_{1}(a)+f_{2}(a)\mathsf{i}+f_{3}(a)\mathsf{j}+f_{4}(a)\mathsf{k},\qquad a\in\mathcal{A}

where
f_{\ell}
and
\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{l}}(t)
are real-valued for all
\ell\in\{1,2,3,4\}
and
\mathsf{l}\in\{1,\mathsf{i},\mathsf{j},\mathsf{k}\}
.
Consider the
4\times 4

\mathbb{R}
-matrix-valued kernel
J(t)
defined as follows:

J(t;a,b)=\left[\begin{array}[]{cccc}\widehat{K}_{1}(t;a,b)&-\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{i}}(t;a,b)&-\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{j}}(t;a,b)&-\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{k}}(t;a,b)\\
\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{i}}(t;a,b)&\widehat{K}_{1}(t;a,b)&-\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{k}}(t;a,b)&\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{j}}(t;a,b)\\
\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{j}}(t;a,b)&\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{k}}(t;a,b)&\widehat{K}_{1}(t;a,b)&-\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{i}}(t;a,b)\\
\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{k}}(t;a,b)&-\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{j}}(t;a,b)&\widehat{K}_{\mathsf{i}}(t;a,b)&\widehat{K}_{1}(t;a,b)\end{array}\right].

If we denote the entries of
g(a)=\widehat{K}(t)f(a)=\int\widehat{K}(t;a,b)f(b)~\mathrm{d}\mu(b)

in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
as
g_{\ell}(a)
, i.e.,

g(a)=g_{1}(a)+g_{2}(a)\mathsf{i}+g_{3}(a)\mathsf{j}+g_{4}(a)\mathsf{k},\qquad a\in\mathcal{A},

then by definition of quaternion product, we have that

g(a)_{\ell}=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\sum_{m=1}^{4}J(t;a,b)_{\ell,m}f(b)_{m}~\mathrm{d}\mu(b)=\prec J(t;a,\cdot)_{\ell,\cdot},f\succ_{\mu}

for
1\leq\ell\leq 4
, where we view
J(t;a,b)_{\ell,\cdot}
as the
quaternion/vector in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
whose components are given by the
\ell^{\mathrm{th}}
row of the
matrix
J(t;a,b)
.

With this in hand, we can now apply the same argument in Section

6.1.1
to the
4\times 4
matrix kernel
J(t;a,b)_{\ell,m}
,
which yields that
J(t)
coincides with the operator
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}
defined
via (
5.5
),
as well as the trace formula

(6.4)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\Lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})}=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{Tr}\big[J(t;a,a)\big]~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)

(note that
\widehat{K}(t;a,b)=\widehat{K}(t;b,a)^{*}
means that
J(t;a,b)_{\ell,m}=J(t;b,a)_{m,\ell}
).
If we pull this back to
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{H})
with
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mu}
,
this implies that
\widehat{K}(t)=\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}}
, with the semigroup now defined via (
2.11
).
The trace formula can then be obtained by combining (
5.7
) and (
6.4
) with
the equalities

(6.5)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathrm{Tr}\big[J(t;a,a)\big]~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)=4\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}_{1}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)=4\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a),

where the last equality comes from
\widehat{K}(t;a,a)=\widehat{K}(t;a,a)^{*}
, i.e., Proposition
6.1
-(1).

6.1.3.
Propositions
6.1

and
6.2

At this point, the only elements in the proof of Theorem
3.5

that remain to be established are Propositions
6.1

and
6.2
.
The nontrivial elements of the proof of Proposition
6.1
are mostly technical
and can be found in Section

6.3
.
The proof of Proposition
6.2
,
which we provide in full in Section
6.4
, has the following structure:
Let
f\in D(\widehat{H})
.
For any
a\in\mathcal{A}
, we can write

t^{-1}\big(f(a)-\widehat{K}(t)f(a)\big)=\mathfrak{G}_{0}(t;a)+\mathfrak{G}_{1}(t;a)+\mathfrak{G}_{2}(t;a),

where we define the
\mathfrak{G}_{k}
by adding an indicator fixing the value of
N(t)
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{0}(t;a)

\displaystyle=t^{-1}\left(f(a)-\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{N(t)=0\}}\right]\right),

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{1}(t;a)

\displaystyle=t^{-1}\left(-\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{N(t)=1\}}\right]\right),

(6.6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{2}(t;a)

\displaystyle=t^{-1}\left(-\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{N(t)\geq 2\}}\right]\right).

With this in hand, the proof of Proposition
6.2
is split into three steps:

(6.7)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\|\mathfrak{G}_{0}(t;\cdot)-(H+\mathrm{diag}(\xi))f\|_{2}=0,

(6.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\|\mathfrak{G}_{1}(t;\cdot)-Qf\|_{2}=0,

(6.9)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\|\mathfrak{G}_{2}(t;\cdot)\|_{2}=0,

where we recall that
H=-\frac{1}{2}\Delta+V

is defined in (
5.1
),

\mathrm{diag}(\xi)
denotes the direct sum
\xi_{1,1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\xi_{r,r}
,
and
Qf
is defined as

Qf(i,x)=\sum_{j\neq i}\xi_{i,j}(x)f(j,x)=\sum_{j\neq i}\xi_{i,j}(x)f(j,x).

The rationale behind these three steps can be explained by the following remarks:

Remark 6.3
.

Regarding (
6.7
), write

a=(i,x)
. If
N(t)=0
, then the process
U^{i}
is equal to
i

in the time interval
[0,t]
. On this event, we notice that

\displaystyle\bullet f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)=f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big);

\displaystyle\bullet\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)t};

\displaystyle\bullet\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})=\mathfrak{B}^{i}_{t}(Z^{x});

\displaystyle\bullet\int_{0}^{t}S\big(A^{a}(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}S\big(i,Z^{x}(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s;

where we define

\mathfrak{B}^{i}_{t}(Z^{x})=\begin{cases}0&\text{Case 1,}\\
\displaystyle\overline{\alpha}_{i}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{t}(X^{x})&\text{Case 2,}\\
\displaystyle\overline{\alpha}_{i}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{t}(Y^{x})+\overline{\beta}_{i}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{t}(Y^{x})&\text{Case 3.}\end{cases}

Therefore, we can write

\mathfrak{G}_{0}\big(t;(i,x)\big)=t^{-1}\left(f(i,x)-\mathbf{E}_{Z}\left[\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)t}\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(i,Z^{x}(s))~\mathrm{d}s-\mathfrak{B}^{i}_{t}(Z^{x})}f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\right]\mathbf{P}[N(t)=0]\right)\\
=t^{-1}\left(f(i,x)-\mathbf{E}_{Z}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(i,Z^{x}(s))~\mathrm{d}s-\mathfrak{B}^{i}_{t}(Z^{x})}f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\right]\right),

where the last equality follows from the fact that
N(t)
is Poisson with rate
(r-1)t
.
Recalling the definition of the one-dimensional operators
H_{i}
in
(
2.7
),
as well as the Feynman-Kac formula for
H_{i}+\xi_{i,i}
in (
3.7
),
this can be further simplified to

\mathfrak{G}_{0}\big(t;(i,x)\big)=t^{-1}\big(f(i,x)-\mathrm{e}^{-t(H_{i}+\xi_{i,i})}f(i,x)\big).

In addition to that, given that
H+\mathrm{diag}(\xi)=(H_{1}+\xi_{1,1})\oplus\cdots\oplus(H_{r}+\xi_{r,r})
, we note that
for every
1\leq i\leq r
,

\big(H+\mathrm{diag}(\xi)\big)f(i,x)=\big(H_{i}+\xi_{i,i}\big)f(i,x).

Therefore,

(6.10)

\|\mathfrak{G}_{0}(t;\cdot)-(H+\mathrm{diag}(\xi))f\|_{2}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{I}\left|\mathfrak{G}_{0}\big(t;(i,x)\big)-(H+\mathrm{diag}(\xi))f(i,x)\right|^{2}~\mathrm{d}x\\
=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{I}\left|t^{-1}\big(f(i,x)-\mathrm{e}^{-t(H_{i}+\xi_{i,i})}f(i,x)\big)-(H_{i}+\xi_{i,i})f(i,x)\right|^{2}~\mathrm{d}x.

The right-hand side of the above converges to zero thanks to the
one-dimensional Feynman-Kac formula (
3.7
);
see Section
6.4.1
for detailed references.

Remark 6.4
.

Regarding (
6.8
),
denote the events

\mathcal{J}_{j}(t)=\{N(t)=1\}\cap\{U(\tau_{1})=j\},\qquad t>0,~1\leq j\leq r.

With this in hand, we write
\mathbf{1}_{\{N(t)=1\}}=\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{J}_{1}(t)}+\cdots+\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{J}_{r}(t)}
,
noting that the
\mathcal{J}_{j}(t)
are disjoint and
N(t)
and
U(\tau_{1})
are independent.
On the event
\mathcal{J}_{j}(t)
, we
have the simplification

\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{(i,x)})=-\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)t}\xi_{i,j}\big(Z^{x}(\tau_{1})\big).

Therefore, we can write

\mathfrak{G}_{2}\big(t;(i,x)\big)=\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)t}}{t}\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\xi_{i,j}\big(Z^{x}(\tau_{1})\big)\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{(i,x)}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{(i,x)})}f\big(j,Z^{x}(t)\big)\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{J}_{j}(t)}\right].

Moreover, given that

\mathbf{P}[\mathcal{J}_{j}(t)]=\mathbf{P}[N(t)=1]\cdot\mathbf{P}[U(\tau_{1})=j]=(r-1)t\mathrm{e}^{-(r-1)t}\cdot\frac{1}{r-1}=\left(\frac{\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)t}}{t}\right)^{-1},

we can further simplify this expression to

(6.11)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{G}_{1}\big(t;(i,x)\big)=\sum_{j\neq i}\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\xi_{i,j}\big(Z(\tau_{1})\big)\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{(i,x)}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{(i,x)})}f\big(j,Z^{x}(t)\big)~\bigg|~\mathcal{J}_{j}(t)\right].

By continuity of
\xi_{i,j}
and
Z
and
0\leq\tau_{1}\leq t
, we expect that (
6.11
) should converge to

\sum_{j\neq i}\xi_{i,j}\big(Z^{x}(0)\big)f\big(j,Z^{x}(0)\big)=\sum_{j\neq i}\xi_{i,j}(x)f(j,x)=Qf(i,x)

as
t\to 0
;
see Section
6.4.2
for the technical details.

Remark 6.5
.

The proof of (
6.9
)
relies on the fact that
\mathbf{P}[N(t)\geq 2]=O(t^{2})
as
t\to 0
,
which makes
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
vanish despite the
t^{-1}
term
in (
6.6
).
See Section
6.4.3
for details.

6.2.
Growth Estimates of
S
and
Q

We begin with a preliminary estimate on the growth
of
S
and
Q
:

Lemma 6.6
.

Let
\mathfrak{a}>0
be as in Assumption
2.8
,
and let
\mathfrak{b}>1/2
be arbitrary.
The following two conditions hold almost surely:

(1)

For all
1\leq i\leq r
,
S(i,\cdot)
is bounded below, locally integrable on
I
’s closure, and
in Cases 1 and 2,

(6.12)

\displaystyle\lim_{|x|\to\infty}\frac{S(i,x)}{|x|^{\mathfrak{a}}}=\infty.

(2)

In Cases 1 and 2, for all
1\leq i<j\leq r
,

(6.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{|x|\to\infty}\frac{|\xi_{i,j}(x)|}{(\log|x|)^{\mathfrak{b}}}=0.

In Case 3, we need only prove that
S(i,x)=V(i,x)+\xi_{i,i}(x)
are bounded below and locally integrable.
This follows from the fact that
V
already satisfies these conditions (Assumption
2.8
),
and that
\xi_{i,i}
are continuous real-valued functions on a bounded interval.

Consider then Cases 1 and 2. First, we note that by a Borel-Cantelli argument
(e.g.,
[
28
, Corollary B.2]
),
the fact that
\xi_{i,i}
and
\xi_{i,j}=\xi_{j,i}^{*}
are continuous and stationary
implies that there exists a finite random
\nu>0
such that, almost surely,

|\xi_{i,i}(x)|,|\xi_{i,j}(x)|\leq\nu\sqrt{\log(2+|x|)},\qquad x\in I,\qquad 1\leq i\neq j\leq r.

This immediately implies (
6.13
) since
\mathfrak{b}>1/2
.
To prove that

S(i,\cdot)=V(i,\cdot)+\xi_{i,i}
are bounded below and locally integrable and
(
6.12
),
we simply notice that
V(i,\cdot)
is already assumed to grow faster than
|x|^{\mathfrak{a}}
by Assumption
2.8

(which itself grows faster than
\sqrt{\log|x|}
),
and that
\xi_{i,i}
is continuous.
∎

6.3.
Proof of Proposition
6.1

We split the proof into four steps, corresponding to Proposition
6.1
-(1) to -(4).
Throughout the proof of Proposition
6.1
,
we assume without further mention that we are working on the probability-one event (with respect to
the randomness in
\xi
) where the conclusions of Lemma
6.6
hold.

6.3.1.
Proof of Proposition
6.1
-(1)

The symmetry property follows from applying a time reversal to
the path
A
, in which case a bridge from
a
to
b
becomes a bridge from

b
to
a
: On the one hand, the boundary local time term
\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A)
and the area
\int_{0}^{t}S\big(A(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s

are invariant with respect to time reversal, hence these terms remain the same under
this operation. On the other hand, since a time reversal inverts the order of the jump times
\tau_{k}

and the individual jumps
J_{k}
,
under this operation the product term
\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)
is simply conjugated (since
Q
is
self-adjoint).

6.3.2.
Proof of Proposition
6.1
-(2)

Given
a,b,c\in\mathcal{A}
, if we condition the process
A^{a,b}_{s+t}
on
the event
\{A^{a,b}_{s+t}(s)=c\}
,
then the path segments

\big(A^{a,b}_{s+t}(u):0\leq u\leq s\big)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\big(A^{a,b}_{s+t}(u):s\leq u\leq s+t\big)

are independent and have respective distributions
A^{a,c}_{s}
and
A^{c,b}_{t}
.
Next, recalling that
A^{(a,c),(c,b)}_{(s,t)}
denotes the path on the time interval
[0,s+t]
obtained by concatenating
the independent paths of
A^{a,c}_{s}
and
A^{c,b}_{t}
, we notice that

\int_{0}^{s}S\big(A^{a,c}_{s}(u)\big)~\mathrm{d}u+\int_{0}^{t}S\big(A^{c,b}_{t}(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}u=\int_{0}^{s+t}S\big(A^{(a,c),(c,b)}_{(s,t)}(u)\big)~\mathrm{d}u

and

\mathfrak{B}_{s}(A^{a,c}_{s})+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{c,b}_{t})=\mathfrak{B}_{s+t}(A^{(a,c),(c,b)}_{(s,t)}),

as well as

\mathfrak{n}_{s}(Q,A^{a,c}_{s})\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{c,b}_{t})=\mathfrak{n}_{s+t}(Q,A^{(a,c),(c,b)}_{(s,t)}).

With this, Proposition
6.1
-(2) is an immediate consequence of the
law of total expectation, i.e., for any functional
F
, one has

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\Pi_{A}(s;a,c)\Pi_{A}(t;c,b)\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[F\left(A^{(a,c),(c,b)}_{(s,t)}\right)\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu(c)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{A}(s+t;a,b)\int_{\mathcal{A}}\frac{\Pi_{A}(s;a,c)\Pi_{A}(t;c,b)}{\Pi_{A}(s+t;a,b)}\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[F\left(A^{a,b}_{s+t}\right)\Big|A^{a,b}_{s+t}(s)=c\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu(c)

\displaystyle=\Pi_{A}(s+t;a,b)\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[F\big(A^{a,b}_{s+t}\big)\Big].

6.3.3.
Proof of Proposition
6.1
-(3)

We first prove that
\|\widehat{K}(t)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}<\infty
.
We can write

\|\widehat{K}(t)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{A}(t;a,b)^{2}\left|\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a,b}_{t})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a,b}_{t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a,b}_{t})}\right]\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\mathrm{d}\mu(b).

By Jensen’s inequality,

(6.14)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{K}(t)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}\leq\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{A}(t;a,b)^{2}\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a,b}_{t})|\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a,b}_{t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a,b}_{t})}\right]^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\mathrm{d}\mu(b).

By arguing in the same way as in part (1) of this
lemma, but replacing
\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)
by its modulus, we
note that

\Pi_{A}(t;a,b)\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a,b}_{t})|\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a,b}_{t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a,b}_{t})}\right]\\
=\Pi_{A}(t;b,a)\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{b,a}_{t})|\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{b,a}_{t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{b,a}_{t})}\right].

If we combine this with a use of Tonelli’s theorem in the
\mathrm{d}\mu(b)
integral in (
6.14
),
the same argument as in part (2) of this lemma then implies that

(6.15)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{K}(t)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}\leq\int_{\mathcal{A}}\Pi_{A}(2t;a,a)\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{2t}(Q,A^{a,a}_{2t})|\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{2t}S(A^{a,a}_{2t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{2t}(A^{a,a}_{2t})}\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu(a).

In order to control the terms inside the expectation in (
6.15
),
we use the following estimates, which we will use several more times in this section:

Proposition 6.7
.

In Cases 1 or 2
(i.e.,
Z=B,X
),
we let
W^{0}
be a standard Brownian motion started at zero coupled
with
Z
in such a way that

(6.16)

\displaystyle Z(t)=\begin{cases}B(0)+W^{0}(t)&\text{Case 1,}\\
|X(0)+W^{0}(t)|&\text{Case 2.}\end{cases}

For any
t>0
and
p\geq 1
, there exists
constants
\kappa,\nu>0
and
0<\overline{\mathfrak{a}}\leq 1
such that

(6.17)

\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)|^{p}\mathrm{e}^{-p\int_{0}^{t}S(A(s))~\mathrm{d}s+p\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A)}\right]\\
\leq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\exp\left(-\kappa t|B(0)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}+\kappa t\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|W^{0}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}+\nu t\right)&\text{Case 1,}\\
\displaystyle\exp\left(-\kappa t|X(0)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}+\kappa t\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|W^{0}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}+\nu t+p\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{t}(X)\right)&\text{Case 2,}\\
\displaystyle\exp\left(\nu t+p\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{t}(Y)+p\overline{\beta}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{t}(Y)\right)&\text{Case 3.}\end{cases}

where
\mathbf{E}_{N}[\cdot]
denotes the expectation with respect to
the unconditioned Poisson process
N=\big(N(t):t\geq 0\big)
only
(conditional on all other sources of randomness),
and

\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}=\max_{1\leq i\leq r}\overline{\alpha}_{i}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\overline{\beta}_{\mathrm{m}}=\max_{1\leq i\leq r}\overline{\beta}_{i}.

First, we note that in Cases 2 and 3, it follows from
(
3.1
),
(
3.2
),
and (
3.3
)
that

(6.18)

\displaystyle p\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A)\leq\begin{cases}p\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{t}(X)&\text{Case 2},\\
p\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{t}(Y)+p\overline{\beta}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{t}(Y)&\text{Case 3}.\end{cases}

Since the terms on the right-hand side of (
6.18
)
do not depend on
N
, they can be pulled out of the expectation
\mathbf{E}_{N}[\cdot]
;
this explains the presence of the local time terms on the right-hand side of (
6.17
)
in Cases 2 and 3.
Thus, it only remains to control the expression
\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)|^{p}\mathrm{e}^{-p\int_{0}^{t}S(A(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right]
.

Consider first Cases 1 and 2.
Let
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}=\min\{1,\mathfrak{a}\}
.
By (
6.12
), we get
that for every
\kappa>0
, there exists some
\nu_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\min_{1\leq i\leq r}S(i,x)\geq\tfrac{2\kappa}{p}|x|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}-\tfrac{\nu_{0}}{p}.

Thus,

(6.19)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)|^{p}\mathrm{e}^{-p\int_{0}^{t}S(A(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right]\leq\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)|^{p}\big]\mathrm{e}^{-2\kappa\int_{0}^{t}|Z(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}~\mathrm{d}s+\nu_{0}t},

where only the term
|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)|^{p}
remains in the expectation because
the other term no longer depends on
N
.
Next,
by (
6.13
),
we can find
\nu_{1}>0
such that

\max_{1\leq i<j\leq r}|\xi_{i,j}(x)|^{p}\leq\frac{\kappa}{r-1}|x|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}+\nu_{1}.

Denoting
\eta(x)=\frac{\kappa}{r-1}|x|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}+\nu_{1}
for simplicity, we then get

(6.20)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)|^{p}\big]\leq\mathrm{e}^{p(r-1)t}\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{N(t)}\eta\big(Z(\tau_{k})\big)\right].

Conditional on
N(t)=n
, the points
(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{n})

are the order statistics of
n
i.i.d. uniform points on
[0,t]
. Therefore,
since
\eta
is real-valued and commutes,
we can rewrite (
6.20
) as follows by conditioning
on
N(t)
:

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)|^{p}\big]

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{e}^{p(r-1)t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{N(t)}\eta\big(Z(\tau_{k})\big)\Bigg|N(t)=n\right]\mathbf{P}[N(t)=n]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{p(r-1)t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{t^{n}}\int_{[0,t]^{n}}\eta\big(Z(s_{1})\big)\cdots\eta\big(Z(s_{n})\big)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\right)\frac{(r-1)^{n}t^{n}}{n!}\mathrm{e}^{-(r-1)t}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{(p-1)(r-1)t}\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\frac{(r-1)^{n}}{n!}\left(\int_{0}^{t}\eta\big(Z(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s\right)^{n}

(6.21)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)\int_{0}^{t}\eta(Z(s))~\mathrm{d}s+(p-1)(r-1)t}=\mathrm{e}^{\kappa\int_{0}^{t}|Z(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}~\mathrm{d}s+(p-1+\nu_{1})(r-1)t}

Thus,
if we apply (
6.21
) to
(
6.19
), with
\nu=(p-1+\nu_{1})(r-1)+\nu_{0}
, we get

(6.22)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)|^{p}\mathrm{e}^{-p\int_{0}^{t}S(A(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right]\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\kappa\int_{0}^{t}|Z(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}~\mathrm{d}s+\nu t}.

By (
6.16
) and the reverse triangle inequality (since
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}\leq 1
),

(6.23)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}|Z(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}~\mathrm{d}s\geq\int_{0}^{t}|Z(0)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}-|W^{0}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}~\mathrm{d}s\geq t|Z(0)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}-t\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|W^{0}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}.

Together with (
6.22
),
this concludes the proof of (
6.17
)
in Cases 1 and 2.

It now only remains to show that there exists some
\nu>0

such that

(6.24)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)|^{p}\mathrm{e}^{-p\int_{0}^{t}S(A(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\right]\leq\mathrm{e}^{\nu t}

in Case 3. On the one hand, the fact that
S
is bounded below
(Lemma
6.6
)
means that
\mathrm{e}^{-p\int_{0}^{t}S(A(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\leq\mathrm{e}^{\nu_{1}t}

for some
\nu_{1}>0
. Next, since
Q
is continuous and
I

is bounded in Case 3, there exists some constant
\nu_{2}>0

such that

\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A)|^{p}\big]\leq\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{p(r-1)t}\nu_{2}^{pN(t)}\right]=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)(\nu_{2}^{p}+p-1)t}.

We then get (
6.24
)
by taking
\nu=\nu_{1}+(r-1)(\nu_{2}^{p}+p-1)
.
∎

We now return to the task at hand, which is to
prove that (
6.15
) is finite
using the bound in (
6.17
).
For this purpose, however, the conditioning on the process
A^{a,a}_{2t}

is problematic, since the endpoint
U(2t)

is not independent of
N
’s distribution. In order to get around this
we denote
a=(i,x)\in\mathcal{A}
, and note that for any nonnegative functional
F
,
we can write

\Pi_{A}(2t;a,a)\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[F\big(A^{a,a}_{2t}\big)\Big]=\Pi_{Z}(2t;x,x)\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[F\big(U^{i},Z^{x,x}_{2t}\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{U(2t)=i\}}\Big]\\
\leq\Pi_{Z}(2t;x,x)\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[\big[F\big(U^{i},Z^{x,x}_{2t}\big)\Big].

If we apply this to (
6.15
),
together with the inequality

(6.25)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in I}\Pi_{Z}(2t;x,x)<\infty

(e.g.,
[
28
, (5.16)]
),
then we get

(6.26)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{K}(t)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}\leq C\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\big|\mathfrak{n}_{2t}\big(Q,(U^{i},Z^{x,x}_{2t})\big)\big|\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{2t}S(U^{i}(s),Z^{x,x}_{2t}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{2t}(U^{i},Z^{x,x}_{2t})}\right]~\mathrm{d}x

for some finite
C>0
that depends only on
t
.

In Cases 1 and 2,
if we let
W^{0}
be defined as in the statement of Proposition

6.7
, then
under the conditioning
Z^{x,x}_{2t}
,
the process
W^{0}
has law
W^{0,0}_{2t}
; hence

Z^{x,x}_{2t}(s)=\begin{cases}x+W^{0,0}_{2t}(s)&\text{Case 1},\\
\left|x+W^{0,0}_{2t}(s)\right|&\text{Case 2},\end{cases}\qquad\text{for }0\leq s\leq t

Therefore,
if we apply Proposition
6.7
with
p=1

to the expectation in (
6.26
)
(more precisely, we first write
\mathbf{E}_{A}[\cdot]=\mathbf{E}_{A}\big[\mathbf{E}_{N}[\cdot]\big]

by the tower property—noting that
N
is independent of the event
\{U(0)=i\}
—and then notice that the upper bound
in Proposition
6.7

does not depend on the process
U
),
then for every
\kappa>0
, there is
\nu>0
such that

(6.27)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{K}(t)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}\leq\begin{cases}\displaystyle Cr\mathrm{e}^{2\nu t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{e}^{-2\kappa t|x|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{2\kappa t\sup_{0\leq s\leq 2t}|W^{0,0}_{2t}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}}\right]~\mathrm{d}x&\text{Case 1,}\\
\displaystyle Cr\mathrm{e}^{2\nu t}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-2\kappa t|x|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{2\kappa t\sup_{0\leq s\leq 2t}|W^{0,0}_{2t}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}+\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{2t}(X^{x,x}_{2t})}\right]~\mathrm{d}x&\text{Case 2,}\\
\displaystyle Cr\mathrm{e}^{2\nu t}\int_{0}^{\vartheta}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{2t}(Y^{x,x}_{2t})+\overline{\beta}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{2t}(Y^{x,x}_{2t})}\right]~\mathrm{d}x&\text{Case 3.}\end{cases}

It now only remains to prove that (
6.27
) is finite for all
t>0
.
By applying Hölder’s inequality to the expectation in (
6.27
),
the finiteness of
\|\widehat{K}(t)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
is a consequence of the following statements:

Lemma 6.8
.

For every
\kappa,t>0
and
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}>0
,

(6.28)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{e}^{-2\kappa t|x|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}}~\mathrm{d}x<\infty.

For every
t,\kappa>0
and
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}\leq 1
, one has

(6.29)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\kappa\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|W^{0}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}}\right]<\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\kappa\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|W^{0,0}_{t}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}}\right]<\infty.

In Cases 2 and 3,
for every
t,\nu>0
, one has

(6.30)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in I}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\nu\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{t}(Z^{x})}\right]<\infty\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sup_{x\in I}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\nu\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{t}(Z^{x,x}_{t})}\right]<\infty,

where
c=0
in Case 2 and
c\in\{0,\vartheta\}
in Case 3.

(
6.28
) is a trivial calculation.
Next, (
6.29
)
follows from the fact that Brownian motion/bridge maxima have
Gaussian tails; see the reflection principle for
W^{0}

and, e.g.,
[
37
, Remark 3.1]
for
W^{0,0}_{t}
.
Finally, (
6.30
)
is
[
28
, Lemmas 5.6 and 5.8]
.
∎

We therefore conclude that
\|\widehat{K}(t)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}<\infty
.
With this, the only claim in part (3) of this lemma that remains to be established is

(6.31)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}|\widehat{K}(t;a,b)|^{2}~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\mathrm{d}\mu(b)=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}(2t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a).

To this effect, property (1) of this lemma implies that

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}|\widehat{K}(t;a,b)|^{2}~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\mathrm{d}\mu(b)=\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\widehat{K}(t;b,a)\widehat{K}(t;a,b)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\mathrm{d}\mu(b).

At this point, we obtain (
6.31
) by part (2) of this lemma, so long as we
can apply Fubini’s theorem to integrate out
\mathrm{d}\mu(a)
in the above expression.
We can do this thanks to our knowledge that
\|\widehat{K}(t)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}<\infty
.

6.3.4.
Proof of Proposition
6.1
-(4) Part 1: Reduction to Smooth and Compactly Supported Functions

We now begin our proof of Proposition
6.1
-(4).
We first argue that it suffices to prove the result
under the assumption that every
f(i,\cdot)
is smooth and compactly supported
on
I
’s closure.
For this, it is enough to show that there exists a constant
C>0

such that

(6.32)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\|\widehat{K}(t)f\|_{2}\leq C\|f\|_{2}

for every
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
. Indeed, with this we can write

\|\widehat{K}(t)f-f\|_{2}\leq\|\widehat{K}(t)\tilde{f}-\tilde{f}\|_{2}+(C+1)\|f-\tilde{f}\|_{2}\qquad\text{for all }t\in(0,1),

where
\tilde{f}
is a smooth and compactly supported approximation of
f
.

We now establish (
6.32
). Write

\displaystyle\|\widehat{K}(t)f\|_{2}^{2}=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\left|\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)\right]\right|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(a).

Taking the absolute value inside the expectation by Jensen’s inequality, we get

(6.33)

\displaystyle\|\widehat{K}(t)f\|_{2}^{2}\leq\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})|\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}\big|f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)\big|\right]^{2}\mathrm{d}\mu(a).

We aim to apply Proposition
6.7
to
the expectation in (
6.33
). In this view, the
presence of
\big|f\big(A(t)\big)\big|
in (
6.33
)
is problematic, since again the enpoint
U(t)
depends on the point process
N
.
In order to get around this, we change the initial point
U(0)
of
the process
U
to its stationary measure: For any point
x\in I

and nonnegative functional
F
, we note that we can write

\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[F(A^{(i,x)})\,\big|f\big(A^{(i,x)}(t)\big)\big|\Big]\leq\sum_{j=1}^{r}\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[F(A^{(j,x)})\,\big|f\big(A^{(j,x)}(t)\big)\big|\Big]\\
=r\,\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[F(A^{(u,x)})\,\big|f\big(A^{(u,x)}(t)\big)\big|\Big],

where
u
is a uniform random variable on
\{1,\ldots,r\}

that is independent of
Z
,
N
, and the jumps
J_{k}
;
and
A^{(u,x)}
denotes
A=(U,Z)

conditioned on
\{Z(0)=x\}
and
\{U(0)=u\}
. Since the
uniform distribution is the
stationary measure of
U
, under the conditioning
\{U(0)=u\}
,
the endpoint
U(t)
is now independent of the Poisson process
N
.
Thus, we can now apply the tower property, whereby

(6.34)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[F(A^{(i,x)})\,\big|f\big(A^{(i,x)}(t)\big)\big|\Big]\leq r\,\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[F(A^{(u,x)})\big]\,\big|f\big(A^{(u,x)}(t)\big)\big|\Big].

Finally, in the context of this type of expectation sitting inside a
\mathrm{d}\mu(a)
integral
with a square,
(
6.34
) yields

(6.35)

\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[F(A^{a})\,\big|f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)\big|\Big]^{2}~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[F(A^{(i,x)})\,\big|f\big(A^{(i,x)}(t)\big)\big|\Big]^{2}~\mathrm{d}x\\
\leq r^{3}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{E}_{A}\Big[\mathbf{E}_{N}\big[F(A^{(u,x)})\big]\,\big|f\big(A^{(u,x)}(t)\big)\big|\Big]^{2}~\mathrm{d}x.

If we use (
6.35
) to apply
Proposition
6.7
(with
p=1
) to
(
6.33
), then we
get

(6.36)

\|\widehat{K}(t)f\|_{2}^{2}\leq r^{3}\mathrm{e}^{2\nu t}\\
\cdot\begin{cases}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathrm{e}^{-2\kappa t|x|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\kappa t\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\left|W^{0}(s)\right|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}}\big|f\big(u,B^{x}(t)\big)\big|\right]^{2}\mathrm{d}x&\text{Case 1},\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-2\kappa t|x|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\kappa t\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}\left|W^{0}(s)\right|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}+\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{t}(X^{x})}\big|f\big(u,X^{x}(t)\big)\big|\right]^{2}\mathrm{d}x&\text{Case 2},\\
\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\vartheta}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{t}(Y^{x})+\overline{\beta}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{t}(Y^{x})}\big|f\big(u,Y^{x}(t)\big)\big|\right]^{2}\mathrm{d}x&\text{Case 3,}\end{cases}

where
u
is uniform on
\{1,\ldots r\}
and independent of
Z^{x}
and
W^{0}
.
Note that for any
t>0
and
x\in\mathbb{R}
, one has
\mathrm{e}^{-2\kappa t|x|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}}\leq 1
.
Thus, by an application of Hölder’s inequality
(i.e.,
\mathbf{E}\left[\mathfrak{X}\,\big|f\big(u,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|\right]^{2}\leq\mathbf{E}[\mathfrak{X}^{2}]\mathbf{E}\big[\big|f\big(u,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\big]

for any random variable
\mathfrak{X}
) as well as (
6.29
)
and (
6.30
)
(for the latter, notice that
0\leq\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{t}(Z)\leq\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{1}(Z)

for all
t\in(0,1)
by (
3.1
)), it suffices to prove
that there exists some constant
C>0
such that

(6.37)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\int_{I}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(u,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x\leq C\|f\|_{2}^{2}.

Toward this end, the fact that
u
is uniform and independent of
Z
implies that

(6.38)

\displaystyle\int_{I}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(u,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{I^{2}}\Pi_{Z}(t;x,y)|f(i,y)|^{2}~\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y=\frac{\|f\|_{2}^{2}}{r},

where the last equality follows from the symmetry of
Z
’s transition kernel and Tonelli’s theorem
(i.e., integrating out the
x
variable first).
Hence (
6.32
) holds.

6.3.5.
Proof of Proposition
6.1
-(4) Part 2: Pointwise Convergence

Let us then assume that each
f(i,\cdot)
is continuous and compactly supported.
We begin by proving the pointwise convergence

(6.39)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\widehat{K}(t)f(a)=f(a)\qquad\text{for every }a\in\mathcal{A}.

For this, by an application of the tower property, we can write

(6.40)

\displaystyle\widehat{K}(t)f(a)=\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)\right]\right].

Since
f
and
Z
are continuous and
U
is piecewise constant, for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
,

(6.41)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)=f(a)\qquad\text{almost surely.}

With
(
6.41
) in hand, we first argue that the same limit holds
when we take the expectation with respect to
N
. For this purpose, we combine
(
6.18
) with the facts that
that
f
is bounded, that there exists some constant
\nu>0
such that
S(i,x)\geq-\nu

(by Lemma
6.6
), and some
\kappa,\nu_{1}>0
such that

\max_{1\leq i<j\leq r}|\xi_{i,j}(x)|\leq\eta(x)=\kappa\big(\log(1+|x|\big)^{\mathfrak{b}}\big)+\nu_{1}

(by (
6.13
)) to bound

\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\left|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)\right|\\
\leq\begin{cases}\displaystyle\max\left\{1,\eta\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}|B^{x}(s)|\right)^{N(t)}\right\}\mathrm{e}^{\nu t}\|f\|_{\infty}&\text{Case 1,}\\
\displaystyle\max\left\{1,\eta\left(\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}X^{x}(s)\right)^{N(t)}\right\}\mathrm{e}^{\nu t+\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}_{t}^{0}(X^{x})}\|f\|_{\infty}&\text{Case 2,}\\
\displaystyle\max\left\{1,\left(\sup_{1\leq i,j\leq r,~y\in(0,\vartheta)}|\xi_{i,j}(y)|\right)^{N(t)}\right\}\mathrm{e}^{\nu t+\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}_{t}^{0}(Y^{x})+\overline{\beta}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}_{t}^{\vartheta}(Y^{x})}\|f\|_{\infty}&\text{Case 3,}\end{cases}

The expectation of this upper bound in
N
is finite almost surely (conditional on
Z
)
for every
a\in\mathcal{A}
,
and thus by the dominated convergence theorem we have that

(6.42)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)\right]=f(a)\qquad\text{almost surely.}

Finally, in order to conclude (
6.39
),
it only remains to prove that the limit (
6.42
)
persists once we take the expectation
\mathbf{E}_{A}
in
(
6.40
). Toward this end,
we note that, by Jensen’s inequality and the boundedness of
f
, one has

(6.43)

\left|\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)\right]\right|\\
\leq\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})|\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}\right]\|f\|_{\infty}.

An application of Proposition
6.7
with
p=1
to (
6.43
) then yields

(6.44)

\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\left|\mathbf{E}_{N}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{a})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{a}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a})}f\big(A^{a}(t)\big)\right]\right|\\
\leq\begin{cases}\mathrm{e}^{\kappa\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}|W^{0}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}+\nu}\|f\|_{\infty}&\text{Case 1,}\\
\mathrm{e}^{\kappa\sup_{0\leq s\leq 1}|W^{0}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}+\nu+\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{1}(X^{x})}\|f\|_{\infty}&\text{Case 2,}\\
\mathrm{e}^{\nu+\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{1}(Y^{x})+\overline{\beta}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{1}(Y^{x})}\|f\|_{\infty}&\text{Case 3.}\end{cases}

By combining (
6.29
) and
(
6.30
) with an application of
Hölder’s inequality, the expectation
\mathbf{E}_{A}
of the dominating function
on the right-hand side of (
6.44
)
is finite for any choice of
a\in\mathcal{A}
. This proves (
6.39
) by dominated convergence.

6.3.6.
Proof of Proposition
6.1
-(4) Part 3: Uniform Integrability

In order to conclude that
\|\widehat{K}(t)f-f\|_{2}\to 0
as
t\to 0

from the pointwise convergence (
6.39
),
we use the Vitali convergence theorem (e.g.,
[
26
, Theorem 2.24]
):
Convergence in
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
follows from
pointwise convergence if
for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a
\delta>0
such that

(6.45)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\int_{\mathcal{K}}|\widehat{K}(t)f(a)|^{2}~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)<\varepsilon\qquad\text{for all }\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{A}\text{ with }\mu(\mathcal{K})<\delta,

and there also exists a
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{A}
with
\mu(\mathcal{K})<\infty
and

(6.46)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\int_{\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{K}}|\widehat{K}(t)f(a)|^{2}~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)<\varepsilon.

Toward this end, if we reapply the bounds in
(
6.35
)–(
6.37
)
to
|\widehat{K}(t)f(a)|^{2}
,
then we see that the proof of (
6.45
)
and (
6.46
) can be reduced to showing that
for every fixed
1\leq i\leq r
, the following holds:
For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a
\delta>0
such that

(6.47)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\int_{\mathcal{K}}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x<\varepsilon\qquad\text{for all }\mathcal{K}\subset I\text{ with }|\mathcal{K}|<\delta,

and there also exists a
\mathcal{K}\subset I
with
|\mathcal{K}|<\infty
and

(6.48)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\int_{I\setminus\mathcal{K}}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x<\varepsilon

(here,
|\mathcal{K}|
denotes the Lebesgue measure).

Toward this end, we note that (
6.47
) is trivial in all cases since
f
is bounded:

(6.49)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\int_{\mathcal{K}}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x\leq\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}\,|\mathcal{K}|;

we can then simply choose
\delta=\varepsilon/\|f\|_{\infty}^{2}
.
Similarly, (
6.48
) is trivial in Case 3 since in that situation

I
is bounded. Thus, it only remains to prove (
6.48
)
in Cases 1 and 2:
If we let
\mathcal{K}_{\kappa}=I\cap[-\kappa,\kappa]
for
\kappa>0
, then

(6.50)

\int_{I\setminus\mathcal{K}_{\kappa}}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x=\int_{(I\setminus\mathcal{K}_{\kappa})\times I}\Pi_{Z}(t;x,y)|f(i,y)|^{2}~\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y\\
=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{P}\big[|Z^{y}(t)|>\kappa\big]|f(i,y)|^{2}~\mathrm{d}y.

For every fixed
y>0
, in Cases 1 and 2 we have that

(6.51)

\displaystyle\lim_{\kappa\to\infty}\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\mathbf{P}\big[|Z^{y}(t)|>\kappa\big]=0.

Since
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
, (
6.48
)
then follows from dominated convergence.
This then concludes the proof of Proposition
6.1
-(4),
and thus also of Proposition
6.1
.

6.4.
Proof of Proposition
6.2

Following-up on the roadmap provided in Section
6.1.3
,
the proof of Proposition
6.2
relies on three distinct
steps, namely: (
6.7
), (
6.8
), and (
6.9
).
The proofs of these steps were outlined in Remarks
6.3
,
6.4
and
6.5
.
We now carry out the details of these calculations.
Similarly to the proof of Proposition
6.1
,
we assume throughout Section
6.4
that
we are restricted to a probability-one event wherein Lemma
6.6
holds.

6.4.1.
Proof of Proposition
6.2
Part 1

We begin with (
6.7
).
Given (
6.10
), it only remains to verify
that the operators
H_{i}+S(i,0)
satisfy the hypotheses of the
one-dimensional Feynman-Kac formula (
3.7
) for each
1\leq i\leq r
.
For this purpose, we note that
a formal version of (
3.7
)
can be obtained by combining
[
62
, Theorem 4.9]

with
a straightforward modification of
[
53
, (3.3’), (3.4), Theorem 3.4 (b), and Lemmas 4.6 and 4.7]
for
the Robin/mixed boundary condition, and
[
11
, Chapter 3-(34) and Theorem 3.27]
for the Dirichlet
boundary condition. For convenience, we point to
[
28
, Theorem 5.4]
for a unified statement that
specifically contains all cases considered in this paper: According to that result,
in order for the Feynman-Kac formula
for
H_{i}+\xi_{i,i}
to hold,
it suffices to check that
S(i,\cdot)=V(i,\cdot)+\xi_{i,i}

can be written as a difference
S(i,\cdot)=S(i,\cdot)_{+}-S(i,\cdot)_{-}
,
where
S(i,\cdot)_{+}
and
S(i,\cdot)_{-}
are nonnegative,

S(i,\cdot)_{-}
is in the Kato class (e.g.,
[
28
, (5.2)]
),
and
S(i,\cdot)_{+}
restricted to any compact set is in the Kato class.
This follows from Lemma
6.6
,
since
S(i,\cdot)_{-}
is bounded and any one-dimensional locally integrable function
(in particular,
S(i,\cdot)_{+}
) is in the Kato class
when restricted to a compact set.

6.4.2.
Proof of Proposition
6.2
Part 2

Next, we prove (
6.8
).
Our first step is to establish the pointwise convergence

(6.52)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0}\big|\mathfrak{G}_{1}\big(t;(i,x)\big)-Qf(i,x)\big|=0\qquad\text{for every }(i,x)\in\mathcal{A}.

Thanks to (
6.11
),
by the triangle and Jensen’s inequalities,

(6.53)

\big|\mathfrak{G}_{1}\big(t;(i,x)\big)-Qf(i,x)\big|\\
\leq\sum_{j\neq i}\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\left|\xi_{i,j}\big(Z^{x}(\tau_{1})\big)\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{(i,x)}(s))\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{(i,x)})}f\big(j,Z^{x}(t)\big)-\xi_{i,j}(x)f(j,x)\right|~\bigg|~\mathcal{J}_{j}(t)\right].

Conditional on
\mathcal{J}_{j}(t)
,
\tau_{1}
is uniform on the interval
[0,t]
. Thus,
if we let
u_{t}
denote a uniform random variable on
[0,t]
independent of
Z
,
and we denote

\mathfrak{Y}_{i,j}(t;x)=\xi_{i,j}\big(Z^{x}(u_{t})\big)\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{u_{t}}S(i,Z^{x}(s))~\mathrm{d}s-\int_{u_{t}}^{t}S(j,Z^{x}(s))~\mathrm{d}s\\
+\mathfrak{Z}_{i,j}(u_{t},Z^{x})}f\big(j,Z^{x}(t)\big),

where

\mathfrak{Z}_{i,j}(u_{t},Z^{x})=\begin{cases}0&\text{Case 1,}\\
\overline{\alpha}_{i}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{[0,u_{t})}(X^{x})+\overline{\alpha}_{j}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{[u_{t},t]}(X^{x})&\text{Case 2,}\\
\overline{\alpha}_{i}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{[0,u_{t})}(Y^{x})+\overline{\beta}_{i}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{[0,u_{t})}(Y^{x})+\overline{\alpha}_{j}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{[u_{t},t]}(Y^{x})+\overline{\beta}_{j}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{[u_{t},t]}(Y^{x})&\text{Case 3,}\\
\end{cases}

then we obtain from (
6.53
) that

(6.54)

\displaystyle\big|\mathfrak{G}_{1}\big(t;(i,x)\big)-Qf(i,x)\big|\leq\sum_{j\neq i}\mathbf{E}_{A}\big[\big|\mathfrak{Y}_{i,j}(t;x)-\xi_{i,j}(x)f(j,x)\big|\big].

Since we only consider regular noises
(in the sense of Definition
2.6
) in Theorem
3.5
, the
\xi_{i,j}

are continuous. Moreover, since
f\in D(\widehat{H})\subset D(\mathcal{E})

(by Proposition
2.14
)
and
D(\mathcal{E})

only contains direct sums of locally absolutely continuous functions (see Definition
2.11
),
each
f(j,\cdot)
is continuous.
Thus,
\mathfrak{Y}_{i,j}(t;x)\to\xi_{i,j}(x)f(j,x)
as
t\to 0
almost surely
for any fixed
i,j
, and
x
. At this point, (
6.52
)
follows if we show that we can apply the dominated convergence theorem
to the expectation
\mathbf{E}_{A}
in (
6.54
).
For this purpose, we remark that since
f\in D(H)
, both
\|f\|_{\mu}
and
\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mu}
are finite;
hence
f
is bounded (see, e.g.,
[
5
, Fact 3.1]

and
[
28
, Lemma 3.1]
).
If we combine this with the facts that
\xi_{i,j}(x)\leq\eta(x)
with
\eta(x)=\kappa\big(\log(1+|x|)\big)^{\mathfrak{b}}+\nu

for some
\kappa,\nu>0

in Cases 1 and 2 (by (
6.13
)),
that there exists some
c>0
such that
S(i,x)\geq-c

for all
1\leq i\leq r
(by Lemma
6.6
),
then we can bound

(6.55)

\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\big|\mathfrak{Y}_{i,j}(t;x)\big|\\
\leq\begin{cases}\eta\left(\sup_{t\in(0,1)}|B^{x}(t)|\right)\mathrm{e}^{c}\|f\|_{\infty}&\text{Case 1,}\\
\eta\left(\sup_{t\in(0,1)}X^{x}(t)\right)\mathrm{e}^{c+\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{1}(X^{x})}\|f\|_{\infty}&\text{Case 2,}\\
\left(\sup_{1\leq i,j\leq r,~y\in(0,\vartheta)}|\xi_{i,j}(y)|\right)\mathrm{e}^{c+\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{1}(Y^{x})+\overline{\beta}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{1}(Y^{x})}\|f\|_{\infty}&\text{Case 3.}\end{cases}

By (
6.30
) and the fact that Brownian motion suprema
have Gaussian tails, this
has a finite expectation for any
x
; thus (
6.52
)
follows by dominated convergence.

We now conclude the proof of (
6.8
)
by showing that (
6.52
) can be improved
to convergence in
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
. For this purpose, we once
again use the Vitali convergence theorem. That is, we must prove
for every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists a
\delta>0
such that

(6.56)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\int_{\mathcal{K}}\big|\mathfrak{G}_{1}\big(t;(i,x)\big)\big|^{2}~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)<\varepsilon\qquad\text{for all }\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{A}\text{ with }\mu(\mathcal{K})<\delta,

and there also exists a
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{A}
with
\mu(\mathcal{K})<\infty
and

(6.57)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\int_{\mathcal{A}\setminus\mathcal{K}}\big|\mathfrak{G}_{1}\big(t;(i,x)\big)\big|^{2}~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)<\varepsilon.

For this purpose, we note that by Jensen’s inequality,

\big|\mathfrak{G}_{1}\big(t;(i,x)\big)\big|^{2}\leq(r-1)\sum_{j\neq i}\mathbf{E}\big[\big|\mathfrak{Y}_{i,j}(t;x)\big|\big]^{2};

then the same argument used to obtain (
6.55
)
(except that we do not bound
f
by its infinity norm)
yields that for every
t\in(0,1)
,

(6.58)

\big|\mathfrak{G}_{1}\big(t;(i,x)\big)\big|^{2}\leq(r-1)\mathrm{e}^{2c}\sum_{j\neq i}\cdots\\
\begin{cases}\mathbf{E}\left[\eta\left(\sup_{t\in(0,1)}|B^{x}(t)|\right)\big|f\big(j,B^{x}(t)\big)\big|\right]^{2}&\text{Case 1,}\\
\mathbf{E}\left[\eta\left(\sup_{t\in(0,1)}X^{x}(t)\right)\mathrm{e}^{\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{1}(X^{x})}\big|f\big(j,X^{x}(t)\big)\big|\right]^{2}&\text{Case 2,}\\
\left(\sup_{1\leq i,j\leq r,~y\in(0,\vartheta)}|\xi_{i,j}(y)|\right)^{2}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{1}(Y^{x})+\overline{\beta}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{1}(Y^{x})}\big|f\big(j,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|\right]^{2}&\text{Case 3.}\end{cases}

By
Hölder’s inequality, the second line of (
6.58
) is bounded above by

\begin{cases}\mathbf{E}\left[\eta\left(\sup_{t\in(0,1)}|B^{x}(t)|\right)^{2}\right]\mathbf{E}\big[\big|f\big(j,B^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\big]&\text{Case 1,}\\
\mathbf{E}\left[\eta\left(\sup_{t\in(0,1)}X^{x}(t)\right)^{2p}\right]^{1/p}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{2q\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{1}(X^{x})}\right]^{1/q}\mathbf{E}\big[\big|f\big(j,X^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\big]&\text{Case 2,}\\
C\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{2\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{1}(Y^{x})+2\overline{\beta}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{1}(Y^{x})}\right]\mathbf{E}\big[\big|f\big(j,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\big]&\text{Case 3,}\end{cases}

for any
p,q>1
such that
1/p+1/q=1
, where
C
is a constant independent of
t
.

By (
6.30
),
this means that in Case 3 the uniform integrability follows from
(
6.47
) and (
6.48
).
We now deal with Cases 1 and 2:
Recall that
\eta(x)=\kappa\big(\log(1+|x|)\big)^{\mathfrak{b}}+\nu
. If we combine the coupling
(
6.16
) with the triangle and Jensen’s inequalities,
and
\log(1+|x|+|y|)^{\mathfrak{b}}\leq C\log(1+|x|)^{\mathfrak{b}}+C|y|^{\mathfrak{b}}
for some constant
C>0
,
we can find some
\overline{\kappa},\overline{\nu}>0
large enough so that

\textstyle\mathbf{E}\left[\eta\left(\sup_{t\in(0,1)}|B^{x}(t)|\right)^{2}\right],\mathbf{E}\left[\eta\left(\sup_{t\in(0,1)}X^{x}(t)\right)^{2p}\right]^{1/p}\leq\overline{\kappa}\big(\log(1+|x|)\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}+\overline{\nu}.

Here, we used the fact that

\textstyle\mathbf{E}\left[\sup_{t\in(0,1)}|W^{0}(s)|^{\mathfrak{c}}\right]<\infty\qquad\text{for all }\mathfrak{c}>0,

and thus this expectation can be absorbed in the constant
\overline{\nu}
.
By (
6.30
),

\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{2q\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{1}(X^{x})}\right]^{1/q}<\infty.

Thus, by (
6.47
) and (
6.48
),
the uniform integrability in (
6.56
) and (
6.57
)
in Cases 1 and 2 follows from this:
For every
\varepsilon>0
, there is some
\delta>0
such that

(6.59)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\int_{\mathcal{K}}\big(\log(1+|x|)\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x<\varepsilon\qquad\text{for all }\mathcal{K}\subset I\text{ with }|\mathcal{K}|<\delta,

and there also exists a
\mathcal{K}\subset I
with
|\mathcal{K}|<\infty
and

(6.60)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\int_{I\setminus\mathcal{K}}\big(\log(1+|x|)\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x<\varepsilon.

Toward this end, we note that for any set
\mathcal{K}\subset I
,
and functions
g,h
, we can rewrite

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{K}}g(x)\mathbf{E}\left[h\big(Z^{x}(t)\big)\right]~\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{I\times I}g(x)\mathbf{1}_{\{x\in\mathcal{K}\}}\Pi_{Z}(t;x,y)h(y)~\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y

(6.61)

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\mathbf{E}\Big[g\big(y+Z^{0}(t)\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{y+Z^{0}(t)\in\mathcal{K}\}}\Big]h(y)~\mathrm{d}y.

Applying this to
g(x)=\big(\log(1+|x|)\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}
and
h(y)=|f(i,y)|^{2}
, we then get

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{K}}\big(\log(1+|x|)\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{I}\mathbf{E}\left[\big(\log(1+|y+Z^{0}(t)|)\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}\mathbf{1}_{\{y+Z^{0}(t)\in\mathcal{K}\}}\right]|f(i,y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{I}\mathbf{E}\left[\big(\log(1+|y|\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}+|Z^{0}(t)|^{2\mathfrak{b}}\big)\mathbf{1}_{\{y+Z^{0}(t)\in\mathcal{K}\}}\right]|f(i,y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y

for some
C>0
independent of
t
.
Then, an application of Hölder’s inequality yields

(6.62)

\int_{\mathcal{K}}\big(\log(1+|x|)\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x\leq C\int_{I}\mathbf{P}\big[Z^{y}(t)\in\mathcal{K}\big]\log(1+|y|\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}|f(i,y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y\\
+C\int_{I}\mathbf{E}\left[|Z^{0}(t)|^{4\mathfrak{b}}\right]^{1/2}\mathbf{P}\big[Z^{y}(t)\in\mathcal{K}\big]^{1/2}|f(i,y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y

for some
C>0
independent of
t
.
Regarding the second line in (
6.62
),
we note that

\sup_{t\in(0,1)}\mathbf{E}\left[|Z^{0}(t)|^{4\mathfrak{b}}\right]^{1/2}<\infty,

and then, by Jensen’s inequality and the fact that
\int|f(i,y)|^{2}~\mathrm{d}y<\infty
,
there exists some
C>0
independent of
t

such that

\int_{I}\mathbf{P}\big[Z^{y}(t)\in\mathcal{K}\big]^{1/2}|f(i,y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y\leq C\left(\int_{I}\mathbf{P}\big[Z^{y}(t)\in\mathcal{K}\big]|f(i,y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y\right)^{1/2}.

Thus, (
6.62
) becomes

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{K}}\big(\log(1+|x|)\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{I}\mathbf{P}\big[Z^{y}(t)\in\mathcal{K}\big]\log(1+|y|\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}|f(i,y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y+C\left(\int_{I}\mathbf{P}\big[Z^{y}(t)\in\mathcal{K}\big]|f(i,y)|^{2}\mathrm{d}y\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=C\int_{\mathcal{K}}\mathbf{E}\left[\log(1+|Z^{x}(t)|\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}\big|f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x+C\left(\int_{\mathcal{K}}\mathbf{E}\left[\big|f\big(i,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\right]~\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/2}

for some
C>0
independent of
t
,
where we have used (
6.61
)
in reverse (with
g=1
) in the third line.
At this point, if we show that
\log(1+|y|\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}|f(i,y)|^{2}

is both integrable and bounded, then (
6.59
)
and (
6.60
)
follows from the same arguments used in (
6.49
)–(
6.51
)
(replace
|f(i,y)|^{2}
therein by
\log(1+|y|\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}|f(i,y)|^{2}
).

Toward this end, since
f\in D(\widehat{H})
we know that

\|S_{+}^{1/2}f\|_{2}<\infty
(see Proposition
2.14

and the definition of
D(\mathcal{E})
therein). Thus,
by (
6.12
) and (
6.13
),
this implies that

(6.63)

\displaystyle\int_{I}\log(1+|y|\big)^{\mathfrak{c}}|f(i,y)|^{2}~\mathrm{d}y<\infty\qquad\text{for every }\mathfrak{c}>0

(in particular for
\mathfrak{c}=2\mathfrak{b}
).
Regarding boundedness,
up to expanding
f(i,y)
into a sum of its real and imaginary parts, we may
assume without loss of generality that
f(i,y)
is real-valued.
Under this assumption, by the fundamental theorem of calculus,

\displaystyle\log(1+|x|\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}f(i,x)^{2}\leq\left|\int_{x}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}y}\big(\log(1+|y|\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}f(i,y)^{2}\big)~\mathrm{d}y\right|,\qquad x\in I.

Using the product rule and Jensen’s inequality, this implies

\log(1+|x|\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}f(i,x)^{2}\leq 2\int_{I}\big(\log(1+|y|)\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}\big|f(i,y)f^{\prime}(i,y)\big|+\frac{\mathfrak{b}\log(1+|y|)^{2\mathfrak{b}-1}f(i,y)^{2}}{1+|y|}~\mathrm{d}y;

thus it only remains to check that the above integral is finite. On the one hand, since
\frac{1}{1+|y|}\leq 1
,
we get that

\int_{I}\frac{\mathfrak{b}\log(1+|y|)^{2\mathfrak{b}-1}f(i,y)^{2}}{1+|y|}~\mathrm{d}y<\infty

by (
6.63
). On the other hand, if we use the inequality
|xy|\leq x^{2}+y^{2}
,
we get

\int_{I}\big(\log(1+|y|)\big)^{2\mathfrak{b}}\big|f(i,y)f^{\prime}(i,y)\big|~\mathrm{d}y\leq\int_{I}\big(\log(1+|y|)\big)^{4\mathfrak{b}}f(i,y)^{2}+f^{\prime}(i,y)^{2}~\mathrm{d}y;

this is finite by a combination of (
6.63
)
and
\|f^{\prime}\|_{\mu}<\infty
. With this in hand, the proof of (
6.8
) is now complete.

6.4.3.
Proof of Proposition
6.2
Part 3

We now finish the proof of Proposition
6.2
—and therefore of Theorem
3.5
—by
establishing (
6.9
).
Recalling the definition of
\mathfrak{G}_{2}
in (
6.6
),
by Jensen’s and Hölder’s inequalities,

(6.64)

\big|\mathfrak{G}_{2}\big(t;(i,x)\big)\big|^{2}\leq t^{-2}\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{(i,x)})|^{4}\mathrm{e}^{-4\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{(i,x)}(s))~\mathrm{d}s-4\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{(i,x)})}\right]^{1/2}\\
\cdot\mathbf{P}\big[N(t)\geq 2\big]^{1/2}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|f\big(A^{(i,x)}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{N(t)\geq 2\}}\Big].

Then, by (
6.34
) (but replacing
f
by
|f|^{2}
), we get that

(6.65)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|f\big(A^{(i,x)}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\mathbf{1}_{\{N(t)\geq 2\}}\Big]\leq r\,\mathbf{P}\big[N(t)\geq 2\big]\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|f\big(u,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\Big],

where
u
is uniform on
\{1,\ldots,r\}
and independent of
Z
.
If we now combine Proposition
6.7
(in the case
p=4
)
with (
6.29
), (
6.30
),
and the fact that
\mathrm{e}^{-t\kappa|x|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}}\leq 1
, then we get

\sup_{t\in(0,1),~(i,x)\in\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[|\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q,A^{(i,x)})|^{4}\mathrm{e}^{-4\int_{0}^{t}S(A^{(i,x)}(s))~\mathrm{d}s-4\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{(i,x)})}\right]^{1/2}<\infty.

If we combine this with (
6.64
) and (
6.65
),
given that
\mathbf{P}[N(t)\geq 2]=O(t^{2})
as
t\to 0
, then we conclude that there exists a constant
C>0
independent of
t
such that

(6.66)

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{G}_{2}(t;\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}\leq Ct\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{E}\Big[\big|f\big(u,Z^{x}(t)\big)\big|^{2}\Big]~\mathrm{d}\mu(i,x).

We then obtain (
6.9
) from (
6.38
).

7.
Proof of Theorem
4.9
: Trace Moment Formulas

We assume throughout Section
7
that
Assumptions
2.3
,

2.4
, and
2.8
hold,
that
\xi_{i,i}
are white noises with variance
\sigma^{2}>0
,
and that
\xi_{i,j}
(
i\neq j
) are white noises with variance
\upsilon^{2}>0
.
This section is organized as follows: We begin with an outline in Section
7.1
;
then the technical results appearing in that outline are proved in Sections
7.2
–
7.8
.

7.1.
Proof Outline

7.1.1.
Step 0. Smooth Approximations

Following Section
1.3.3
, we aim to approximate
\widehat{H}

with a sequence of operators whose noises are regular, and then apply the Feynman-Kac
formula in Theorem
3.5

to compute trace moments of these approximations. For this purpose, we introduce the following smooth approximations of

\xi
’s entries:

Notation 7.1
.

Given
\varphi,\psi:I\to\mathbb{R}
, we let
\varphi\star\psi
denote their convolution.
That is,

\varphi\star\psi(x)=\int_{I}\varphi(y)\psi(x-y)~\mathrm{d}y,\qquad x\in I.

More generally, if
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{F}
and
\psi:I\to\mathbb{R}
,
then we let
f\star\psi:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{F}
denote the convolution of
\psi
with
f
’s components.
That is, for every
a=(i,x)\in\mathcal{A}
, one has

f\star\psi(a)=f\star\psi(i,x)=\int_{I}f(i,y)\psi(y-x)~\mathrm{d}y.

Definition 7.2
.

Suppose that
(W_{i,j})_{1\leq i\leq j\leq r}
denote the independent Brownian motions
in
\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{F}
that are used to define the white noises
\xi_{i,j}
as

\xi_{i,j}(f,f)=\begin{cases}\sigma W_{i,i}^{\prime}(f,f)&\text{if }i=j,\\
\upsilon W_{i,j}^{\prime}(f,f)&\text{if }i<j,\end{cases}

in the sense of (
2.5
). More specifically,
W_{i,i}
are just
standard Brownian motions in
\mathbb{R}
, and for
i<j
,
we can decompose
W_{i,j}
as the sum

(7.1)

\displaystyle W_{i,j}(x)=\begin{cases}W_{i,j;1}(x)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\big(W_{i,j;1}(x)+W_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}\big)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C},\\
\frac{1}{2}\big(W_{i,j;1}(x)+W_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}+W_{i,j;\mathsf{j}}(x)\mathsf{j}+W_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}(x)\mathsf{k}\big)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H},\end{cases}

where
W_{i,j;1},W_{i,j;\mathsf{i}},W_{i,j;\mathsf{j}},W_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}
are i.i.d. standard Brownian motions in
\mathbb{R}
.

Let
\overline{\varrho}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a nonnegative, smooth, compactly supported,
and even probability density function (i.e.,
\overline{\varrho}(-x)=\overline{\varrho}(x)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\int_{\mathbb{R}}\overline{\varrho}(x)~\mathrm{d}x=1
). Let
\varrho=\overline{\varrho}\star\overline{\varrho}
. For every
\varepsilon>0
,
define the rescaled function
\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)=\varepsilon^{-1}\overline{\varrho}(x/\varepsilon)
, and for
\zeta,\eta>0
, let

(7.2)

\displaystyle\varrho_{\zeta}=\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}=\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\eta}.

For
\varepsilon=0
, we use the convention that
\overline{\varrho}_{0}=\varrho_{0,0}=\delta_{0}

is the Dirac delta distribution.

For every
1\leq i\leq j\leq r
and
\varepsilon\geq 0
, we define the quadratic forms

\xi^{\varepsilon}_{i,j}(f,g)=\begin{cases}\sigma(W_{i,j}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})^{\prime}(f,g)&\text{if }i=j\\
\upsilon(W_{i,j}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})^{\prime}(f,g)&\text{if }i<j\end{cases}.

In particular,
\xi^{0}_{i,j}=\xi_{i,j}
, and for
\varepsilon>0
, the process
\xi^{\varepsilon}_{i,j}
is a regular noise
with covariance
\sigma^{2}\varrho_{\varepsilon}
in
\mathbb{R}
or covariance
\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\varepsilon}
in
\mathbb{F}

in the sense of Definition
2.6
,
depending on whether
i=j

(see, e.g.,
[
28
, Remark 3.7]
).

Finally, for any
\varepsilon,\zeta\geq 0
, we define the matrix noise

\xi^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(f,g)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi^{\varepsilon}_{i,i}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(i,\cdot)\big)+\sum_{i\neq j}\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(j,\cdot)\big),\qquad\varepsilon,\zeta\geq 0;

in particular, when
\varepsilon
and
\zeta
are positive, the diagonal terms
\xi^{\varepsilon}_{i,i}
are i.i.d. regular noises in
\mathbb{R}
with covariance
\sigma^{2}\varrho_{\varepsilon}
,
and the off-diagonal terms
\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}
are i.i.d. regular noises in
\mathbb{F}
with covariance
\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}
. In keeping with some of the notation introduced in Theorem
3.5
,
for
\zeta>0
we denote the off-diagonal part of
\xi^{\varepsilon,\zeta}
as

(7.3)

\displaystyle Q^{\zeta}=\sum_{i\neq j}\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}.

We can now define the smooth approximations of
\widehat{H}
, which are all on the same probability
space by virtue of
\xi^{\varepsilon,\zeta}
all being defined from the same Brownian motions
W_{i,j}
:
For any
\varepsilon,\zeta\geq 0
, let

(7.4)

\displaystyle\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}=H+\xi^{\varepsilon,\zeta}

be the operator constructed in Proposition
2.14
; since the noises
\xi_{i,i}^{\varepsilon}
and
\xi_{i,j}^{\zeta}
are all regular or white—depending on whether or not
\varepsilon,\zeta=0
—the
assumptions of Proposition
2.14
are satisfied for any choice of
\varepsilon,\zeta\geq 0
. In particular, we note
that
\widehat{H}=\widehat{H}^{0,0}.

In the remainder of Section
7.1

(namely, Sections
7.1.2
and
7.1.3
), we state a number of technical
results related to the limits of
\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}
as
\varepsilon\to 0
and

\zeta\to 0
, and then use the latter to prove Theorem
4.9
.
Before getting on with this, however, we make a remark on the specific form
of our approximation scheme:
At first glance, it might appear more natural to simply consider the sequence of
operators
\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon}=\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon,\varepsilon}
and then take a single limit of the
latter as
\varepsilon\to 0
—as suggested by the informal outline of proof in Section
1.3.3
.
The reason why we consider the two separate parameters
\varepsilon
