and
\zeta
is entirely technical, and has to do with the convergence
of the kernels

\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(t;a,b)=\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}}(a,b),\qquad a,b\in\mathcal{A}.

Namely, it is easier to
analyze the limits of this object if we first send
\varepsilon\to 0
and then after that send
\zeta\to 0

(specifically, the sequence of limits (
7.12
)–(
7.14
) below).
A key factor in this is that, as mentoned in
(
1.22
), it is possible to provide a pathwise interpretation of
the kernel of
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}
when
\zeta>0
:

Definition 7.3
.

Recall the Brownian motions
W_{i,i}
introduced in Definition
7.2
;
i.e.,
\xi_{i,i}=\sigma W_{i,i}^{\prime}
.
Given
a=(i,x)\in\mathcal{A}
, we denote

(7.5)

\displaystyle W^{0}_{\mathrm{d}}(a)=W^{0}_{\mathrm{d}}(i,x)=\sigma W_{i,i}(x)

and for any
continuous
f:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}
with compact support, we let

\int_{\mathcal{A}}f(a)~\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{\mathrm{d}}(a)=\sigma\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{I}f(i,x)~\mathrm{d}W_{i,i}(x),

where the summands on the right-hand side are interpreted as pathwise stochastic integrals
(see Remark
7.4
for more details).
Then, let
S^{0}
denote the functional

(7.6)

\displaystyle S^{0}(f)=\int_{\mathcal{A}}V(a)f(a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)+\int_{\mathcal{A}}f(a)~\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{\mathrm{d}}(a),

and let
Q^{\zeta}
be as in (
7.3
).
For any
\zeta>0
, we define the kernel

(7.7)

\displaystyle\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t;a,b)=\Pi_{A}(t;a,b)\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q^{\zeta},A^{a,b}_{t})\,\mathrm{e}^{-S^{0}(L_{t}(A^{a,b}_{t}))+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a,b}_{t})}\right],\qquad a,b\in\mathcal{A},

where we recall that the local time
L_{t}(A)
is defined in Definition
4.4
.

Remark 7.4
.

If
f:I\to\mathbb{R}
is continuously differentiable, then a pathwise stochastic integral
can be defined by means of the integration by parts formula

\int_{I}f(x)~\mathrm{d}W_{i,i}(x)=-\int_{I}f^{\prime}(x)W_{i,i}(x)~\mathrm{d}x+\text{boundary terms}.

However, in (
7.7
) we evaluate these stochastic integrals
in the local time process of
A^{a,b}_{t}
, which is only locally Hölder continuous
with exponent strictly less than
1/2
. Thus, we need a slightly more sophisticated notion of pathwise stochastic integration.
For the purposes of this paper, the only two properties that we need this stochastic integral to satisfy
are as follows:

(1)

If we let
(\Omega_{1},\mathcal{F}_{1},\mathbf{P}_{1})
denote the probability space on which

W_{1,1},\ldots,W_{r,r}
are defined, and
(\Omega_{2},\mathcal{F}_{2},\mathbf{P}_{2})
be the probability space on which
(A^{a,b}_{t}:a,b\in\mathcal{A})
(and its local times) are defined, then for every fixed
1\leq i\leq r
and
t>0
the map

\big((a,b),\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\big)\mapsto\int_{I}L^{(i,y)}\big(A^{a,b}_{t}(\omega_{2})\big)~\mathrm{d}W_{i,i}(\omega_{1};y)\in\mathbb{R}

is measurable with respect to the product
\mu\times\mathbf{P}_{1}\times\mathbf{P}_{2}
and the Borel
\sigma
-algebra on
\mathbb{R}
,
where
W_{i,i}(\omega_{1};\cdot)
denotes the realization of the Brownian motion
W_{i,i}
for the sample
\omega_{1}\in\Omega_{1}
,
and likewise for
A^{a,b}_{t}(\omega_{2})
.
Among other things, this ensures that the map

\big((a,b),\omega_{1},\omega_{2}\big)\mapsto\Pi_{A}(t;a,b)\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q^{\zeta},A^{a,b}_{t})\,\mathrm{e}^{-S^{0}_{\omega_{1}}(L_{t}(A^{a,b}_{t}(\omega_{2})))+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a,b}_{t}(\omega_{2}))}\in\mathbb{R}

is measurable, where
S^{0}_{\omega_{1}}(f)
denotes the realization of (
7.6
)
associated to
W_{i,i}(\omega_{1};\cdot)
.
Therefore, the random kernel in (
7.7
) is a well-defined
measurable function with respect to
\mu\times\mathbf{P}_{1}

by Fubini’s theorem.

(2)

For any continuous and compactly supported functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}:I\to\mathbb{R}

and indices
i_{1},\ldots,i_{n}\in\{1,\ldots,r\}
, the map

\omega_{1}\mapsto\left(\int_{I}f_{1}(x)~\mathrm{d}W_{i_{1},i_{1}}(\omega_{1};x),\ldots,\int_{I}f_{n}(x)~\mathrm{d}W_{i_{n},i_{n}}(\omega_{1};x)\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

is a multivariate Gaussian random vector with mean zero and covariance

\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{I}f_{k}(x)~\mathrm{d}W_{i_{k},i_{k}}(x)\int_{I}f_{\ell}(x)~\mathrm{d}W_{i_{\ell},i_{\ell}}(x)\right]=\mathbf{1}_{\{i_{k}=i_{\ell}\}}\langle f_{k},f_{\ell}\rangle.

Thus, the contribution of the
W_{i,i}
’s to any joint moments
\mathbf{E}\big[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{k}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\big]

can be calculated from (
7.7
) using Fubini’s theorem: For any fixed continuous
and compactly supported functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{R}

(such as
A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}}(\omega_{2})
’s local times for any fixed
\omega_{2}\in\Omega_{2}

and
a_{k}\in\mathcal{A}
), the expectation

\mathbf{E}\left[\exp\left(-\sigma\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{I}f_{k}(i,x)~\mathrm{d}W_{i,i}(x)\right)\right]=\exp\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|f_{k}\|_{\mu}^{2}\right)

amounts to a straightforward Gaussian moment generating function calculation.

For this, any measurable pathwise stochastic integral that is almost-surely equal to the
stochastic integral defined as an
L^{2}(\Omega_{1})
-limit (using the Itô isometry) for
any fixed continuous deterministic integrand suffices.
For instance, we can use Karandikar’s simple dyadic construction in
[
44
]

(see
[
28
, Section 3.2.1 and Appendix A]
for the details of how
Karandikar’s pathwise integral satisfies the two conditions above), instead of more sophisticated
constructs such as Terry Lyons’ rough paths
[
47
]
.

We now carry on with the proof of Theorem
4.9
.

7.1.2.
Step 1. Kernel Moment Limits

Thanks to Theorem
3.5
(as well as (
7.7
)),
the trace moments of
\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}
for
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
\zeta>0
can be computed explicitly
as follows:

Proposition 7.5
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{N}
,

\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in(0,\infty)^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n})\in[0,\infty)^{n},
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=(\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n})\in(0,\infty)^{n}

be fixed. Let
|\boldsymbol{t}|=t_{1}+\cdots+t_{n}
denote the
\ell_{1}
norm of
\boldsymbol{t}
,
and for every
0\leq s\leq|\boldsymbol{t}|
, let

(7.8)

\displaystyle\zeta(s)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\zeta_{k}\mathbf{1}_{\{s\in[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})\}},

with the usual convention that
t_{0}=0
. In words,
\zeta(s)=\zeta_{k}
if
s
lies in the
k^{\mathrm{th}}

sub-interval (of length
t_{k}
) in
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)
.
Define the functionals

(7.9)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A)=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\mathbf{1}_{\{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}}\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(p,U)\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta(\tau_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(\tau_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-Z(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big),

(with the convention that the sum over
p
is equal to one if
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)=0
)
where we recall that
\mathcal{P}_{n}
and
\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)
are defined in Definitions
1.13
and
4.2

and that
\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}
is defined in (
7.2
);
and

(7.10)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{n}L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}(A)\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{k}}\right\|_{\mu}^{2},

where we recall that
L^{a}_{[s,t)}(A)
is defined in Definition
4.4
.
It holds that

(7.11)

\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{k},\zeta_{k}}(t_{k};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right]\\
=\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}\left[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{a}).

With this in hand, we can prove that approximate trace moments are finite (in fact, uniformly integrable)
and converge to the expression stated in Theorem
4.9
:

Proposition 7.6
.

For every
\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in(0,\infty)^{n}
,

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in[0,\infty)^{n},~\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in(0,\infty)^{n}}\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{k},\zeta_{k}}(t_{k};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right|^{2}\right]<\infty.

Proposition 7.7
.

For every
t,\zeta>0
,

(7.12)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(t;a,a)-\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right)^{2}\right]=0.

Proposition 7.8
.

For every
\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in(0,\infty)^{n}
,
one has

(7.13)

\displaystyle\lim_{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n}\to 0}\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta_{k}}(t_{k};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right]=\text{right-hand-side of }\eqref{Equation: Trace Moment Formula},

and for every
t>0
, one has

(7.14)

\displaystyle\lim_{\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}\to 0}\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta_{1}}(t;a,a)-\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta_{2}}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right)^{2}\right]=0.

These four propositions are respectively proved in Sections

7.2
,
7.3
,
7.4
,
and
7.5
.

7.1.3.
Step 2. Operator Limits

With Propositions
7.6
–
7.8

in hand, the proof of Theorem
4.9
now relies on ensuring that
the limits in (
7.13
) coincide with the mixed moments
of
\widehat{H}=\widehat{H}^{0,0}
in (
1.2
).
Two key technical ingredients in this process are the following eigenvalue boundedness and convergence results:

Proposition 7.9
.

For every
0<\kappa<1
, there exists a finite random variable
\nu>0
such that,
almost surely,

(1-\kappa)\lambda_{k}(H)-\nu\leq\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon,\zeta})\leq(1+\kappa)\lambda_{k}(H)+\nu

for every
k\geq 1
and
\varepsilon,\zeta\in[0,1)
.

Proposition 7.10
.

Let
\zeta>0
be fixed. Almost surely, every vanishing sequence
(\varepsilon_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}

has a subsequence
(\varepsilon_{m_{\ell}})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
along which

(7.15)

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon_{m_{\ell}},\zeta})=\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta})\qquad\text{for every $k\geq 1$.}

Moreover, almost surely,
every vanishing sequence
(\zeta_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}

has a subsequence
(\zeta_{m_{\ell}})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
along which

(7.16)

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta_{m_{\ell}}})=\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H})\qquad\text{for every $k\geq 1$.}

These results (along with Proposition
2.12
) are proved in
Sections
7.6
,
7.7
, and
7.8
.

Remark 7.11
.

Unlike the kernel convergence results in
Propositions
7.6
–
7.8
,
there is no advantage to considering the limits
\varepsilon\to 0
and
\zeta\to 0
separately in
Proposition
7.10
. This is only done for consistency
with the kernel convergence results.

7.1.4.
Step 3. Proof of Theorem
4.9

We are now in a position to wrap up the proof of Theorem
4.9
.
First, we establish that for every
t,\zeta>0
, almost surely,

(7.17)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a).

As
L^{2}
convergence implies convergence in probability,
thanks to (
7.12
),
there exists a vanishing sequence
(\varepsilon_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,1)
such that

(7.18)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)=\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{m},\zeta}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)

almost surely. By combining Theorem
3.5

with Propositions
7.9
and
7.10
,
we can restrict this convergence to a (possibly) smaller probability-one event on which the following two conditions hold: On the one hand, by Theorem
3.5
,

(7.19)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{m},\zeta}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)=\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon_{m},\zeta}}\big]\qquad\text{for every }m\in\mathbb{N};

on the other hand there exists a subsequence
(\varepsilon_{m_{\ell}})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
(which may depend on the
specific outcome selected in the probability-one event)
along which

(7.20)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]=\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon_{m_{\ell}},\zeta}}\big].

More specifically, in order to get (
7.20
),
we combine the pointwise convergence of eigenvalues in (
7.15
)
along a subsequence
with an application of dominated convergence in the series

\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}}\big]=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t\lambda_{k}(\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon,\zeta})}

using
the bounds in Proposition

7.9
and (
5.3
).
A combination of (
7.19
)
with the limits (
7.18
)
and (
7.20
) yields that (
7.17
) holds on a probability-one
event, as desired.

We now obtain the statement of Theorem
4.9
:
Let
t_{1},\ldots,t_{n}>0
be fixed. By (
7.14
), there exists a vanishing sequence

(\zeta_{m})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
and some square-integrable random variables
\mathrm{T}(t_{k})
such that

(7.21)

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta_{m}}(t_{k};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)=\mathrm{T}(t_{k})

almost surely for all
1\leq k\leq n
.
By (
7.13
) and Proposition
7.6

(the latter of which implies the uniform integrability of
\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t_{k};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)
over
\zeta\in(0,1)
),

\lim_{m\to\infty}\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta_{m}}(t_{k};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right]=\mathbf{E}\big[\mathrm{T}(t_{1})\cdots\mathrm{T}(t_{n})\big]=\text{right-hand-side of }\eqref{Equation: Trace Moment Formula}.

It now only remains to show that

(7.22)

\displaystyle\mathrm{T}(t_{k})=\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{k}\widehat{H}}\big]\qquad\text{almost surely for all }1\leq k\leq n.

Toward this end,
by (
7.16
) and
(
5.3
)/Proposition
7.9

(the latter two of which once again allows to use dominated convergence), we can restrict to
a probability-one event on which there always exists a subsequence
(\zeta_{m_{\ell}})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
along which

\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{k}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta_{m_{\ell}}}}\big]=\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{k}\widehat{H}}\big]\qquad\text{for all }1\leq k\leq n.

If we combine this limit with (
7.17
) and (
7.21
),
then we obtain (
7.22
),
thus concluding the proof of Theorem
4.9
.

7.2.
Proof of Proposition
7.5

Recall the notations
Q^{\zeta}
(
\zeta>0
) and
S^{0}

introduced respectively in (
7.3
) and (
7.6
),
and for each
\varepsilon>0
, define also the functional

(7.23)

\displaystyle W^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{d}}(a)=W^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{d}}(i,x)=\sigma W^{\varepsilon}_{i,i}(x);\qquad S^{\varepsilon}(f)=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\Big(V(a)+\big(W^{\varepsilon}_{\mathrm{d}}(a)\big)^{\prime}\Big)f(a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a),

By definition of the kernels
\widehat{K}^{\zeta,\varepsilon}
for
\varepsilon\geq 0
and
\zeta>0

in (
3.5
) and (
7.7
), one has

(7.24)

\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{k},\zeta_{k}}(t_{k};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right]\\
=\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\Pi_{A}(t_{k};a_{k},a_{k})\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q^{\zeta_{k}},A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}})\,\mathrm{e}^{-S^{\varepsilon_{k}}(L_{t_{k}}(A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}}))+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}})}\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu(a_{k})\right],

where
A
is independent of
\xi
(i.e., the randomness in
Q^{\zeta_{k}}
and
S^{\varepsilon_{k}}
).
In order to calculate this expectation, we aim to apply Fubini’s theorem three times:
Firstly to take the product over
k
inside the
\mathrm{d}\mu(a)

integrals (and thus combine the latter into a single integral with respect to
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\mathcal{A}^{n}
);
secondly to combine the product of expectations
\mathbf{E}_{A}[\cdot]

into a single expectation with respect to a concatenated path
A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
;
and thirdly to bring the expectation with respect to
\xi

inside the integral and expectation with respect to
\mathcal{A}^{n}
and
A
.
Thus, if we define the functionals

(7.25)

\mathfrak{n}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(Q^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},A)=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}(-1)^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\prod_{k=1}^{n}\left(\prod_{\ell=N(t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1})+1}^{N(t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}\xi^{\zeta_{k}}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)\right)\\
=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}(-1)^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big),

where we recall the notation of
\zeta(s)
in (
7.8
), and

(7.26)

\displaystyle S^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big(L_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\big)=\sum_{i=1}^{k}S^{\varepsilon_{k}}\left(L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0},\ldots,t_{k})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\right)

(i.e., the respective product and sum
\prod_{k=1}^{n}\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q^{\zeta_{k}},A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}})
and
\sum_{k=1}^{n}S^{\varepsilon_{k}}(L_{t_{k}}(A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}}))

where the independent processes
A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}}
in each expectation
\mathbf{E}_{A}[\cdot]
in (
7.24
) have been combined into a single
concatenated process
A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
), then
an application of Fubini’s theorem as described above allows to rewrite (
7.24
) as follows:

(7.27)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(Q^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\,\mathrm{e}^{-S^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(L_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}))+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{a}).

Once this is established, the proof of Proposition
7.5

reduces to verifying that (
7.27
)
is equal to the right-hand side of (
7.11
). Before doing so,
however, we verify that the application of Fubini’s theorem is legitimate.

7.2.1.
Step 1. Justify Fubini’s Theorem

In order to be able to apply Fubini’s theorem and obtain (
7.27
), it suffices to check
that the product

\prod_{k=1}^{n}\Pi_{A}(t_{k};a_{k},a_{k})\mathfrak{n}_{t}(Q^{\zeta_{k}},A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}})\,\mathrm{e}^{-S^{\varepsilon_{k}}(L_{t_{k}}(A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}}))+\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}})}

is integrable with respect to
\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\otimes\mathbf{E}_{A}\otimes\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{a})
, which
by Tonelli’s theorem reduces to

(7.28)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\left|\mathfrak{n}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(Q^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\right|\mathrm{e}^{-S^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(L_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}))+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{a})<\infty.

Since the diagonal and off-diagonal entries of
\xi
are independent
and the boundary term
\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}
does not depend on
\xi
we can write this as

(7.29)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\left|\mathfrak{n}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(Q^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\right|\right]\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-S^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(L_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}))}\right]\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{a})<\infty.

We control the terms appearing in the expectation
\mathbf{E}_{A}[\cdot]

in (
7.29
) one at a time:
If we denote
\boldsymbol{a}
’s components by
a_{k}=(i_{k},x_{k})

for
1\leq k\leq n

and we let
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, then
by (
6.18
) we have that

(7.30)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\leq\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=\begin{cases}0&\text{Case 1},\\
\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(X^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})&\text{Case 2},\\
\overline{\alpha}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{0}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Y^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\overline{\beta}_{\mathrm{m}}\mathfrak{L}^{\vartheta}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Y^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})&\text{Case 3}.\end{cases}

Next, by (
7.25
) and Hölder’s inequality,

\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\left|\mathfrak{n}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(Q^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\right|\right]\leq\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\left|\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)\right|^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\right]^{1/N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}.

Note that the components of
\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}
(or
\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}
itself if
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
)
are i.i.d. centered Gaussian processes on
\mathbb{R}
with stationary covariance
\upsilon^{2}\varrho^{\zeta}=\upsilon^{2}\zeta^{-1}\varrho(\cdot/\zeta)
. Therefore, if we denote the minimum
\zeta_{\mathrm{m}}=\min\{\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n}\}
,
then there exists some deterministic constant
\theta>0

(which depends only on
|\boldsymbol{t}|
and
\mathbb{F}
) such that

(7.31)

\mathbf{E}_{Q}\big[|\mathfrak{n}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Q,A)|\big]\leq\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\mathbf{E}_{Q}\Big[\big|\xi_{J_{\ell}}^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}(0)\big|^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\Big]^{1/N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\\
\leq\theta\big(2\upsilon^{2}\zeta_{\mathrm{m}}^{-1}\varrho(0)\big)^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)+1}{2}\right),

where
\Gamma
is the Gamma function.

Lastly,
by definition of
S^{\varepsilon}
in (
7.6
) and
(
7.23
), we can alternatively write

S^{\varepsilon}\big(L_{[s,t)}(A)\big)=\int_{s}^{t}V\big(A(u)\big)~\mathrm{d}u+\int_{\mathcal{A}}L_{[s,t)}(A)\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(a)~\mathrm{d}W_{\mathrm{d}}(a).

Thus, by definition of
S^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}
in (
7.26
)
and of
W_{\mathrm{d}}
in (
7.5
), we get that

S^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}\big(L_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\big)=\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V\big(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s+\int_{\mathcal{A}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}(A)\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{k}}(a)~\mathrm{d}W_{\mathrm{d}}(a)

is Gaussian with mean
\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V\big(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s
and variance

\sigma^{2}\left\|\sum_{k=1}^{n}L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}(A)\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{k}}\right\|_{\mu}^{2}.

Thus, by definition of
\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
in (
7.10
) and a moment generating function calculation,

(7.32)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-S^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(L_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}))}\right]=\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}.

By Assumption
2.8
, there exists some
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}\in(0,1]
and
\kappa,\nu>0

such that
V(i,x)\geq\kappa|x|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}-\nu
for all
x\in I
(in Case 1 and 2, take
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}=\min\{1,\mathfrak{a}\}
, where

\mathfrak{a}>0
in as in Assumption
2.8
; in Case 3, this follows from the fact
that
V
is bounded below). Therefore,

(7.33)

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s}\leq\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}~\mathrm{d}s+\nu|\boldsymbol{t}|}.

By Jensen’s inequality,

(7.34)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\leq\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{k}}\|_{\mu}^{2}.

Then, since
\varrho_{\varepsilon}
is a probability density function for all
\varepsilon>0
, it follows from applying Young’s convolution inequality to (
7.34
) that

(7.35)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\leq\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}.

For any nonnegative function
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{R})
,
if we denote
\overline{f}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}f(i,x)
, then

(7.36)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\int_{I}f(i,x)^{2}~\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{I}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{r}f(i,x)\right)^{2}~\mathrm{d}x=\|\overline{f}\|_{2}^{2}.

Thus, we get from (
7.35
) that

(7.37)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\leq\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}.

At this point, if we put (
7.30
),
(
7.31
), (
7.33
),
and (
7.37
) into (
7.29
),
then justifying (
7.27
) reduces
to proving the following claim:

(7.38)

\mathbf{E}\left[\big(2\upsilon^{2}\zeta_{\mathrm{m}}^{-1}\varrho(0)\big)^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)+1}{2}\right)\right]\int_{I^{n}}\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\\
\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}~\mathrm{d}s+\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\exp\left(\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right]~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}<\infty.

Since
N
is a Poisson process,
\mathbf{E}\left[\big(2\upsilon^{2}\zeta_{\mathrm{m}}^{-1}\varrho(0)\big)^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)+1}{2}\right)\right]
is finite.
Thus, by an application of Hölder’s inequality, (
7.38
) reduces to

(7.39)

\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in I^{n}}\mathbf{E}\left[\exp\left(\frac{3n\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}\right)\right]^{1/3}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{3\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]^{1/3}\\
\cdot\int_{I^{n}}\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-3\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}~\mathrm{d}s}\right]^{1/3}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}<\infty.

If we combine (
6.23
), (
6.25
), and (
6.28
),
then we get that the second line in (
7.39
) is finite.
As for the expectations on the first line of (
7.39
),
we note that

(7.40)

\displaystyle L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{i},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{i+1})}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{distr.}}}{{=}}L_{t_{i+1}}(Z^{x_{i+1},x_{i+1}}_{t_{i+1}})\qquad\text{for }0\leq i\leq n-1,

and that these variables are independent for different
i
’s. Moreover,

(7.41)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{i},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{i+1})}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}),

(7.42)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{i},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{i+1})}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathrm{distr.}}}{{=}}\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{t_{i+1}}(Z^{x_{i+1},x_{i+1}}_{t_{i+1}})\qquad\text{for }0\leq i\leq n-1,

with the random variables on the second line being independent for different
i
’s.
Thus, we get that the first line of (
7.39
)
is finite by combining (
6.30
)
with

(7.43)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in I}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{\nu\|L_{t}(Z_{t}^{x,x})\|_{2}^{2}}\right]<\infty\qquad\text{for every }\nu,t>0,

the latter of which is proved in
[
28
, Lemma 5.11]
.

This concludes the proof that we can use Fubini’s theorem in the calculation
of (
7.24
); we thus proceed to the computation of
the right-hand side of (
7.11
)
using (
7.27
).

7.2.2.
Step 2. Smooth Mixed Moments Calculation

If we use the independence of the diagonal and off-diagonal noises in
\xi
and the fact that
\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}

does not depend on
\xi
, then we can rewrite (
7.27
) as

(7.44)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}_{A}\left[\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(Q^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\right]\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-S^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(L_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}))}\right]\mathrm{e}^{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{a}).

If we plug (
7.32
) into this and compare the result with
the right-hand side of (
7.11
), then we see that the only
claim that remains to be proved to get the statement of Proposition
7.5

is the following:

(7.45)

\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\mathfrak{n}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(Q^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},A)\right]=\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A)\\
=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\mathbf{1}_{\{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(t)}}\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(p,U)\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\varrho_{\zeta(\tau_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(\tau_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-Z(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big),

where the second line is just a repeat of (
7.9
) for convenience.
When
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}
, this is a
straightforward consequence of Isserlis’ theorem
[
43
]
.
The argument is somewhat similar for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}
, but
rather more involved since the product in
\mathbb{H}
is not commutative.
We thus finish the proof of Proposition
7.5

on a case-by-case basis:

7.2.3.
Step 2 Part 1. Proof of (
7.45
) for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C}

For
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
, we invoke the statement
of Isserlis’ theorem from
[
51
, (1.7) and (1.8)]
, whereby

\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)\right]=\mathbf{1}_{\{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(t)}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell_{1}})}_{J_{\ell_{1}}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell_{1}})\big)\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell_{1}})}_{J_{\ell_{2}}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big)\right]

By independence,

\mathbf{E}\big[\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{k,\ell}(y)\big]=0

whenever
(i,j)\neq(k,\ell)
and
(i,j)\neq(\ell,k)
. Otherwise, the analysis depends on
\mathbb{F}
:

If
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
,
then it is easy to see (e.g., the calculation in
[
28
, (3.3)]
,
where the values of
\varepsilon
in
\Xi_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}
are not equal) that for any
\zeta,\eta>0
and
x,y\in I
,

(7.46)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{i,j}(y)\right]=\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{j,i}(y)\right]=\upsilon^{2}\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\eta}=\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}(x-y).

(
7.45
) for
\mathbb{R}
then follows directly by the definition of
\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)

in Definition
4.2
-(1).

If
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
,
then looking back at the decomposition (
7.1
),
we can write

\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}(x)=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\left(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}\right),\qquad\zeta>0,~x\in I,

where
\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{l}}=(W_{i,j;\mathsf{l}}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta})^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{l}=1,\mathsf{i}
.
Thus, if we reuse the calculation done in (
7.46
),
we get that, in the complex case,

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{i,j}(y)\right]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;1}(y)+\big(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(y)+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;1}(y)\big)\mathsf{i}-\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(y)\right]

(7.47)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\upsilon^{2}}{2}\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}(x-y)+0-\tfrac{\upsilon^{2}}{2}\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}(x-y)=0.

and similarly

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{j,i}(y)\right]=\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{i,j}(y)^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\tfrac{1}{2}\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;1}(y)+\big(-\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(y)+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;1}(y)\big)\mathsf{i}+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(y)\right]

(7.48)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{\upsilon^{2}}{2}\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}(x-y)+0+\tfrac{\upsilon^{2}}{2}\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}(x-y)=\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}(x-y).

where in both calculations we have used the fact that
W_{i,j;1}
and
W_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}

are independent.
(
7.45
) for
\mathbb{C}
then follows by the definition of
\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)

in Definition
4.2
-(2).

7.2.4.
Step 2 Part 1. Proof of (
7.45
) for
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}

In the case of quaternions, Isserlis’ theorem does not
apply directly due to the lack of commutativity.
To get around this, we proceed as follows:
Let
q=a+b\mathsf{i}+c\mathsf{j}+d\mathsf{k}
be a quaternion.
We recall that there exists an injective homomorphism from

\mathbb{F}
to the space of
2\times 2
complex matrices via
the representation

\displaystyle q=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}a+b\mathsf{i}&c+d\mathsf{i}\\
-(c-d\mathsf{i})&a-b\mathsf{i}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}q^{(0,0)}&q^{(0,1)}\\
q^{(1,0)}&q^{(1,1)}\end{array}\right].

(Note that we index the entries
q^{(h,l)}
of
q

using binary pairs
(h,l)\in\mathcal{B}_{1}
, recalling
the notations
\mathcal{B}_{n}
and
\mathcal{B}_{n}^{h,l}
in Definition
4.1
.)
Moreover, if
q
is random, then

\mathbf{E}[q]=\mathbf{E}[a]+\mathbf{E}[b]\mathsf{i}+\mathbf{E}[c]\mathsf{j}+\mathbf{E}[d]\mathsf{k}

implies by linearity of expectation that

\mathbf{E}[q]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{E}[a]+\mathbf{E}[b]\mathsf{i}&\mathbf{E}[c]+\mathbf{E}[d]\mathsf{i}\\
-\mathbf{E}[c]+\mathbf{E}[d]\mathsf{i}&\mathbf{E}[a]-\mathbf{E}[b]\mathsf{i}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathbf{E}[a+b\mathsf{i}]&\mathbf{E}[c+d\mathsf{i}]\\
\mathbf{E}[-c+d\mathsf{i}]&\mathbf{E}[a-b\mathsf{i}]\end{array}\right];

in other words,
\mathbf{E}[q]^{(h,l)}=\mathbf{E}[q^{(h,l)}]
for all
0\leq h,l\leq 1
.
Therefore, by a matrix product,

\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)\right]^{(h,l)}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m\in\mathcal{B}^{h,l}_{N(t)}\end{subarray}}\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)^{(m_{k-1},m_{k})}\right],\qquad 0\leq h,l\leq 1.

Since the entries of the matrices (
7.2.4
)
are complex-valued, we may now apply the classical statement of
Isserlis’ theorem (e.g.,
[
51
, (1.8)]
),
whereby

(7.53)

\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)\right]^{(h,l)}=\mathbf{1}_{\{N(t)\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{m\in\mathcal{B}^{h,l}_{N(t)}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(t)}}\Bigg\{\\
\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell_{1}})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell_{1}})\big)^{(m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}})}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell_{2}})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big)^{(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})}\right]\Bigg\}.

In order to calculate the above expectations we use the following:

Lemma 7.12
.

Let
\zeta,\eta>0
and
x,y\in I
be arbitrary.
On the one hand,

(7.54)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta}_{J_{\ell_{1}}}(x)^{(m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}})}\xi^{\eta}_{J_{\ell_{2}}}(y)^{(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})}\right]=0

whenever
J_{\ell_{1}}\not\in\{J_{\ell_{2}},J_{\ell_{2}}^{*}\}
.
On the other hand:

(1)

If
J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}
, then

\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta}_{J_{\ell_{1}}}(x)^{(m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}})}\xi^{\eta}_{J_{\ell_{2}}}(y)^{(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})}\right]\\
=\begin{cases}\frac{\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}(x-y)}{2}&\text{if $\big((m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}}),(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})\big)=\big((0,0),(1,1)\big)$ or $\big((1,1),(0,0)\big)$},\\
-\frac{\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}(x-y)}{2}&\text{if $\big((m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}}),(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})\big)=\big((0,1),(1,0)\big)$ or $\big((1,0),(0,1)\big)$},\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(2)

If
J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}^{*}
, then

\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta}_{J_{\ell_{1}}}(x)^{(m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}})}\xi^{\eta}_{J_{\ell_{2}}}(y)^{(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})}\right]=\frac{\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}(x-y)}{2}

if
\big((m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}}),(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})\big)
is equal to
\big((0,0),(0,0)\big)
,
\big((1,1),(1,1)\big)
,
\big((0,1),(1,0)\big)
, or
\big((1,0),(0,1)\big)
;
and

\mathbf{E}\left[\xi^{\zeta}_{J_{\ell_{1}}}(x)^{(m_{\ell_{1}-1},m_{\ell_{1}})}\xi^{\eta}_{J_{\ell_{2}}}(y)^{(m_{\ell_{2}-1},m_{\ell_{2}})}\right]=0

otherwise.

Referring back to (
7.1
),
in the case
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}

we can write

\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}(x)=\tfrac{1}{2}\left(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{j}}(x)\mathsf{j}+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}(x)\mathsf{k}\right),\qquad\zeta>0,~x\in I,

where
\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{l}}=(W_{i,j;\mathsf{l}}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta})^{\prime}
for
\mathsf{l}=1,\mathsf{i},\mathsf{j},\mathsf{k}
.
Therefore, for any
1\leq i<j\leq r
and
x\in I
,

\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}&\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{j}}(x)+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}(x)\mathsf{i}\\
-\big(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{j}}(x)-\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}(x)\mathsf{i}\big)&\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)-\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}\end{array}\right]

and

\xi^{\zeta}_{j,i}(x)=\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}(x)^{*}=\frac{1}{2}\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)-\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}&-\big(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{j}}(x)+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}(x)\mathsf{i}\big)\\
\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{j}}(x)-\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}(x)\mathsf{i}&\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}\end{array}\right].

With this in mind, (
7.54
) follows immediately
by independence.
As for the calculations stated as items (1) and (2) of this lemma, we use
a simple matrix product along with the remarks that

\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)\pm\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}\right)\left(\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;\mathsf{j}}(x)\pm\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}(x)\right)\right]=0

by independence, and

\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)\pm\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}\right)\left(\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;1}(x)\pm\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}\right)\right]\\
=\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{j}}(x)\pm\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}(x)\mathsf{i}\right)\left(\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;\mathsf{j}}(x)\pm\xi^{\eta}_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}(x)\mathsf{i}\right)\right]=0,

and

\tfrac{1}{4}\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}\right)\left(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;1}(x)-\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}(x)\mathsf{i}\right)\right]\\
=\tfrac{1}{4}\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{j}}(x)+\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}(x)\mathsf{i}\right)\left(\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{j}}(x)-\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j;\mathsf{k}}(x)\mathsf{i}\right)\right]=\frac{\varrho^{\zeta,\eta}(x-y)}{2}

by replicating the calculations in (
7.47
) and (
7.48
).
∎

We may now wrap up the proof of (
7.45
):
Firstly, we claim that

(7.55)

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)\right]^{(0,1)}=\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)\right]^{(1,0)}=0.

Indeed, by Lemma
7.12
, the only binary sequences
m\in\mathcal{B}_{N(t)}^{h,l}

that can contribute to (
7.53
) are those made up of
some permutation of pairs of steps in the following set:

\Big\{\big\{(0,0),(1,1)\big\},\big\{(0,1),(1,0)\big\},\big\{(0,0),(0,0)\big\},\big\{(1,1),(1,1)\big\}\Big\}.

In particular, there is only one type of pair that induces steps that go from
0
to
1
or vice versa—
namely,
\big\{(0,1),(1,0)\big\}
—and each such pair induces one
(0,1)
step and one
(1,0)
step.
Therefore, any
m\in\mathcal{B}_{N(t)}^{h,l}
that contributes to (
7.53
) must have
an equal number of
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
steps, meaning that
h=m_{0}=m_{N(t)}=l
.
Hence (
7.55
) holds.

Secondly, we note that, by (
7.2.4
),

\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)\right]^{(0,0)}=\left(\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)\right]^{(1,1)}\right)^{*}.

By combining (
7.53
) and Lemma
7.12
,
it is clear that the above entries have no complex part; hence we conclude that

\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)\right]=\mathbf{E}_{\xi}\left[\prod_{\ell=1}^{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}\xi^{\zeta(\tau_{\ell})}_{J_{\ell}}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell})\big)\right]^{(0,0)},

noting furthermore that the above is just a real number.
With this in hand,
we obtain (
7.45
)
in the case
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}

by combining
(
7.53
)
with the fact that the constant
\mathfrak{D}_{t}(p,U)
in
Definition
4.2
-(3)
is constructed by a direct application of Lemma
7.12
.
More specifically, the binary sequences that respect
(p,J)
are exactly
those that give a nonvanishing contribution to (
7.53
)
(as per the covariance calculations in Lemma
7.12
-(1) and -(2)),
and the flips correspond to the terms in Lemma
7.12
-(1)
that have a negative sign multiplying the covariance
-\frac{\varrho^{\zeta,\eta}(x-y)}{2}
.

7.3.
Proof of Proposition
7.6

Let
\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},\ldots,t_{n})\in(0,\infty)^{n}
be fixed.
According to (
3.6
) (for the case
\varepsilon,\zeta>0
)
and (
7.17
) (for the case
\varepsilon=0
and
\zeta>0
),

\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)=\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}}\big]

for all
t>0
,
\varepsilon\in[0,1)
and
\zeta\in(0,1)
. In particular, since the trace of
\mathrm{e}^{-t\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}}
is always positive, we can write

\mathbf{E}\left[\left|\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{k},\zeta_{k}}(t_{k};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right|^{2}\right]=\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{k},\zeta_{k}}(t_{k};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right)^{2}\right].

Therefore, up to relabelling the
\varepsilon_{k}
’s,
\zeta_{k}
’s and
t_{k}
’s to account for repeated indices in the squared
term above,
it suffices to prove the following:

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in[0,\infty)^{n},~\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in(0,\infty)^{n}}\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{k},\zeta_{k}}(t_{k};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right]<\infty.

By Proposition
7.5
, this means that it is enough to prove

(7.56)

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\\
\cdot\sup_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in[0,\infty)^{n},~\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in(0,\infty)^{n}}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{a})<\infty.

Recall that we use
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
\boldsymbol{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})
to denote the components
a_{k}=(i_{k},x_{k})
of
\boldsymbol{a}
.
Thus, if we combine (
7.30
), (
7.33
), and
(
7.37
) with the fact that
\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})=\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\Pi_{U}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i})
,
then (
7.56
)
reduces to

(7.57)

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\sup_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in(0,\infty)^{n}}\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\Pi_{U}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i})\mathbf{E}_{U}\left[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\right]\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}~\mathrm{d}s+\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\\
\cdot\exp\left(\frac{n\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}\right)\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{x})<\infty.

Thanks to (
4.2
), (
7.9
),
and the fact that
\overline{\varrho}
(and thus
\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}
for any choice of
\zeta,\eta>0
) is nonnegative, we have that

\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A)\leq\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A),

where

(7.58)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A)=\mathbf{1}_{\{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta(\tau_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(\tau_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-Z(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big),

Calculating the expectation of this quantity with respect to
U
(i.e., the jump process
N
) is easier without the endpoint conditionings on

U^{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
; for this purpose, note that

(7.59)

\Pi_{U}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i})\mathbf{E}_{U}\left[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\right]\leq\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\Pi_{U}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i})\mathbf{E}_{U}\left[\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(U^{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\right]\\
=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\mathbf{E}_{U}\left[\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathbf{1}_{\{\forall k:~U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)\to i_{k}\text{ as }s\to(t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})^{-}\}}\right]\leq\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\mathbf{E}_{U}\left[\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\right].

Once we remove the endpoint conditioning on
\boldsymbol{i}
, the jump times in
U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
are given by a Poisson process
with rate
(r-1)
. In particular, conditional on
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)=m
for some
m\in\mathbb{N}
, the times

\tau_{1}\leq\tau_{2}\leq\cdots\leq\tau_{m}
are the order statistics of
m
i.i.d. uniform variables on
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)
.
Thus, by applying the law of total probability to
(
7.58
) (conditioning on
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)=2m
and then on the
values of
\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{2m}
), we can directly calculate

(7.60)

\mathbf{E}_{U}\bigg[\overline{\mathfrak{m}}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\bigg]=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)_{<}^{2m}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}}\Bigg\{\cdots\\
\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)\Bigg\}\,\frac{(2m)!}{|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2m}}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\frac{\big((r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|\big)^{2m}}{(2m)!}\mathrm{e}^{-(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|},

where
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)_{<}^{2m}
denotes the simplex of components
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{2m})

in the interval
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}

such that
s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{2m}
(which has area
\frac{|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2m}}{(2m)!}
).
Since the function

\boldsymbol{s}\mapsto\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)

is symmetric on
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}
, we can extend the product of the integral in (
7.60
)
to the whole interval—up to removing the term
(2m)!
—whereby

(7.61)

\mathbf{E}_{U}\bigg[\overline{\mathfrak{m}}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\bigg]=\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}}\Bigg\{\cdots\\
\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)\Bigg\}\,\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2m}}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\cdot\frac{\big((r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|\big)^{2m}}{(2m)!}\mathrm{e}^{-(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}.

For any fixed
p\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}
, it follows from Young’s convolution inequality that

\displaystyle\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2}}\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{1})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{2})\big)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\int_{I^{2}}L^{y}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}(y-z)L^{z}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})~\mathrm{d}y\mathrm{d}z

(7.62)

\displaystyle\leq\upsilon^{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2m}.

With this in hand, if we recall
that
|\mathcal{P}_{2m}|=(2m-1)!!
and that
\frac{(2m-1)!!}{(2m)!}=\frac{1}{2^{m}m!}
, then we obtain from (
7.61
) that

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}\in(0,\infty)^{n}}\mathbf{E}_{U}\bigg[\overline{\mathfrak{m}}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\bigg]\leq\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{m!}\left(\frac{(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\right)^{m}\mathrm{e}^{-(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}/2-(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}

(7.63)

\displaystyle\leq\exp\left(\frac{n(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}-(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|\right),

where the last line follows from an application of Jensen’s inequality.
Therefore, if we combine (
7.63
) with (
7.59
),
then we get that (
7.57
)—and thus the proof of Proposition
7.6
—reduces to

(7.64)

\int_{I^{n}}\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|^{\overline{\mathfrak{a}}}~\mathrm{d}s+\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\\
\cdot\exp\left(\frac{n\sigma^{2}+n(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\|L_{[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}\right)\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}<\infty.

This follows from the same argument used to prove (
7.38
).

7.4.
Proof of Proposition
7.7

By Fubini’s theorem, we can write

(7.65)

\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(t;a,a)-\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right)^{2}\right]=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{E}\left[\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(t;a,a)\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(t;b,b)\right]\\
-2\mathbf{E}\left[\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(t;a,a)\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t;b,b)\right]+\mathbf{E}\left[\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t;a,a)\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t;b,b)\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(a,b).

Thus, in order to show that (
7.65
) vanishes
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, it suffices to prove the following:

\lim_{(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})\to(0,0)}\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\mathbf{E}\left[\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{1},\zeta}(t;a_{1},a_{1})\widehat{K}^{\varepsilon_{2},\zeta}(t;a_{2},a_{2})\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a})\\
=\int_{\mathcal{A}}\mathbf{E}\left[\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t;a_{1},a_{1})\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t;a_{2},a_{2})\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a}).

If we let
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2})
,
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=(\zeta,\zeta)
, and
\boldsymbol{t}=(t,t)
,
by Proposition
7.5
this translates to

(7.66)

\lim_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\to(0,0)}\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}\left[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a})\\
=\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}\left[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{s}^{(0,0)}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a}).

The only term that depends on
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}
above is

(7.67)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=\left\|L_{t}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{1}}+L_{[t,2t)}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{2}}\right\|_{\mu}^{2}.

Thus, the proof of (
7.66
) can be done
in three steps. That is, first we examine the almost-sure limit of (
7.67
),
and then ensure that this limit persists at the level of the expectation and the integral over
\mathcal{A}^{2}
:

7.4.1.
Step 1. Convergence Inside the Expectations

For any
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
,
one has
\|f\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}-f\|_{\mu}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
(e.g.,
[
7
, Lemma 2.2.2]
).
Since the local time process is almost-surely continuous and compactly supported (see Definition
4.4
),
this means by (
7.67
)
that
\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\to\mathfrak{s}^{(0,0)}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})
as
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\to(0,0)
almost surely; hence

(7.68)

\lim_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\to(0,0)}\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\\
=\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{s}^{(0,0)}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}

almost surely.

7.4.2.
Step 2. Convergence Inside the Integrals

We now aim to prove

(7.69)

\lim_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\to(0,0)}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]\\
=\mathbf{E}\left[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{s}^{(0,0)}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right].

By applying the dominated convergence theorem to (
7.68
),
it suffices to dominate the left-hand side of (
7.69
)
with an integrable random variable.
For this purpose, we invoke the fact that
V\geq-\nu
for some constant
\nu>0

(by Assumption
2.8
) together with (
7.30
),
(
7.37
), and (
7.58
) to get

\sup_{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\in[0,\infty)^{2}}\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\frac{1}{2}\mathfrak{s}^{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\\
\leq\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{(\nu+r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|+\sigma^{2}(\|L_{t}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}+\|L_{[t,2t)}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2})+\overline{\mathfrak{B}}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}.

In order to prove that the expectation of the dominating random variable on the second line of the above display is finite,
we first apply
(
7.59
) and (
7.63
) to
control the expectation of
\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})
with respect to
U
,
and then apply the same argument used to prove (
7.38
)
to deal with the remaining terms involving
Z
.

7.4.3.
Step 3. Convergence of the Integrals

Thanks to
(
7.69
) and
another application of the dominated convergence theorem,
the proof of (
7.66
)
(and therefore Proposition
7.7
)
is reduced to finding a
\mu^{2}
-integrable function that dominates
the integrands on the left-hand side of (
7.66
)
for all
\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2}\geq 0
.
This follows from (
7.56
).

7.5.
Proof of Proposition
7.8

By Fubini’s theorem, we can write the limit in (
7.14
) as follows:

(7.70)

\displaystyle\lim_{\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2}\to 0}\sum_{1\leq u,v\leq 2}(-1)^{u+v}\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta_{u}}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta_{v}}(t;a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right].

since (
7.13
) in the case
n=2

implies that the summands in (
7.70
) cancel out,
the proof of Proposition
7.8
is reduced to establishing (
7.13
).

By (
7.10
), we note that we can simplify

\mathfrak{s}^{(0,\ldots,0)}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A)\|_{\mu}^{2}.

Thus, by Proposition
7.5
, for every
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=(\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n})\in(0,\infty)^{n}
, we have

(7.71)

\mathbf{E}\left[\prod_{k=1}^{n}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta_{k}}(t_{k};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right]\\
=\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}\left[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{a}).

In order to recover the right-hand side of (
4.10
) from the limit
of this expression as
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\to 0
,
our aim is to write the right-hand side of (
7.71
)
as nested sums/integrals involving all the possible realizations of
U
(i.e., the jumps and
jump times). That said, the fact that we are conditioning on the endpoints of
U

in
A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
changes the distribution of
N
’s jump times.
In order to get around this difficulty and simplify the analysis, we
recall the following: If we use
\boldsymbol{x}=(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
and
\boldsymbol{i}=(i_{1},\ldots,i_{n})
to denote the components
a_{k}=(i_{k},x_{k})
of
\boldsymbol{a}
,
then we can factor
\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})=\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\Pi_{U}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i})
, and thus rewrite the right-hand side of
(
7.71
) by absorbing the conditioning into an indicator as follows:

(7.72)

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s),Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\\
\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{\forall k:~U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(t_{1}+\cdots+t_{k-1}+s)\to i_{k}\text{ as }s\to t_{k}^{-}\}}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{x}).

In order to understand the limiting behavior of this object as
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\to 0
, we now point out that drawing a realization of
U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
requires
the following steps:

\bullet

Sample
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)
according to a Poisson variable with parameter
(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|
:

\mathbf{P}\big[N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)=m\big]=\frac{\big((r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|\big)^{m}}{m!}\mathrm{e}^{-(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|},\qquad m=0,1,2,3,\ldots

\bullet

Given
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)=m
, sample
(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})
by ordering i.i.d. uniform
variables on
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)
. That is, using the symbol
\overrightarrow{s_{i}}
from
Notation
4.7
,

\mathbf{P}\big[(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{m})\in\mathrm{d}(\overrightarrow{s_{1}},\ldots,\overrightarrow{s_{m}})\big]=\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{t}|^{m}}\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s},\qquad\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{m})\in[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{m}.

\bullet

Given
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)=m
and
\tau_{i}=\overrightarrow{s_{i}}
for
\boldsymbol{s}=[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{m}
,
we let
\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,m,\boldsymbol{s})=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
denote the partition of

m
such that

(7.73)

\displaystyle z_{k}=\big|\big\{1\leq\ell\leq m:\overrightarrow{s_{\ell}}\in[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})\big\}\big|,\qquad t_{0}=0,~1\leq k\leq n.

That is,
z_{k}
is the number of jumps
\tau_{\ell}=\overrightarrow{s_{\ell}}
that land inside the time interval of the

k^{\mathrm{th}}
segment of the concatenated path
U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
.
Finally, let
\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,m,\boldsymbol{s})}
denote the set of all tuples of the form

(7.74)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{u}=(u^{(1)}_{0},\ldots,u^{(1)}_{z_{1}},u^{(2)}_{0},\ldots,u^{(2)}_{z_{2}},\ldots,u^{(n)}_{0},\ldots,u^{(n)}_{z_{n}}),

where for every
1\leq k\leq n
, the sequence

u^{(k)}_{0},\ldots,u^{(k)}_{z_{k}}

form a random walk path on the complete graph with vertices
\{1,\ldots,r\}

(without loops) with
z_{k}
jumps that starts at
u^{(k)}_{0}=i_{k}
.
We note that

|\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,m,\boldsymbol{s})}|=\prod_{k=1}^{n}(r-1)^{z_{k}}=(r-1)^{m},

and that given
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)=m
and
\tau_{i}=\overrightarrow{s_{i}}
, the jumps
(J_{k}:1\leq k\leq m)
in the concatenated path
U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}

are sampled uniformly at random within
\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,m,\boldsymbol{s})}
.

Based on all of this, given some
m\geq 0
,
\boldsymbol{s}\in[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{m}
,
and
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,m,\boldsymbol{s})}
, we let

\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}
denote the realization of
U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
with
m
jumps,
whose jump times are given by
\overrightarrow{s_{i}}
, and whose jumps are
given by
\boldsymbol{u}
. Moreover, to alleviate notation, we henceforth denote

(7.75)

F(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s),Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\\
\cdot\mathbf{1}_{\{\forall k:~U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(t_{1}+\cdots+t_{k-1}+s)\to i_{k}\text{ as }s\to t_{k}^{-}\}}.

By the law of total expectation
(conditioning with respect to
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)
,
\tau_{k}
, and
J_{k}
)
and the fact that
U
and
Z
are independent,
we may write (
7.72
) as follows:

(7.76)

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\Bigg(\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2m}}\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}}\frac{1}{(r-1)^{2m}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,\boldsymbol{s})}}\Bigg\{\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big)\\
\cdot F\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big)\Bigg\}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\Bigg)\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\big((r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|\big)^{2m}}{2m!}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{x});

we note that we have only included the case where
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)
is an even number
2m

in the above because of the indicator function in
\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
(i.e., (
7.9
)).

Given a fixed choice of
m
,
\boldsymbol{s}
,
\boldsymbol{u}
, and
\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}
in (
7.76
),
it follows from (
7.9
) that

\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z\big)=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}}\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\big(p,\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}\big)\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta(\overrightarrow{s_{\ell_{1}}}),\zeta(\overrightarrow{s_{\ell_{2}}})}\big(Z(\overrightarrow{s_{\ell_{1}}})-Z(\overrightarrow{s_{\ell_{2}}})\big).

As explained in (
1.24
)–(
1.27
), we aim to interpret
the limit of
\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}
as
\zeta,\eta\to 0
as the ”density function” of
Z
’s self-intersection
probability measures. That said, the fact that we have the ordered points
\overrightarrow{s_{\ell_{i}}}
in the above
display instead of
s_{\ell_{i}}
is problematic for this purpose. We thus introduce a change of variables
to circumvent this:
Given any permutation
\pi
of the set
\{1,\ldots,2m\}

and
p\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}
,
we let
\pi(p)\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}
denote the perfect matching such that

\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p
if and only if
\{\pi(\ell_{1}),\pi(\ell_{2})\}\in\pi(p)
.
Then, given
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{2m})
, we let
\pi_{\boldsymbol{s}}
denote the specific permutation
of
\{1,\ldots,2m\}
such that for any
1\leq\ell\leq 2m
, one has

\overrightarrow{s_{\pi_{\boldsymbol{s}}(\ell)}}=s_{\ell}
, or equivalently

\overrightarrow{s_{\ell}}=s_{\pi^{-1}_{\boldsymbol{s}}(\ell)}
.
Then, by the change of variables
q=\pi_{\boldsymbol{s}}^{-1}(p)
, we can rewrite

\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z\big)=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\sum_{q\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}}\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{s}}(q),\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}\big)\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z(s_{\ell_{2}})\big).

At this point, if we insert this expression into (
7.76
)
(and take this opportunity to cancel out both appearances of
|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2m}
and
\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}
therein,
as well as move
\upsilon^{2m}
in front of the constant
\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{s}}(q),\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}\big)
),
then we obtain the following expression for the mixed moment (
7.71
):

(7.77)

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\Bigg(\sum_{q\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}}\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q}\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)\\
\cdot\frac{1}{(r-1)^{2m}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,\boldsymbol{s})}}\upsilon^{2m}\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{s}}(q),\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}\big)F\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\Bigg)\frac{(r-1)^{2m}}{2m!}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{x}).

We are now in a position to establish the convergence of
\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}
to the self-intersection measures.
For this purpose, we begin with the following definition:

Definition 7.13
.

Let

\mathsf{L}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m,q,Z}=\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q}\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s},

and let
\mathrm{si}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m,q,Z}
be the random probability measure on
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}
with the density function

\boldsymbol{s}\mapsto\frac{1}{\mathsf{L}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m,q,Z}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q}\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z(s_{\ell_{2}})\big),\qquad\boldsymbol{s}\in[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}.

If we sample the random vector

\boldsymbol{T}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}=(T^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{1},\ldots,T^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{2m})

according to
\mathrm{si}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}
,
then we note that the
\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}
integral inside
(
7.77
) can be written as the expectation

(7.78)

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m,q,Z}\mathbf{E}_{\boldsymbol{T}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}\left[\frac{1}{(r-1)^{2m}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,\boldsymbol{T}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}})}}\upsilon^{2m}\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{T}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}}(q),\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{T}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\boldsymbol{u})}\big)F\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{T}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big)\right].

Our claim is that this expression converges to

(7.79)

\displaystyle\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2m}\mathbf{E}_{\boldsymbol{T}}\left[\frac{1}{(r-1)^{2m}}\sum_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,\boldsymbol{T})}}\upsilon^{2m}\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{T}}(q),\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{T},\boldsymbol{u})}\big)F\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{T},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big)\right],

where
\boldsymbol{T}=(T_{1},\ldots,T_{2m})

is now sampled according to
\mathrm{si}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}

(recall the definition of the latter in (
4.6
)).
If this can be established, then we get that the limit of (
7.77
)
as
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\to 0
can be written as follows (in order to obtain the expression below,
we also use the facts that
|\mathcal{P}_{2m}|=(2m-1)!!
and the simplification
\frac{(2m-1)!!}{(2m)!}=\frac{1}{2^{m}m!}
):

(7.80)

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{n}}\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\Bigg(\frac{1}{|\mathcal{P}_{2m}|}\sum_{q\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}}\mathbf{E}_{\boldsymbol{T}}\Bigg[\frac{1}{(r-1)^{2m}}\\
\cdot\sum_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,\boldsymbol{T})}}\upsilon^{2m}\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{T}}(q),\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{T},\boldsymbol{u})}\big)F\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{T},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big)\Bigg]\Bigg)\\
\cdot\left(\frac{(r-1)^{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\right)^{m}\frac{1}{m!}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{x}).

Once established, this convergence concludes the proof of (
7.13
).
Indeed, we now recognize that in (
7.80
), the sums over
m

and
q\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}
correspond to sampling
\widehat{N}(|\boldsymbol{t}|)
and
\widehat{q}
as described in Definition
4.8
,
up to introducing a renormalization due to

\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\left(\frac{(r-1)^{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\right)^{m}\frac{1}{m!}=\mathrm{e}^{\frac{(r-1)^{2}}{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z)\|_{2}^{2}},

which is necessary in order to turn
m\mapsto\left(\frac{(r-1)^{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\right)^{m}\frac{1}{m!}
into a probability measure
(this renormalization also explains the presence of
\frac{(r-1)^{2}}{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z)\|_{2}^{2}
in
(
4.9
)).
This then also implies that

\boldsymbol{T}
is sampled according to
\mathrm{si}_{t,\widehat{N}(t),\widehat{q},Z}
,
that
\widehat{p}
can be taken as
\pi_{\boldsymbol{T}}(\widehat{q})
with
\widehat{q}
sampled uniformly at random in
\mathcal{P}_{\widehat{N}(t)}
,
and that
\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{T},\boldsymbol{u})}
generates a realization of
\widehat{U}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
, all of which are consistent with Definition
4.8
.

Thus, in summary, the proof of Proposition
7.8
will be complete if we
establish that (
7.78
) converges to (
7.79
)
almost surely.
For this purpose, we use the following:

Lemma 7.14
.

Let
\boldsymbol{x}\in I^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{t}\in[0,\infty)^{n}
be fixed.
Almost surely,

(7.81)

\displaystyle\lim_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}\to 0}\mathsf{L}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}=\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2m}

and

\lim_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}\to 0}\mathrm{si}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}=\mathrm{si}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}\qquad\text{weakly}

for every
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
q\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}
.

Lemma 7.15
.

Let
\boldsymbol{x}\in I^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{t}\in[0,\infty)^{n}
be fixed. Define the set

\mathrm{B}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m}=\big\{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{2m})\in[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}:s_{\ell}=t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k}\\
\text{ for at least one pair }1\leq\ell\leq 2m,~1\leq k\leq n-1\big\}.

Almost surely,
\mathrm{si}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}\big(\mathrm{B}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m}\big)=0
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
q\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}
.

Lemma 7.16
.

Let
\boldsymbol{x}\in I^{n}
,
\boldsymbol{t}\in[0,\infty)^{n}
be fixed.
Almost surely, the function

\boldsymbol{s}\mapsto\sum_{\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,\boldsymbol{s})}}\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{s}}(q),\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}\big)F\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big)

is bounded and continuous on
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}\setminus\mathrm{B}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m}

for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
and
q\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}
.

With these three lemmas in hand, the convergence of
(
7.78
) to (
7.79
)
is a consequence of the following: If probability measures
(P_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converge weakly to

P
as
n\to\infty
and
h
is a bounded function whose discontinuities have probability zero
with respect to
P
,
then
\mathbf{E}_{P_{n}}[h]\to\mathbf{E}_{P}[h]

(this can be established by combining the continuous mapping theorem
[
3
, Theorem 2.7]
,
the Skorokhod representation theorem
[
3
, Theorem 6.7]
, and the dominated convergence theorem).
We now finish this section with the proofs of Lemmas
7.14
,

7.15
, and
7.16
:

We begin with (
7.81
).
The analysis of the expression
