\mathsf{L}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m,q,Z}=\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q}\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}

is made difficult by the fact that the parameters
\zeta(s)
change with
s
. In order to get around this,
for any
1\leq k_{1},\ldots,k_{2m}\leq n
, we denote

\mathcal{K}_{k_{\ell}}=\big[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k_{\ell}-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k_{\ell}}\big),\qquad\mathcal{C}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{2m}}=\mathcal{K}_{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{K}_{k_{2m}},

with the convention
t_{0}=0
,
and we write

[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}=\bigcup_{1\leq k_{1},\ldots,k_{2m}\leq n}\mathcal{C}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{2m}},

noting that the union is disjoint. With this convention, if
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{2m})\in\mathcal{C}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{2m}}
,
then we have that
\zeta(s_{\ell})=\zeta_{k_{\ell}}
for all
1\leq\ell\leq 2m
thanks to (
7.8
).
Therefore, by definition of local time in (
4.3
), we can write

\displaystyle\mathsf{L}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m,q,Z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq k_{1},\ldots,k_{2m}\leq n}\int_{\mathcal{C}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{2m}}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q}\varrho_{\zeta_{k_{\ell_{1}}}\zeta_{k_{\ell_{2}}}}\big(Z(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}

(7.82)

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq k_{1},\ldots,k_{2m}\leq n}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q}\int_{I^{2}}L_{\mathcal{K}_{k_{\ell_{1}}}}^{x}(Z)\varrho_{\zeta_{k_{\ell_{1}}}\zeta_{k_{\ell_{2}}}}(x-y)L_{\mathcal{K}_{k_{\ell_{1}}}}^{y}(Z)~\mathrm{d}x\mathrm{d}y.

Recall that
\varrho_{\zeta,\eta}=\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\eta}
is a convolution of mollifiers
(Definition
7.2
). Thus,
on the probability-one event where the local time is continuous (and thus, in particular,
square-integrable, see Definition
4.4
),
(
7.82
) converges to

(7.83)

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leq k_{1},\ldots,k_{2m}\leq n}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q}\left\langle L_{\mathcal{K}_{k_{\ell_{1}}}}(Z),L_{\mathcal{K}_{k_{\ell_{2}}}}(Z)\right\rangle

as
\boldsymbol{\zeta}\to 0
, since convolution against a mollifier is an approximate identity
with respect to any
L^{p}
norm (e.g.,
[
7
, Lemma 2.2.2]
).
We then obtain (
7.81
)
by noting that if we compute the sum in (
7.83
) one index

k_{\ell}
at a time (i.e., keeping all other indices fixed), then the bilinearity of the inner product yields that
(
7.83
) is equal to

\left\langle\sum_{k=1}^{2m}L_{\mathcal{K}_{k}}(Z),\sum_{k=1}^{2m}L_{\mathcal{K}_{k}}(Z)\right\rangle^{m}=\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z)\|_{2}^{2m},

as desired.

We now prove the weak convergence statement.
Since measurable rectangles are a convergence-determining class
for weak convergence (e.g.,
[
3
, Page 18, Example 2.3]
),
it suffices to prove that, almost surely,

\lim_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}\to 0}\mathrm{si}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}\big([0,u_{1})\times\cdots\times[0,u_{2m})\big)=\mathrm{si}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}\big([0,u_{1})\times\cdots\times[0,u_{2m})\big)

for all
0\leq u_{k}\leq|\boldsymbol{t}|
,
1\leq k\leq 2m
.
By definition of
\mathrm{si}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}
and

\mathrm{si}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}
(i.e., (
4.6
))
and the limit (
7.81
),
this reduces to showing that, almost surely,

(7.84)

\lim_{\boldsymbol{\zeta}\to 0}\int_{[0,u_{1})\times\cdots\times[0,u_{n})}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q}\varrho_{\zeta(s_{\ell_{1}}),\zeta(s_{\ell_{2}})}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\\
=\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in q}\left\langle L_{u_{\ell_{1}}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}),L_{u_{\ell_{2}}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\right\rangle

for all
0\leq u_{k}\leq|\boldsymbol{t}|
,
1\leq k\leq 2m
.
The proof of this fact is exactly the same
as that of (
7.81
),
except that we replace the intervals
\mathcal{K}_{k}
and rectangles
\mathcal{C}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{2m}}
therein by

\mathcal{K}^{(u)}_{k_{\ell}}=\big[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k_{\ell}-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k_{\ell}}\big)\cap[0,u_{\ell}),\qquad\mathcal{C}^{(u)}_{k_{1},\ldots,k_{2m}}=\mathcal{K}^{(u)}_{k_{1}}\times\cdots\times\mathcal{K}^{(u)}_{k_{2m}}.

(The fact that we consider uncountably many choices of
u_{k}
’s in (
7.84
)
is not a problem for the almost-sure convergence; the limit
(
7.81
) holds on the event where the local time
process is continuous, which is independent of
\boldsymbol{t}
or the choices of
u_{k}
.)
∎

Given
1\leq\ell\leq 2m
and
t\in[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)
, let us denote

\mathrm{B}^{0}_{\ell,t}=\big\{(s_{1},\ldots,s_{2m})\in[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}:s_{\ell}=t\big\}=[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{\ell-1}\times\{t\}\times[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m-\ell}.

Then, we have that

\mathrm{B}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m}\subset\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq\ell\leq 2m\\
1\leq k\leq n-1\end{subarray}}\mathrm{B}^{0}_{\ell,t_{1}+\cdots+t_{k}}.

Thus, by a union bound, it suffices to show that for any choice of
1\leq\ell\leq 2m
and
t\in[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)
,
one has

\mathrm{si}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}\big(\mathrm{B}^{0}_{\ell,t}\big)=0

almost surely.
By (
4.6
) and continuity from above of probability measures, one has

\mathrm{si}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,q,Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}\big(\mathrm{B}^{0}_{\ell,t}\big)=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\|L_{t}(Z)\|_{2}^{2}}\left\langle L_{[t,t+\varepsilon)}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}),L_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\right\rangle.

This converges to zero
on the probability-one event where
Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
’s local time is continuous:
(
4.3
) implies that
L_{[t,t+\varepsilon)}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\to 0
pointwise,
and we then get the result by dominated convergence.
∎

Referring back to (
7.74
), we remark that the set-valued function
\boldsymbol{s}\mapsto\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,\boldsymbol{s})}
remains constant
so long as we do not change the number of components of
\boldsymbol{s}
that are inside each sub-interval of
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)
the form

[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k}).

In other words,
\boldsymbol{s}\mapsto\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,\boldsymbol{s})}
is constant inside each connected component of the set
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}\setminus\mathrm{B}_{\boldsymbol{t},2m}
.
Thus, if we let
\mathcal{K}
denote one such connected component and we restrict to
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathcal{K}
,
it suffices to show that the map

\boldsymbol{s}\mapsto\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{s}}(q),\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}\big)F\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big),\qquad\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathcal{K}

is almost-surely continuous and bounded on
\mathcal{K}
for any fixed
\boldsymbol{u}\in\mathcal{W}^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{z}(|\boldsymbol{t}|,2m,\boldsymbol{s})}
.

Once we fix the number of coordinates in
\boldsymbol{s}
that are in each sub-interval of
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)

and the random walk path
\boldsymbol{u}
, the quantity
\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\big(\pi_{\boldsymbol{s}}(q),\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}\big)

becomes constant (this is because, by Definition
4.2
,
\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)
actually
only depends on the jumps
J_{k}
and the matching
p
; it does not depend on the jump times
\tau_{k}
).
Thus, we only need to verify the almost-sure continuity and boundedness of
F\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}\big)

on
\mathcal{K}
.

If we examine the definition of
F
in (
7.75
), then we note that the indicator
function that fixes the endpoints of
\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}
does not depend on
\boldsymbol{s}
once
\boldsymbol{u}
is fixed.
Thus, we need only verify the almost-sure continuity and boundedness of

\boldsymbol{s}\mapsto\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}(s),Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}

on
\mathcal{K}
.
That this is bounded is easily obtained from the assumption that
V
is bounded below (Assumption
2.8
),
as well as the fact that we can obtain upper bounds for
\mathfrak{s}^{(0,\ldots,0)}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A)=\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A)\|_{\mu}^{2}

and
\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A)
that only depend on
Z

(e.g., (
7.30
) and (
7.37
)). As for the continuity,
recall the definitions of
z_{k}
and
u^{(k)}_{\ell}
(with
1\leq k\leq n
and
0\leq\ell\leq z_{k}
) in
(
7.73
) and (
7.74
), and let us henceforth
define

\mathcal{S}^{(k)}_{\ell}\subset[t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k-1},t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})

as the interval of time that corresponds to the step
u^{(k)}_{\ell}

in the continuous-time path
\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}
, in the sense that

\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=0}^{z_{k}}u^{(k)}_{\ell}\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{S}^{(k)}_{\ell}};

for instance, for
k=1
we have

\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{0}=\big[0,\overrightarrow{s_{1}}\big),~\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{1}=\big[\overrightarrow{s_{1}},\overrightarrow{s_{2}}\big),\ldots,\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{z_{1}-1}=\big[\overrightarrow{s_{z_{1}-1}},\overrightarrow{s_{z_{1}}}\big),~\mathcal{S}^{(1)}_{z_{1}}=\big[\overrightarrow{s_{z_{1}}},t_{1}\big),

and so on for
k\geq 2
.
Then, by (
3.2
) and (
4.4
), for any
(i,x)\in\mathcal{A}
one has

(7.85)

\displaystyle L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}^{(i,x)}(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell\leq z_{k}:~u^{(k)}_{\ell}=i}L_{\mathcal{S}^{(k)}_{\ell}}^{x}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}),

and for any
c\in\partial I
(in the two cases where
I
has a boundary), one has

(7.86)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{L}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}^{(i,c)}(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell\leq z_{k}:~u^{(k)}_{\ell}=i}\mathfrak{L}^{c}_{\mathcal{S}^{(k)}_{\ell}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}).

Given that we chose versions of
the local time processes
L^{x}_{[u,v)}(Z)
and
\mathcal{L}^{c}_{[u,v)}(Z)
that are jointly continuous
with respect to
u,v
, and
x
in Definitions
3.2
and
4.4
, this implies that
the functionals (
3.2
) and (
4.4
)
are continuous with respect to
\boldsymbol{s}\in\mathcal{K}
with probability one,
since changing
\boldsymbol{s}
’s components only changes the lengths of the intervals
\mathcal{S}^{(k)}_{\ell}
.
Since
x\mapsto L^{x}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})
is almost-surely compactly supported, the continuity of
\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}
and

\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})
follow immediately from this
(recall the definition of the latter in (
3.3
)). As for the integral involving
V
, we note that its continuity
follows from that of (
7.85
) by writing

\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V\big(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})}(s),Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s=\int_{\mathcal{A}}V(a)L^{a}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(\mathcal{U}_{(\boldsymbol{s},\boldsymbol{u})},Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})~\mathrm{d}\mu(a),

noting once again that the above integral can be restricted to a compact set.
This completes the proof of Lemma
7.16
, and thus also of Proposition
7.8
.
∎

7.6.
A Scalar Real-Valued Reduction for Propositions
2.12
,

7.9
, and
7.10

Propositions
2.12
,

7.9
, and
7.10
can all be reduced to similar results from
[
28
]
in the scalar setting with
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
.
This is because, by linearity/bilinearity, the form
\xi^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(f,g)

evaluated in any functions
f,g:\mathcal{A}\to\mathbb{F}
can be written as a linear combination
of white noises in
\mathbb{R}
evaluated in functions from
I
to
\mathbb{R}
. In this section,
we make this observation more precise.

To see this, we first recall that we can write

(7.87)

\displaystyle\xi^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(f,g)=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\xi^{\varepsilon}_{i,i}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(i,\cdot)\big)+\sum_{i\neq j}\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(j,\cdot)\big),\qquad\varepsilon,\zeta\geq 0;

moreover, letting
(W_{i,j})_{i\leq i<j\leq r}
be the Brownian motions introduced in Definition
7.2
,
we have that

(7.88)

\displaystyle\xi^{\varepsilon}_{i,i}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(i,\cdot)\big)=\sigma(W_{i,i}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})^{\prime}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(i,\cdot)\big)\qquad\text{for all }1\leq i\leq r,

and

(7.89)

\displaystyle\xi^{\zeta}_{i,j}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(j,\cdot)\big)=\upsilon(W_{i,j}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta})^{\prime}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(j,\cdot)\big)\qquad\text{for all }1\leq i<j\leq r.

We note that
W_{i,i}
are already standard Brownian motions in
\mathbb{R}
; for
1\leq i<j\leq r
, we recall from Definition
7.2
that we can expand (
7.89
) as

(7.90)

(W_{i,j}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta})^{\prime}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(j,\cdot)\big)\\
=\begin{cases}(W_{i,j;1}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta})^{\prime}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(j,\cdot)\big)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\Big((W_{i,j;1}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta})^{\prime}\big(f(i,\cdot),g(j,\cdot)\big)+(W_{i,j;\mathsf{i}}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta})^{\prime}\big(f(i,\cdot),\mathsf{i}g(j,\cdot)\big)\Big)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C},\\
\frac{1}{2}\Big(\sum_{\ell\in\{1,\mathsf{i},\mathsf{j},\mathsf{k}\}}(W_{i,j;\ell}\star\overline{\varrho}_{\zeta})^{\prime}\big(f(i,\cdot),\ell g(j,\cdot)\big)\Big)&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H},\end{cases}

where
W_{i,j;\ell}
are i.i.d. standard Brownian motions in
\mathbb{R}
.

(
7.88
) and (
7.90
)
show that
\xi^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(f,g)
can always be written as a linear combination of white noises in
\mathbb{R}

evaluated in scalar-valued functions from
I
to
\mathbb{F}
. We now finish this introductory section by showing
that we need only consider functions from
I
to
\mathbb{R}
.
When
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
, this observation
is of course entirely vacuous, as
f(i,x),g(j,x)\in\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in I
.
Otherwise,
pick an arbitrary basis element

\ell\in\begin{cases}\{1,\mathsf{i}\}&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C},\\
\{1,\mathsf{i},\mathsf{j},\mathsf{k}\}&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H};\end{cases}

define
h=\ell g
; and finally expand the components of
f
as

f(i,x)=\begin{cases}f_{1}(i,x)+f_{2}(i,x)\mathsf{i}&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}\\
f_{1}(i,x)+f_{2}(i,x)\mathsf{i}+f_{3}(i,x)\mathsf{j}+f_{4}(i,x)\mathsf{k}&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}\end{cases}

and similarly for
\big(h_{m}(i,\cdot)\big)_{m\geq 1}
.
To simplify the presentation, we use the notational shorthand
\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}=(W\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})^{\prime}
. Then,
by bilinearity and the complex product, one has

(7.91)

\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f(i,\cdot),h(j,\cdot)\big)=\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{1}(i,\cdot),h_{1}(j,\cdot)\big)-\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{2}(i,\cdot),h_{2}(j,\cdot)\big)\\
+\Big(\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{1}(i,\cdot),h_{2}(j,\cdot)\big)+\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{2}(i,\cdot),h_{1}(j,\cdot)\big)\Big)\mathsf{i}.

when
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}
,
and by bilinearity and the definition of quaternion product,

(7.92)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f(i,\cdot),h(j,\cdot)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{1}(i,\cdot),h_{1}(j,\cdot)\big)-\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{2}(i,\cdot),h_{2}(j,\cdot)\big)-\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{3}(i,\cdot),h_{3}(j,\cdot)\big)-\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{2}(i,\cdot),h_{2}(j,\cdot)\big)

\displaystyle+\Big(\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{1}(i,\cdot),h_{2}(j,\cdot)\big)+\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{2}(i,\cdot),h_{1}(j,\cdot)\big)+\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{3}(i,\cdot),h_{4}(j,\cdot)\big)-\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{4}(i,\cdot),h_{3}(j,\cdot)\big)\Big)\mathsf{i}

\displaystyle+\Big(\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{1}(i,\cdot),h_{3}(j,\cdot)\big)-\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{2}(i,\cdot),h_{4}(j,\cdot)\big)+\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{3}(i,\cdot),h_{1}(j,\cdot)\big)+\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{4}(i,\cdot),h_{2}(j,\cdot)\big)\big)\mathsf{j}

\displaystyle+\Big(\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{1}(i,\cdot),h_{4}(j,\cdot)\big)+\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{2}(i,\cdot),h_{3}(j,\cdot)\big)-\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{3}(i,\cdot),h_{2}(j,\cdot)\big)+\mathcal{N}_{\varepsilon}\big(f_{4}(i,\cdot),h_{1}(j,\cdot)\big)\Big)\mathsf{k}

when
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}
,
noting that
f_{m}(i,x),h_{m}(j,x)\in\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in I
and
m\geq 1
.
We are now ready to embark on the proofs of
Propositions
2.12
,

7.9
, and
7.10
.

7.7.
Proof of Propositions
2.12
and
7.9

7.7.1.
Proof of Proposition
2.12
Part 1: (
2.8
)
and Equivalence

For every
1\leq i\leq r
, let us denote the norm

(7.93)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\star;i}^{2}=\|f^{\prime}\|_{2}^{2}+\|f\|_{2}^{2}+\|V_{+}(i,\cdot)^{1/2}f\|_{2}^{2},\qquad f\in L^{2}(I,\mathbb{F}).

By
[
28
, Proposition 3.2]
, there exists constants
C_{i}>0
large
enough so that

\|f\|_{+1;i}=\sqrt{\mathcal{E}_{i}(f,f)+(C_{i}+1)\|f\|_{2}}

is a norm on
D(\mathcal{E}_{i})
, which is equivalent to
\|f\|_{\star;i}^{2}

(in
[
28
, Proposition 3.2]
, this result is only stated
for real-valued functions, but it can be trivially extended to
\mathbb{C}
and
\mathbb{H}

by linearity and noting that
L^{2}(I,\mathbb{C})\cong L^{2}(I,\mathbb{R})^{\oplus 2}
and
L^{2}(I,\mathbb{H})\cong L^{2}(I,\mathbb{R})^{\oplus 4}
, the latter with
\prec\cdot,\cdot\succ
).
We then obtain the claim regarding (
2.8
) in Proposition
2.12

by noting that, for
f\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
,
if we let
C=C_{1}+\cdots+C_{i}
, then

(7.94)

\displaystyle\|f\|_{\star}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|f(i,\cdot)\|_{\star;i}^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|f\|_{+1}^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\|f(i,\cdot)\|_{+1;i}^{2}.

7.7.2.
Proof of Proposition
2.12
Part 2: (
2.9
)

Suppose first that
\xi_{i,j}
are white noises for all
1\leq i,j\leq r
.
By equivalence of

\|\cdot\|_{+1}
and
\|\cdot\|_{\star}
, it is enough to show that almost surely,
for every
\kappa\in(0,1)
, there exists
\nu>0

such that
|\xi(f,f)|\leq\kappa\|f\|^{2}_{\star}+\nu\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}
for every
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
.
Toward this end, by
(
7.88
)–(
7.90
)
(with
\varepsilon=\zeta=0
)
and the triangle inequality,
it suffices to show the following: Let
W
be a standard Brownian motion in
\mathbb{R}
.
For any choice of
\kappa\in(0,1)
and

\ell\in\begin{cases}\{1\}&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}\\
\{1,\mathsf{i}\}&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}\\
\{1,\mathsf{i},\mathsf{j},\mathsf{k}\}&\text{if }\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H},\end{cases}

there exists
\nu>0
such that

(7.95)

\displaystyle\big|W^{\prime}\big(f(i,\cdot),\ell f(j,\cdot)\big)\big|\leq\kappa\|f\|_{\star}^{2}+\nu\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}

for every
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
and
1\leq i,j\leq r
.

Suppose first that we are in Case 1 or Case 2.
Consider the averaged process

\tilde{W}(x)=\int_{x}^{x+1}W(x)~\mathrm{d}x,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},

and write
W=\tilde{W}+(W-\tilde{W})
. Then, by definition of
W^{\prime}

in (
2.4
)
and an integration by parts, we have that

W^{\prime}\big(f(i,\cdot),\ell f(j,\cdot)\big)=f(i,0)^{*}\tilde{W}(0)\mathsf{\ell}f(j,0)+\big\langle f(i,\cdot),\tilde{W}^{\prime}\ell f(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\\
+\big\langle f^{\prime}(i,\cdot),(\tilde{W}-W)\ell f(j,\cdot)\big\rangle+\big\langle f(i,\cdot),(\tilde{W}-W)\ell f^{\prime}(j,\cdot)\big\rangle.

By
[
28
, Lemma 3.1]
, for every
\kappa^{\prime}>0
, there exists some
\nu^{\prime}>0

such that

(7.96)

\displaystyle|f(i,x)|^{2}\leq\kappa^{\prime}\|f^{\prime}(i,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}+\nu^{\prime}\|f(i,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}\qquad\text{for every }x\in I.

Thus, since
\tilde{W}(0)
is finite and
xy\leq x^{2}+y^{2}
for
x,y\geq 0
, the latter of which implies

(7.97)

\displaystyle|f(i,0)^{*}\tilde{W}(0)\ell f(j,0)|\leq|f(i,0)|^{2}|\tilde{W}(0)|+|f(j,0)|^{2}|\tilde{W}(0)|,

in order to prove (
7.95
),
it suffices to show that almost surely, for every
\kappa\in(0,1)
, there exists
\nu>0
such that,

(7.98)

\Big|\big\langle f(i,\cdot),\tilde{W}^{\prime}\ell f(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|+\Big|\big\langle f^{\prime}(i,\cdot),(\tilde{W}-W)\ell f(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|+\Big|\big\langle f(i,\cdot),(\tilde{W}-W)\ell f^{\prime}(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\\
\leq\kappa\|f\|_{\star}^{2}+\nu\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}.

For this purpose,
by combining Jensen’s inequality with the fact that
xy\leq\frac{\theta}{2}x^{2}+\frac{1}{2\theta}y^{2}
for any
x,y\geq 0
and
\theta>0
,

(7.99)

\Big|\big\langle f(i,\cdot),\tilde{W}^{\prime}\ell f(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\leq\int_{I}|f(i,x)||\tilde{W}^{\prime}(x)|^{1/2}|f(j,x)||\tilde{W}^{\prime}(x)|^{1/2}~\mathrm{d}x\\
\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}|f(i,x)|^{2}|\tilde{W}^{\prime}(x)|~\mathrm{d}x+\frac{1}{2}\int_{I}|f(j,x)|^{2}|\tilde{W}^{\prime}(x)|~\mathrm{d}x;

(7.100)

\Big|\big\langle f^{\prime}(i,\cdot),(\tilde{W}-W)\ell f(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\leq\int_{I}|f^{\prime}(i,x)||W(x)-\tilde{W}(x)||f(j,x)|~\mathrm{d}x\\
\leq\frac{\theta}{2}\|f^{\prime}(i,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\theta}\int_{I}|f(j,x)|^{2}\big(W(x)-\tilde{W}(x)\big)^{2}~\mathrm{d}x;

and similarly,

(7.101)

\displaystyle\Big|\big\langle f^{\prime}(i,\cdot),(\tilde{W}-W)\ell f(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\leq\frac{\theta}{2}\|f^{\prime}(j,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{1}{2\theta}\int_{I}|f(i,x)|^{2}\big(W(x)-\tilde{W}(x)\big)^{2}~\mathrm{d}x.

By
[
28
, (4.2)]
, there exists a finite random variable
C>0
such that,
almost surely,

|\tilde{W}^{\prime}(x)|,\big(W(x)-\tilde{W}(x)\big)^{2}\leq C\log(2+|x|)

for all
x\in I
. In particular, by the growth lower bound in Assumption
2.8
,
this means that almost surely, for any
\theta>0
, there exists a
\nu_{\theta}>0
such that for every
1\leq i\leq r
,

|\tilde{W}^{\prime}(x)|,\big(W(x)-\tilde{W}(x)\big)^{2}\leq\theta^{2}V(i,x)_{+}+\nu_{\theta}

for all
x\in I
. If we plug this back into (
7.99
)–(
7.101
),
then we get

(7.102)

\Big|\big\langle f(i,\cdot),\tilde{W}^{\prime}\ell f(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\\
\leq\tfrac{\theta^{2}}{2}\big(\|f(i,\cdot)V(i,\cdot)_{+}^{1/2}\|_{2}^{2}+\|f(j,\cdot)V(j,\cdot)_{+}^{1/2}\|_{2}^{2}\big)+\tfrac{\nu_{\theta}}{2}\big(\|f(i,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}+\|f(j,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}\big);

(7.103)

\displaystyle\Big|\big\langle f(i,\cdot),\tilde{W}^{\prime}\ell f(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\leq\tfrac{\theta}{2}\|f^{\prime}(i,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}+\tfrac{\theta}{2}\|f(j,\cdot)V(j,\cdot)_{+}^{1/2}\|_{2}^{2}+\tfrac{\nu_{\theta}}{2\theta}\|f(j,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2};

and similarly

(7.104)

\displaystyle\Big|\big\langle f^{\prime}(i,\cdot),(\tilde{W}-W)\ell f(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\Big|\leq\tfrac{\theta}{2}\|f^{\prime}(j,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}+\tfrac{\theta}{2}\|f(i,\cdot)V(i,\cdot)_{+}^{1/2}\|_{2}^{2}+\tfrac{\nu_{\theta}}{2\theta}\|f(i,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}

Given that
\theta>0
above can be taken arbitrarily small, and that

\|f^{\prime}(i,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2},\|f(i,\cdot)V(i,\cdot)_{+}^{1/2}\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|f\|_{\star}^{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\|f(i,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}\leq\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}

for all
1\leq i\leq r
, we obtain (
7.98
)
from (
7.102
)–(
7.104
).

We now finish the proof of (
2.9
)—and thus of Proposition
2.12
in the case of white noises—by establishing
(
7.95
) in Case 3. In that case, by definition of
W^{\prime}
, we can write

\big|W^{\prime}\big(f(i,\cdot),\ell f(j,\cdot)\big)\big|\leq|f(i,\vartheta)||f(j,\vartheta)||W(\vartheta)|+\big|\big\langle f^{\prime}(i,\cdot),Wf(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|+\big|\big\langle f(i,\cdot),Wf^{\prime}(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|.

With this in hand, we obtain (
2.9
) by controlling
|f(i,\vartheta)||f(j,\vartheta)||W(\vartheta)|
using (
7.96
)
and (
7.97
), and then noting that

(7.105)

\big|\big\langle f^{\prime}(i,\cdot),W\ell f(j,\cdot)\big\rangle\big|\leq\sup_{y\in[0,\vartheta]}|W(y)|\int_{I}|f^{\prime}(i,x)||f(j,x)|~\mathrm{d}x\\
\leq\sup_{y\in[0,\vartheta]}|W(y)|\big(\tfrac{\theta}{2}\|f^{\prime}(i,\cdot)\|_{2}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2\theta}\|f(j,x)\|_{2}^{2}\big)

for any choice of
\theta>0
(a similar bound holds for
\langle f(i,\cdot),W\ell f^{\prime}(j,\cdot)\rangle
).

As a final remark, we note that exactly the same argument applies if
\xi
contain
regular noises, as per Definition
2.6
. For this,
we simply replace
W
in the above argument by the integral
\Xi(x)=\int_{0}^{x}\mathcal{X}(x)~\mathrm{d}x
,
where
\Xi
is one of the regular noises used to define any of the
\xi_{i,j}
.
This case is also covered by
[
28
, (4.2)]

(in the terminology of
[
28
]
, the case of a regular noise
is called
bounded
, which comes from the fact that the noise’s covariance
function is in
L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
; see
[
28
, Example 2.28]
).
Thus, the proof of Proposition
2.12
is now complete.

7.7.3.
Proof of Proposition
7.9

We now conclude this subsection by proving Proposition
7.9
.
The reason why we prove this in the same subsection as Proposition
2.12

is that its proof is very similar to (
2.9
). Indeed, by the variational characterization
of the eigenvalues in (
2.10
) and the equivalence of
\|\cdot\|_{\star}
with
the norm induced by
H
’s quadratic form in (
2.8
), the statement of Proposition

7.9
can be reduced to the following:
Almost surely, for every
\kappa\in(0,1)
, there exists some
\nu>0
such that for every

\varepsilon,\zeta\in[0,1)
and
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
, one has

(7.106)

\displaystyle|\xi^{\varepsilon,\zeta}(f,f)|\leq\kappa\|f\|^{2}_{\star}+\nu\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}.

In similar fashion to the reduction we obtained in (
7.95
),
by
(
7.88
)–(
7.90
),
this latter claim can be reduced to showing that

(7.107)

\displaystyle\big|(W\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})^{\prime}\big(f(i,\cdot),\ell f(j,\cdot)\big)\big|\leq\kappa\|f\|_{\star}^{2}+\nu\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}

for every
\varepsilon>0
(here
\varepsilon
plays the role of both
\varepsilon
and
\zeta
) and
f\in C_{0}^{\infty}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
.
By replicating the proof of (
7.95
) above verbatim,
for this it suffices to show that if we define

\tilde{W}_{\varepsilon}(x)=\int_{x}^{x+1}W\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)~\mathrm{d}x,\qquad x\in\mathbb{R},

then there exists a finite random variable
C>0
such that, almost surely,

|\tilde{W}_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}(x)|,\big(W_{\varepsilon}(x)-\tilde{W}_{\varepsilon}(x)\big)^{2}\leq C\log(2+|x|)

for all
\varepsilon\in[0,1)
, and moreover that

\sup_{\varepsilon\in[0,1)}\sup_{x\in[0,\vartheta]}|W\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon}(x)|<\infty.

These are proved in
[
28
, Lemma 5.18]
.

7.8.
Proof of Proposition
7.10

7.8.1.
Part 1: Two Lemmas

This proof relies on two technical lemmas:

Lemma 7.17
.

If
(f_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset D(\mathcal{E})
is such that
\sup_{n}\|f_{n}\|_{\star}<\infty
, then
there exists
f\in D(\mathcal{E})
and a subsequence
(n_{i})_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
along which

(1)

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\|f_{n_{i}}-f\|_{\mu}=0;

(2)

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\langle g,f^{\prime}_{n_{i}}\rangle_{\mu}=\langle g,f^{\prime}\rangle_{\mu}
for every
g\in L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})
;

(3)

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}f_{n_{i}}=f
uniformly on compact sets; and

(4)

\displaystyle\lim_{i\to\infty}\langle g,f_{n_{i}}\rangle_{\star}=\langle g,f\rangle_{\star}

for every
g\in D(\mathcal{E})
.

Lemma 7.18
.

Let
(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in[0,1)}
be a collection of bilinear forms such that the following conditions hold:

(1)

There exists
\kappa\in(0,1)
and
\nu>0
such that

|\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}(f,f)|\leq\kappa\mathcal{E}(f,f)+\nu\|f\|_{\mu}^{2}\qquad\text{for every }\varepsilon\in[0,1)\text{ and }f\in D(\mathcal{E}).

(2)

(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in[0,1)}
have a common form core
\mathrm{FC}
. For every
f,g\in\mathrm{FC}
, one has

(7.108)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}(f,g)=\mathcal{Q}_{0}(f,g).

(3)

If
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,1)
is a vanishing sequence,
\sup_{n}\|f_{n}\|_{\star}<\infty
, and
f_{n}\to f
in the
sense of Lemma
7.17
(1)–(4), then for every
g\in\mathrm{FC}
, one has

(7.109)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon_{n}}(f_{n},g)=\mathcal{Q}_{0}(f,g).

If we let
T_{\varepsilon}
denote the unique self-adjoint operators with form
\mathcal{E}+\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}
, then
every vanishing sequence
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,1)
has a subsequence
(\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}})_{\ell\in\mathbb{N}}
along which

(7.110)

\displaystyle\lim_{\ell\to\infty}\lambda_{k}(T_{\varepsilon_{n_{\ell}}})=\lambda_{k}(T_{0})\qquad\text{for every }k\geq 1.

Since the proof of these lemmas are mostly technical in nature and amount to trivial modifications
of results previously proved in
[
28
]
, we omit a full proof and opt instead
to provide precise references:

On the one hand,
Lemma
7.17

follows from expanding
\|\cdot\|_{\star}^{2}=\sum_{i}\|\cdot\|_{\star;i}^{2}

using (
7.93
) and (
7.94
),
and noting that
[
28
, Lemma 5.15]
proved
the same statement as Lemma
7.18

with
\|\cdot\|_{\star}
,
D(\mathcal{E})
, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mu}
/
\|\cdot\|_{\mu}
/
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})

respectively replaced by
\|\cdot\|_{\star;i}
,
D(\mathcal{E}_{i})
, and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
/
\|\cdot\|_{2}
/
L^{2}(I,\mathbb{F})

(see also
[
55
, Fact 2.2]
,
[
4
, Fact 2.2]
, and
[
5
, Fact 3.2]
).

On the other hand,
Lemma
7.18
follows from a verbatim application
of the argument presented in
[
28
, Pages 33 and 34, starting from (5.31)]
.
More specifically:

(1)

Lemma
7.17
plays the role of

[
28
, Lemma 5.15]
;

(2)

the forms
\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon}
and the operators
T_{\varepsilon}
play the role of

\mathcal{E}_{\varepsilon}
and
\widehat{H}_{\varepsilon}
in
[
28
]
;

(3)

Lemma
7.18
-(1) plays the role of

[
28
, Lemmas 5.17 and 5.18 and Remark 5.19]
;

(4)

Lemma
7.18
-(2) plays the role of

[
28
, Lemma 5.20, (5.29)]
; and

(5)

Lemma
7.18
-(3) plays the role of

[
28
, Lemma 5.20, (5.30)]
.

7.8.2.
Part 2: Application of Lemmas

At this point, in order to prove Proposition
7.10
,
it suffices to show that for every fixed
\zeta>0
,
almost surely,
the sequence of forms
(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in[0,1)}=(\xi^{\varepsilon,\zeta})_{\varepsilon\in[0,1)}

satisfies the conditions (1)–(3) in the statement of Lemma
7.18
,
and similarly for
(\mathcal{Q}_{\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in[0,1)}=(\xi^{0,\varepsilon})_{\varepsilon\in[0,1)}

(where in this latter sequence
\varepsilon
now plays the role of the parameter
\zeta
in (
7.16
)).

For this purpose, we first note that the claim of Lemma
7.18
-(1)
for both sequences follows from (
7.106
)
and the equivalence of the norms
\|\cdot\|_{\star}
and
\|\cdot\|_{+1}

(the latter of which was established in the proof of Proposition
2.12

above). Next, regarding items (2) and (3), we have by Remark

5.4
that all forms have a common core
\mathrm{FC}=\mathrm{FC}_{1}\oplus\cdots\oplus\mathrm{FC}_{r}
, where the
\mathrm{FC}_{i}
are defined
in Remark
5.1
. If we combine this with
the decompositions
(
7.87
),
(
7.91
),
and (
7.92
),
then the claims in (
7.108
) and
(
7.109
) can be reduced to the following:
Let
W
be a standard Brownian motion, and let
1\leq i\leq r
be fixed.
For every real-valued
f,g\in\mathrm{FC}_{i}
, one has

(7.111)

\displaystyle\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}(W\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon})^{\prime}(f,g)=W^{\prime}(f,g).

Moreover, if
(\varepsilon_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subset(0,1)
is a vanishing sequence,
the sequence of functions

f_{n}\in L^{2}(I,\mathbb{R})
is such that

\sup_{n}\|f_{n}\|_{\star;i}<\infty
, and
f_{n}\to f
in the
sense of Lemma
7.17
(1)–(4)
(with
\|\cdot\|_{\star}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\mu}
/
\|\cdot\|_{\mu}
/
L^{2}(\mathcal{A},\mathbb{F})

respectively replaced by
\|\cdot\|_{\star;i}
and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
/
\|\cdot\|_{2}
/
L^{2}(I,\mathbb{F})
), then for every real-valued
g\in\mathrm{FC}_{i}
, one has

(7.112)

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}(W\star\overline{\varrho}_{\varepsilon_{n}})^{\prime}(f_{n},g)=W^{\prime}(f,g).

These are the statements proved in
[
28
, Lemma 5.20]
,
in the special case where, in the notation of
[
28
]
,
\Xi=W

and
\xi=W^{\prime}
.
The proof of Proposition
7.10
is thus complete.

8.
Proof of Theorem
4.13
: Trace Covariance Asymptotics

Notation 8.1
.

Throughout this section, we use
C,c>0

to denote constants that are independent of
t_{1},t_{2}
,
whose exact value may change from one display to the next.

Remark 8.2
.

In Cases 1 and 2, we assume that there exists

\kappa,\nu>0
such that

(8.1)

\displaystyle V(i,x)\geq\kappa|x|-\nu\qquad\text{ for all $1\leq i\leq r$ and $x\in I$}.

In these two cases, the fact that
I
is infinite makes this a nontrivial strengthening
of Assumption
2.8
.
However, the assumption that
V
is bounded below and the fact that
I
is bounded
in Case 3 implies that (
8.1
) also holds in that case.
Thus, for convenience we assume that (
8.1
) holds
in all cases in this section.

8.1.
Proof Outline

The only technical input into the proof of Theorem
4.13

is the following:

Proposition 8.3
.

Let the hypotheses of Theorem
4.13
hold,
and recall the definition of
\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}
for
\zeta>0
in (
7.4
).
There exists
C>0
such that for every
t_{1},t_{2}\in(0,1]
,

(8.2)

\displaystyle\sup_{\zeta>0}\left|\mathbf{Cov}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big],\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\Big]\right|\leq C\cdot\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{(t_{1}t_{2})^{1/4}}{\sqrt{\max\{t_{1},t_{2}\}}}&\text{Cases 1 and 2},\\
(t_{1}t_{2})^{1/4}&\text{Case 3}.\end{cases}

By (
7.17
), for any
\zeta>0
we can write

\mathbf{Cov}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big],\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\Big]=\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t_{1};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t_{2};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right]\\
-\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t_{1};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right]\mathbf{E}\left[\int_{\mathcal{A}}\widehat{K}^{0,\zeta}(t_{2};a,a)~\mathrm{d}\mu(a)\right].

By combining (
7.13
) and Theorem
4.9
, this converges
as
\zeta\to 0
to

\mathbf{E}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}}\big]\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}}\big]\Big]-\mathbf{E}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}}\big]\Big]\mathbf{E}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}}\big]\Big]=\mathbf{Cov}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}}\big],\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}}\big]\Big].

Thus, we conclude that

(8.3)

\displaystyle\lim_{\zeta\to 0}\left|\mathbf{Cov}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big],\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\Big]\right|=\left|\mathbf{Cov}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}}\big],\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}}\big]\Big]\right|.

If we combine Proposition
8.3
with (
8.3
), we obtain that

(8.4)

\displaystyle\left|\mathbf{Cov}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}}\big],\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}}\big]\Big]\right|\leq C\cdot\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{(t_{1}t_{2})^{1/4}}{\sqrt{\max\{t_{1},t_{2}\}}}&\text{Cases 1 and 2},\\
(t_{1}t_{2})^{1/4}&\text{Case 3},\end{cases}

whenever the hypotheses of Theorem
4.13
hold.
This obviously implies (
1.8
)
and (
1.9
), hence the proof of
Theorem
4.13
is complete.

Remark 8.4
.

Given that we spend much of this paper proving exact formulas for the mixed moments
of
\widehat{H}
with white noise (as opposed to its smooth approximations
\widehat{H}^{\varepsilon,\zeta}
), it is natural to
wonder why we prove (
8.2
) instead of
establishing (
8.4
) directly. The reason for this is entirely technical,
and lies in the fact that manipulating the conditioned process
\widehat{U}
is more difficult
than the random walk
U
. To get around this, on the way to establishing
(
8.2
), we use several bounds that simplify the expression for the
covariance before taking
\zeta\to 0
, the latter of which allow to sidestep

\widehat{U}
entirely.

It now only remains to prove Proposition
8.3
:

8.2.
Proof of Proposition
8.3

8.2.1.
Step 1. Covariance Formula

Our first step toward Proposition
8.3

is to use the trace moment formula in Proposition
7.5

to provide an expression for

(8.5)

\mathbf{Cov}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big],\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\Big]\\
=\mathbf{E}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\Big]-\mathbf{E}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\Big]\mathbf{E}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\Big]

that is amenable to computation. For the first term on the right-hand side of (
8.5
),
Proposition
7.5
in the case
n=2
yields:

(8.6)

\mathbf{E}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\Big]=\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\\
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a}),

where we denote
\boldsymbol{t}=(t_{1},t_{2})
and
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=(\zeta,\zeta)
.
In contrast, two applications of Proposition
7.5
in the case
n=1

yields

(8.7)

\mathbf{E}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\Big]\mathbf{E}\Big[\mathrm{Tr}\big[\mathrm{e}^{-t_{2}\widehat{H}^{0,\zeta}}\big]\Big]=\prod_{k=1}^{2}\int_{\mathcal{A}}\Pi_{A}(t_{k};a_{k},a_{k})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{k}}(A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}})\\
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{t_{k}}V(A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{t_{k}}(A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}})+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|L_{[0,t_{k})}(A^{a_{k},a_{k}}_{t_{k}})\|_{\mu}^{2}}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu(a_{k}).

In order to relate this to (
8.7
), we introduce the following couplings:

Notation 8.5
.

For any given
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},a_{2})\in\mathcal{A}^{2}
, where
a_{k}=(i_{k},x_{k})
for
k=1,2
,
we let
A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}}=(U^{i_{1},i_{1}}_{t_{1}},Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}})
be as in
Definitions
3.2

and
3.3
, and let

\overline{A}^{a_{2},a_{2}}_{t_{2}}=(\overline{U}^{i_{2},i_{2}}_{t_{2}},\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})

be a process that has the same distribution as
A_{t_{2}}^{a_{2},a_{2}}
, but which we assume
is independent of
A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}}
. Then we can couple these two processes with
the concatenated process
A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
in (
8.7
)
in such a way that

A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)=\begin{cases}A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}}(s)&\text{if }0\leq s<t_{1},\\
\overline{A}^{a_{2},a_{2}}_{t_{2}}(s-t_{1})&\text{if }t_{1}\leq s\leq t_{1}+t_{2}.\end{cases}

Under this coupling, the additivity of integrals and boundary local time yields

\int_{0}^{t_{1}}V\big(A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}}(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t_{2}}V\big(\overline{A}^{a_{2},a_{2}}_{t_{2}}(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V\big(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)\big)~\mathrm{d}s,

\mathfrak{B}_{t_{1}}(A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}})+\mathfrak{B}_{t_{2}}(\overline{A}^{a_{2},a_{2}}_{t_{2}})=\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}),

where
\boldsymbol{a}=(a_{1},a_{2})
. Moreover,

L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}})=L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}});\qquad L_{[0,t_{2})}(\overline{A}^{a_{2},a_{2}}_{t_{2}})=L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}});

and
\prod_{k=1}^{2}\Pi_{A}(t_{k};a_{k},a_{k})=\Pi(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})
. Therefore, an application of Fubini’s theorem yields that
(
8.7
) is equal to

(8.8)

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{1}}(A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}})\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{2}}(\overline{A}^{a_{2},a_{2}}_{t_{2}})\\
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2})}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a}).

In summary, the only two differences between (
8.6
) and (
8.8
) are

\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\quad\text{vs.}\quad\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{1}}(A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}})\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{2}}(\overline{A}^{a_{2},a_{2}}_{t_{2}})

and

\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}\quad\text{vs.}\quad\tfrac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}).

In order to analyze the contributions of these differences
to the covariance one at a time,
we add and subtract the intermediary term

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\\
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2})}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a});

in doing so, the covariance in (
8.5
) (which we write as the difference between
(
8.6
) and (
8.8
) with the intermediary term
added and subtracted)
can be written as the sum of the following two terms:

(8.9)

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\\
\cdot\left(\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}}-\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2})}\right)\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a});

and

(8.10)

\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\left(\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})-\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{1}}(A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}})\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{2}}(\overline{A}^{a_{2},a_{2}}_{t_{2}})\right)\\
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2})}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a}).

The proof of
Proposition
8.3
now relies on showing that for any choice of
\zeta>0
,

(8.11)

\displaystyle|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|~,|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 2}|\leq\text{right-hand side of }\eqref{Equation: Rigidity RHS}.

We bound these terms one at a time.

8.2.2.
Step 2. Upper Bound for (
8.9
)

Noting that

\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}\\
=\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+2\big\langle L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}),L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\big\rangle_{\mu},

and then recalling the bound
|\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A)|\leq\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A),

where
\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A)
was defined in (
7.58
),
for any
t_{1},t_{2}\in(0,1]
we can bound the absolute value of (
8.9
) as follows:

(8.12)

|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\\
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\sigma^{2}\langle L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}),L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\rangle_{\mu}}-1\right)\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a}).

Recall from (
4.4
) that

\sum_{i=1}^{r}L_{[0,t_{1})}^{(i,x)}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=L_{t_{1}}^{x}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sum_{i=1}^{r}L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}^{(i,x)}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=L_{t_{2}}^{x}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}}).

In particular, since local time is nonnegative,

\langle L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}),L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\rangle_{\mu}\leq\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\rangle.

Moreover, by (
7.36
) applied to the case where
f
is
A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
’s local time,

\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}\leq\|L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}})\|_{2}^{2},\quad\|L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}\leq\|L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\|_{2}^{2}.

Next, by
[
30
, Proposition A.1]
, there exists some
C>0
such that

\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in I^{2}}\Pi_{Z}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x})\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2},\qquad\text{for all }t_{1},t_{2}\in(0,1].

If we combing all this with (
7.30
) and (
8.1
), we get
from (
8.12
) that

(8.13)

|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2}\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{U}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|~\mathrm{d}s+\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\\
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(\|L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}})\|_{2}^{2}+\|L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\|_{2}^{2})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\sigma^{2}\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\rangle}-1\right)\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a}).

At this point, we notice that the only term that depends on
U
in (
8.13
)
is
\overline{\mathfrak{m}}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})
. Thus, by replicating the analysis performed in
(
7.59
)–(
7.63
) (with
n=2
), if we first average
over
U
only in (
8.13
), then we get from (
8.13
)
the
\zeta
-independent bound

(8.14)

|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2}\int_{I^{2}}\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|~\mathrm{d}s+\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\\
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{\frac{2(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}+\sigma^{2}}{2}(\|L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}})\|_{2}^{2}+\|L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\|_{2}^{2})}\left(\mathrm{e}^{\sigma^{2}\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\rangle}-1\right)\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

Then, an application of Hölder’s inequality in (
8.14
) yields

(8.15)

|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2}\int_{I^{2}}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-4\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|~\mathrm{d}s}\right]^{1/4}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{4\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]^{1/4}\\
\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{2(n(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}+\sigma^{2})(\|L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}})\|_{2}^{2}+\|L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\|_{2}^{2})}\right]^{1/4}\\
\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\mathrm{e}^{\sigma^{2}\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\rangle}-1\right)^{4}\right]^{1/4}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

If we use exactly the same Brownian scaling arguments as in
[
30
, Lemma 4.6]
, then we see that there exists a constant
C>0
such that

(8.16)

\displaystyle\sup_{\boldsymbol{t}\in(0,1]^{2},~\boldsymbol{x}\in I^{2}}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{4\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})}\right]^{1/4}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{2(n(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}+\sigma^{2})(\|L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}})\|_{2}^{2}+\|L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\|_{2}^{2})}\right]^{1/4}<C.

Thus, we get from (
8.15
) that

(8.17)

|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2}\int_{I^{2}}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{-4\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|~\mathrm{d}s}\right]^{1/4}\\
\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\mathrm{e}^{\sigma^{2}\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\rangle}-1\right)^{4}\right]^{1/4}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

At this point, in order to prove that (
8.17
) is bounded by the right-hand side of (
8.2
),
we separate our analysis for Cases 1 and 2 versus 3.

We begin with Case 3, as it is the simplest. In this case, we notice that since the interval
I=(0,\vartheta)
is bounded and
\mathrm{e}^{-4\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|~\mathrm{d}s}\leq 1
,
(
8.17
) yields

(8.18)

\displaystyle|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2}\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in I^{2}}\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\mathrm{e}^{\sigma^{2}\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\rangle}-1\right)^{4}\right]^{1/4}.

Then, if we apply the inequality
(\mathrm{e}^{x}-1)^{4}\leq(x\mathrm{e}^{|x|})^{4}
, followed by Hölder’s inequality, we get the upper bound

(8.19)

\displaystyle|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2}\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in I^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{2}\mathbf{E}\left[\|L_{t_{k}}(Z^{x_{k},x_{k}}_{t_{k}})\|_{2}^{8}\right]^{1/8}\mathbf{E}\left[\mathrm{e}^{8\sigma^{2}\|L_{t_{k}}(Z^{x_{k},x_{k}}_{t_{k}})\|_{2}}\right]^{1/8},

where the expectation factors into a product over
k=1,2
because of the independence of
Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}

and
\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}}
.
Thanks to (
8.16
), this further reduces to

(8.20)

\displaystyle|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2}\sup_{\boldsymbol{x}\in I^{2}}\prod_{k=1}^{2}\mathbf{E}\left[\|L_{t_{k}}(Z^{x_{k},x_{k}}_{t_{k}})\|_{2}^{8}\right]^{1/8}.

By
[
30
, (4.31)]
(in the notation of
[
30
]
, the parameter
\mathfrak{d}

is equal to
3/2
in the case of white noise; see
[
30
, (2.16)]
), we have the following estimate:

Lemma 8.6
.

In Cases 1, 2, and 3, for any
k=1,2
and
\theta\geq 1
, there exists a constant
C
such that

\sup_{x_{k}\in I}\mathbf{E}\left[\|L_{t_{k}}(Z^{x_{k},x_{k}}_{t_{k}})\|_{2}^{\theta}\right]^{1/\theta}\leq Ct_{k}^{3/4}\qquad\text{for all $t_{k}\in(0,1]$.}

The combination of this with (
8.20
) implies that (
8.11
)
holds for (
8.9
) in Case 3.

Consider now Cases 1 and 2.
If we replicate the bound (
6.23
) with
\overline{\mathfrak{a}}=1

and apply (
6.29
), then we get from
(
8.17
) that

(8.21)

\displaystyle|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2}\int_{I^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}|x_{1}|-t_{2}|x_{2}|}\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\mathrm{e}^{\sigma^{2}\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\rangle}-1\right)^{4}\right]^{1/4}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

In order for the random variable inside the expectation in (
8.21
)
to be nonzero, it is necessary that the supports of the independent Brownian bridges
Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}

and
\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}}
intersect. Thus, by introducing the indicator of this event in the expectation
and applying Hölder’s inequality, we get from (
8.21
) that

(8.22)

|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2}\int_{I^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}|x_{1}|-t_{2}|x_{2}|}\mathbf{P}\left[Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}\text{ and }\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}}\text{ intersect}\right]^{1/8}\\
\cdot\mathbf{E}\left[\left(\mathrm{e}^{\sigma^{2}\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\rangle}-1\right)^{8}\right]^{1/8}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

By replicating the analysis in (
8.18
)–(
8.20
)
and Lemma
8.6
, (
8.22
)
implies that

(8.23)

\displaystyle|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{1/4}\int_{I^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}|x_{1}|-t_{2}|x_{2}|}\mathbf{P}\left[Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}\text{ and }\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}}\text{ intersect}\right]^{1/8}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

By
[
29
, (4.14) and the two following displays]
, there exists
C,c>0
such that

\mathbf{P}\left[Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}\text{ and }\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}}\text{ intersect}\right]^{1/8}\leq C\left(\mathrm{e}^{-c(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}/t_{1}}+\mathrm{e}^{-c(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}/t_{2}}\right)

for all
t_{1},t_{2}\in(0,1]
; hence
(
8.23
) yields

(8.24)

\displaystyle|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{1/4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-t_{1}|x_{1}|-t_{2}|x_{2}|}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-c(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}/t_{1}}+\mathrm{e}^{-c(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}/t_{2}}\right)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x},

where we have replaced
I
by
\mathbb{R}
in Case 2, which only increases the size of the integral.
Denote
t_{*}=\max\{t_{1},t_{2}\}
. Then,
(
8.24
) implies that

(8.25)

\displaystyle|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 1}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{1/4}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\mathrm{e}^{-t_{*}|x_{1}|}\mathrm{e}^{-c(x_{1}-x_{2})^{2}/t_{*}}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}=C(t_{1}t_{2})^{1/4}\cdot\frac{2\sqrt{\pi}}{\sqrt{ct_{*}}};

hence (
8.11
)
holds for (
8.9
) in Case 1 and 2 as well.

8.2.3.
Step 3. Upper Bound for (
8.10
)

Recall the coupling introduced in Definition
8.5
.
Given that
\boldsymbol{\zeta}=(\zeta,\zeta)
, we have from (
7.9
) that
the difference

\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})-\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{1}}(A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}})\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{2}}(A^{a_{2},a_{2}}_{t_{2}})

can be written as

(8.26)

\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\Bigg(\mathbf{1}_{\{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}}\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(p,U^{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big)\\
-\mathbf{1}_{\{N(t_{1}),N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})\text{ are even}\}}\sum_{p_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{N(t_{1})},~p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}}\Bigg\{\cdots\\
\cdots\mathfrak{C}_{t_{1}}(p_{1},U^{i_{1},i_{1}}_{t_{1}})\mathfrak{C}_{t_{2}}(p_{2},\overline{U}^{i_{2},i_{2}}_{t_{2}})\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p_{1}\cup p_{2}}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big)\Bigg\}\Bigg).

Suppose that
N(t_{1})
and
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})
are even, and
let
p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}
be such that we can write
p=p_{1}\cup p_{2}
for some

p_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{N(t_{1})}
and
p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}
.
We claim that in such a case, one has

(8.27)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(p,U^{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(p_{1}\cup p_{2},U^{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=\mathfrak{C}_{t_{1}}(p_{1},U^{i_{1},i_{1}}_{t_{1}})\mathfrak{C}_{t_{2}}(p_{2},\overline{U}^{i_{2},i_{2}}_{t_{2}}).

When
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R}
or
\mathbb{C}
, this follows directly by Definition
4.2
-(1) and -(2):
The matching
p=p_{1}\cup p_{2}
only contains pairs
\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}
such that
J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}
and/or
J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}^{*}

if and only if the same is true for both
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
. When
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{H}
,
by Definition
4.2
-(3) we obtain (
8.27
) if we additionally ensure that

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(p,U^{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=\mathfrak{D}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(p_{1}\cup p_{2},U^{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})=\mathfrak{D}_{t_{1}}(p_{1},U^{i_{1},i_{1}}_{t_{1}})\mathfrak{D}_{t_{2}}(p_{2},\overline{U}^{i_{2},i_{2}}_{t_{2}})

whenever
J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}
or
J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}^{*}
for all
\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p
.
By (
4.1
), this reduces to

(8.28)

\sum_{m\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)},~m\text{ respects }(p,J)}(-1)^{f(m,p,J)}=\sum_{m\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)},~m\text{ respects }(p_{1}\cup p_{2},J)}(-1)^{f(m,p_{1}\cup p_{2},J)}\\
=\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(t_{1})}\\
m_{1}\text{ resp. }(p_{1},J_{1})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{f(m_{1},p_{1},J_{1})}\right)\left(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m_{2}\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}\\
m_{2}\text{ resp. }(p_{2},\overline{J}_{2})\end{subarray}}(-1)^{f(m_{2},p_{2},\overline{J}_{2})}\right),

where
J_{1}
denotes the jumps in the path
U^{i_{1},i_{1}}_{t_{1}}
, and
\overline{J}_{2}
the jumps in the
path
\overline{U}^{i_{2},i_{2}}_{t_{2}}
. If
m\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}
can be written as the concatenation of
m_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(t_{1})}

and
m_{2}\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}
, then clearly
f(m_{1},p_{1},J_{1})+f(m_{2},p_{2},\overline{J}_{2})=f(m,p,J)
since flips can only occur in pairs matched by
p=p_{1}\cup p_{2}
; moreover,
the fact that
m_{1}
respects
(p_{1},J_{1})
and
m_{2}
respects
(p_{2},\overline{J}_{2})
implies that
m
respects
(p,J)
.
Thus, (
8.28
) follows if we show that every

m\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}
that respects
(p_{1}\cup p_{2},J)
can be written as a concatenation of

m_{1}\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(t_{1})}
which respects
(p_{1},J_{1})
and

m_{2}\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}
which respects
(p_{2},\overline{J}_{2})
.
For this, we recall from Definition
4.2
-(3.1) and -(3.2) that in order for
m
to respect
(p_{1}\cup p_{2},J)
,
the steps in
m
that are paired up by
p
must be taken from the following set of pairs of steps:

\Big\{\big\{(0,0),(1,1)\big\},\big\{(0,1),(1,0)\big\},\big\{(0,0),(0,0)\big\},\big\{(1,1),(1,1)\big\}\Big\}.

In the above, the only steps that move from
0
to
1
or vice versa are
(0,1)
and
(1,0)
,
and these must be paired by
p
. In particular, any uninterrupted segment of
m
that only contains pairs of steps
matched by
p
must start and end at the same element.
Consequently, the first
N(t_{1})
steps in
m
, which are matched by
p_{1}
,
must form a path in
\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(t_{1})}
that respects
(p_{1},J_{1})
,
and the following
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})
steps in
m
, which are matched by
p_{2}
,
must form a path in
\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}
that respects
(p_{2},\overline{J}_{2})
.
This then concludes the proof of (
8.27
).

Thanks to (
8.27
),
we now see that every
p\in\mathcal{P}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)}
that we can write
p=p_{1}\cup p_{2}
for some

p_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{N(t_{1})}
and
p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}

is cancelled out by the difference in
(
8.26
).
Thus, if we let

\mathcal{R}_{m_{1},m_{2}}

denote the set of
p\in\mathcal{P}_{m_{1}+m_{2}}
that cannot be written as

p=p_{1}\cup p_{2}
for some

p_{1}\in\mathcal{P}_{m_{1}}
and
p_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{m_{2}}
, then
(
8.26
) simplifies to

\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})-\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{1}}(A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}})\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{2}}(A^{a_{2},a_{2}}_{t_{2}})\\
=\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\mathbf{1}_{\{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{R}_{N(t_{1}),N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}}\mathfrak{C}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(p,U^{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big).

At this point, if we combine (
4.2
) with the fact that

\mathcal{R}_{0,0}
is empty (as
\varnothing=\varnothing\cup\varnothing
),
then we obtain the bound

(8.29)

\big|\mathfrak{m}^{\boldsymbol{\zeta}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})-\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{1}}(A^{a_{1},a_{1}}_{t_{1}})\mathfrak{m}^{\zeta}_{t_{2}}(A^{a_{2},a_{2}}_{t_{2}})\big|\\
\leq\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\mathbf{1}_{\{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)\geq 2\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{R}_{N(t_{1}),N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big).

Thus, plugging this back into (
8.10
) yields

(8.30)

|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 2}|\leq\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\Pi_{A}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a})\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}\mathbf{1}_{\{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)\geq 2\text{ is even}\}}\\
\cdot\sum_{p\in\mathcal{R}_{N(t_{1}),N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big)\\
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}V(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s))~\mathrm{d}s+\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(\|L_{[0,t_{1})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2}+\|L_{[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})}(A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{a}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{\mu}^{2})}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a}).

Then, if we replicate the bounds on
V
,
\Pi_{Z}
,
\mathfrak{B}_{t}(A)
, and the local time
L_{[u,v)}(A)

used to arrive to (
8.13
)—together with an application
of the tower property in the expectation with respect to
U
—we obtain from
(
8.30
) that

(8.31)

|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 2}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2}\int_{\mathcal{A}^{2}}\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\cdots\\
\cdot\Pi_{U}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i})\mathbf{E}_{U^{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}\Bigg[\mathbf{1}_{\{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)\geq 2\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{R}_{N(t_{1}),N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big)\Bigg]\\
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|~\mathrm{d}s+\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(\|L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}})\|_{2}^{2}+\|L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\|_{2}^{2})}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\mu^{2}(\boldsymbol{a}).

Using the fact that

\Pi_{U}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i})\mathbf{E}\big[F(U^{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\big]=\mathbf{E}\big[F(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\mathbf{1}_{\{\forall k:~U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)\to i_{k}\text{ as }s\to(t_{0}+\cdots+t_{k})^{+}\}}\big]\leq\mathbf{E}\big[F(U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\big]

for any nonnegative functional
F
, we note that

\displaystyle\Pi_{U}(\boldsymbol{t};\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i})\mathbf{E}_{U^{\boldsymbol{i},\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}\Bigg[\mathbf{1}_{\{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)\geq 2\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{R}_{N(t_{1}),N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big)\Bigg]

(8.32)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbf{E}_{U^{\boldsymbol{i}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}}\Bigg[\mathbf{1}_{\{N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)\geq 2\text{ is even}\}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{R}_{N(t_{1}),N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)-N(t_{1})}}\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(\tau_{\ell_{2}})\big)\Bigg].

Then, using the law of total expectation, and the facts that
N(|\boldsymbol{t}|)
is a Poisson random variable
with parameter
(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|
and that conditional on
N(t_{1})=2m
,
the jump times
(\tau_{1},\ldots,\tau_{2m})
are the order statistics of i.i.d. uniform
random points on
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}
, we can bound (
8.32
) by:

(8.33)

\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 2 - 7}\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\Bigg(\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}_{<}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{R}_{m_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}),m_{2}(\boldsymbol{s})}}\left\{\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)\right\}\\
\cdot\frac{(2m)!}{|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2m}}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\Bigg)\frac{\big((r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|\big)^{2m}}{(2m)!}\mathrm{e}^{-(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|},

where we recall that
[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)_{<}^{2m}
denotes the tuples
\boldsymbol{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{2m})\in[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}

such that
s_{1}<s_{2}<\cdots<s_{2m}
, and we use
m_{1}(\boldsymbol{s})
and
m_{2}(\boldsymbol{s})
respectively to denote
the number of components of
\boldsymbol{s}
that land in
[0,t_{1})
and
[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})

(i.e.,
m_{1}(\boldsymbol{s})
is the largest integer
\ell
such that
s_{\ell}<t_{1}
, and
2m=m_{1}(\boldsymbol{s})+m_{2}(\boldsymbol{s})
).
If
p\in\mathcal{R}_{m_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}),m_{2}(\boldsymbol{s})}
, then this means that there exists at least one pair of indices
1\leq\ell_{1}<\ell_{2}\leq 2m
such that
s_{\ell_{1}}\in[0,t_{1})
,

s_{\ell_{2}}\in[t_{1},|\boldsymbol{t}|)
, and
\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p
. The indicator function that the tuple
\boldsymbol{s}
satisfies this is obviously bounded by the sum of indicators

\mathcal{I}^{t_{1},t_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s})=\sum_{u,v=1}^{2m}\mathbf{1}_{\{s_{u}\in[0,t_{1})\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{s_{v}\in[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})\}}.

If we combine this upper bound in (
8.33
) with the replacement of the sum over
p\in\mathcal{R}_{m_{1}(\boldsymbol{s}),m_{2}(\boldsymbol{s})}

to the sum over
p\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}
, then we get the further upper bound

(8.34)

\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 2 - 7}\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\Bigg(\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}_{<}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}}\mathcal{I}^{t_{1},t_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s})\left\{\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)\right\}\\
\cdot\frac{(2m)!}{|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2m}}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\Bigg)\frac{\big((r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|\big)^{2m}}{(2m)!}\mathrm{e}^{-(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}.

Now, we note that the function being integrated in (
8.34
) is invariant
with respect to permuting the entries of
\boldsymbol{s}
, hence we can rewrite (
8.34
) by
removing the condition that the
s_{i}
’s be ordered as follows:

\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 2 - 7}\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\Bigg(\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2m}}\sum_{p\in\mathcal{P}_{2m}}\mathcal{I}^{t_{1},t_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s})\left\{\prod_{\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in p}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{1}})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{\ell_{2}})\big)\right\}\\
\cdot\frac{1}{|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2m}}~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\Bigg)\frac{\big((r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|\big)^{2m}}{(2m)!}\mathrm{e}^{-(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}.

Now, if we cancel out both appearances of
|\boldsymbol{t}|^{2m}
, pull out the sums over
p
and with in
\mathcal{I}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s})
,
and then apply a change of variables such that the variables
u
and
v
in
\mathbf{1}_{\{s_{u}\in[0,t_{1})\}}\mathbf{1}_{\{s_{v}\in[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2})\}}

are always
u=1
and
v=2
, then Tonelli’s theorem yields

\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 2 - 7}\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}(2m-1)!!(2m)(2m-1)\Bigg(\int_{[0,t_{1}]\times[t_{1},t_{1}+t_{2}]}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{1})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{2})\big)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\Bigg)\\
\Bigg(\int_{[0,|\boldsymbol{t}|)^{2}}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{1})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{2})\big)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\Bigg)^{m-1}\frac{\big((r-1)\big)^{2m}}{(2m)!}\mathrm{e}^{-(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|},

where the prefactors in front of the integral come from the fact that
|\mathcal{P}_{2m}|=(2m-1)!!
and that there are
(2m)(2m-1)
summands in

\mathcal{I}_{t_{1},t_{2}}(\boldsymbol{s})
. Replicating the application of Young’s convolution inequality in (
7.62
),
we get that for intervals
\mathcal{K}_{1},\mathcal{K}_{2}\subset[0,\infty)
,

\int_{\mathcal{K}_{1}\times\mathcal{K}_{2}}\upsilon^{2}\varrho_{\zeta}\big(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{1})-Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s_{2})\big)~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{s}\leq\upsilon^{2}\big\langle L_{\mathcal{K}_{1}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}),L_{\mathcal{K}_{2}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\big\rangle.

Therefore, we get that (
8.32
)
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{(r-1)|\boldsymbol{t}|}
is bounded above by

\frac{\big\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\big\rangle}{\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}}\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\frac{(2m-1)!!(2m)(2m-1)}{(2m)!}\left(\frac{(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}}{2}\right)^{m}\\
=(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}\big\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\big\rangle\left(\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}+1\right)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}},

where the second line follows from direct calculation.

If we now combine this with (
8.31
) and (
8.32
), then we obtain

|\eqref{Equation: Covariance Formula Piece 2}|\leq C(t_{1}t_{2})^{-1/2}\int_{I^{2}}\mathbf{E}\Bigg[\cdots\\
\cdot\big\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\big\rangle\left(\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}+1\right)\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{2}(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}}\\
\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\int_{0}^{|\boldsymbol{t}|}\kappa|Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}(s)|~\mathrm{d}s+\overline{\mathfrak{B}_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}(\|L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}})\|_{2}^{2}+\|L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\|_{2}^{2})}\Bigg]~\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{x}.

At this point, we obtain
(
8.11
)
for
(
8.10
)
by replicating essentially the same analysis that we performed in (
8.18
):
The only difference is that we replace

\mathrm{e}^{\sigma^{2}\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\rangle}-1

by

\big\langle L_{t_{1}}(Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}),L_{t_{2}}(\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}})\big\rangle\left(\|L_{|\boldsymbol{t}|}(Z^{\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{t}})\|_{2}^{2}(r-1)^{2}\upsilon^{2}+1\right),

but both of these terms are controlled in the same way. That is,
we introduce an indicator that the paths of
Z^{x_{1},x_{1}}_{t_{1}}
and
\overline{Z}^{x_{2},x_{2}}_{t_{2}}
must intersect,
and then use Cauchy-Schwarz with Lemma
8.6
.
With this in hand, the proof of Theorem
4.13
, and thus of all of our results,
is complete.

Appendix A
Illustrations

Figure 1.
This figure shows a realization
A=(U,Z)
.
The
y
-axis represents the output of
Z
, and
the colors represent the value of the process
U
(the jump times
\tau_{k}
therein
are represented by the symbol
\otimes
).

Figure 2.
This figure shows a realization of the càdlàg concatenated path

A^{\boldsymbol{a},\boldsymbol{b}}_{\boldsymbol{t}}
with
\boldsymbol{a}=\big((1,1),(3,0)\big)
,
\boldsymbol{b}=\big((1,1),(3,0)\big)
,
and
\boldsymbol{t}=(1,1)
(i.e., the first path goes from
(i,x)=(1,1)
to itself in time
1
,
and the second path goes from
(i,x)=(3,0)
to itself in time
1
).

Figure 3.
In this figure, the arrows represent the first six jumps of
U
by time
t
,
namely,
(1,2),(2,3),(3,1),(1,2),(2,3),(3,1)
. The partition above the
arrows serve to highlight which jumps were paired by the perfect matching
p=\big\{\{1,4\},\{2,5\},\{3,6\}\big\}
.
With this in hand, we conclude that this particular combination of
(p,U)

is such that
\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)\neq 0
when
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{H}
, since
J_{1}=J_{4}
,
J_{2}=J_{5}
,
and
J_{3}=J_{6}
. However,
\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)=0
when
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{C}

because paired jumps are not reversed.

Figure 4.
In this figure, the six jumps by time
t
are
(1,2),(2,1),(1,3),(3,1),(1,2),(2,1)
,
and
p=\big\{\{1,6\},\{2,5\},\{3,4\}\big\}
.
In this case,
\mathfrak{C}_{t}(p,U)\neq 0
for all
\mathbb{F}=\mathbb{R},\mathbb{C},\mathbb{H}
, since every paired jumps
are reversed (i.e.,
J_{1}=J_{6}^{*}
,
J_{2}=J_{5}^{*}
, and
J_{3}=J_{4}^{*}
).

Figure 5.
In this figure, the black arrows are the steps
J_{k}
until
N(t)
,
the blue arrows are the steps of a binary sequence
m\in\mathcal{B}^{0,0}_{N(t)}
,
and the partition above all that represents the pairs given by
p
. In order to help
decipher whether this choice of
m
contributes to
\mathfrak{D}_{t}(p,U)
,
the matched pairs of steps such that
J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}
are colored in green,
and the matched pairs such that
J_{\ell_{1}}=J_{\ell_{2}}^{*}
are colored in red.
In this case,

m
does not respect
(p,J)
, and thus it does not contribute to
\mathfrak{D}_{t}(p,U)
.
(E.g.,
J_{4}
and
J_{5}
are matched and in opposite directions, but the corresponding steps
in the binary sequence,
\big((0,0),(0,1)\big)
, are not among those listed in
Definition
4.2
-(3.2))

Figure 6.
In this figure,
m
contributes to
\mathfrak{D}_{t}(p,U)
,
since every matched pairs of jumps
J_{\ell_{1}},J_{\ell_{2}}
are accompanied by steps
in the binary sequence that satisfy Definition
4.2
-(3.1) and -(3.2).
Moreover, there is one flip that occurs in this sequence:

J_{1}=J_{6}
and
\big((m_{0},m_{1}),(m_{5},m_{6})\big)=\big((0,1),(1,0)\big)
;
hence
f(m,p,J)=1
.

Figure 7.
This is another example where
m
contributes
to
\mathfrak{D}_{t}(p,U)
. Moreover, in this case we have
f(m,p,J)=2
;
the two flips occur at the pairs
J_{1},J_{5}
and
J_{2},J_{6}
.

Figure 8.
In this figure, the colored path represents a realization of
(\widehat{U},Z)

with
Z(0)=1=Z(1)
.
The symbols
\otimes
along the path represent the jump times
\widehat{\tau}_{k}
,
and the different colors represent the values of the process
\widehat{U}(t)
.
The jumps
J_{k}
and the random matching
\widehat{\pi}(P)
(the latter of which
is represented by the partition above
Z
’s path) are the same as
J_{k}
and
p
in Figure
4
.
In this illustration, we observe that

Z(\widehat{\tau}_{\ell_{1}})=Z(\widehat{\tau}_{\ell_{2}})
for every matched pair
\{\ell_{1},\ell_{2}\}\in\widehat{\pi}(P)
, which is consistent with the fact that
the pairs
\widehat{\tau}_{\ell_{1}}
and
\widehat{\tau}_{\ell_{2}}
were sampled
